
There were two bulbs on the
first floor landing, and a third
above the front door which would
rock when anybody opened the
door. Because of the wind blow-
ing from the estuary, the bulb
swayed violently as Mr. Haxton
came in. He went up the stairs,
stopped on the landing, and listened. The lounge was in
darkness, so nobody was there. Mrs. Hughes, whose sit-
ting room was on the right with a discreet keyhole be-
tween her and the lounge, put down her knitting, sat up,
and listened. Eileen, her deaf and dumb daughter, watched
her quietly. Mr. Haxton took four steps forward which
brought him to the end of the landing where the upper
staircase began. It was a narrow staircase twisting twice be-
fore it reached the second floor. Mr. Haxton had a room on
the second floor, the number of which was 16. That num-
ber had often puzzled him, since there were only ten bed-
rooms in Mrs. Hughes' private hotel.
In his bedroom there was a bed, an easy chair, a wash-
stand, and a little contraption serving as a bookcase; but
Mr. Haxton had no books: he had spent a long life work-
ing for the peace of retirement, which left no time for read-
ing. Besides, his wife had nagged him too much. It was a
small room with two windows, one looking out to sea, the
other facing the estuary which seemed, more or less, as
wide as the sea. On the mantelpiece was the photograph of
his wife. She was a woman with a lot of hair and a clipped
smile. The hair was so abundant that it reminded you of awig; and
you took no notice of the smile. There was a smell
of tobacco and seaweed in the room. He opened the win-
dow; as the smell of tobacco drifted out, stronger smells of
seaweed rushed in. He shivered and closed the window.
Somehow the tobacco smell returned.
It was a world full of gulls and the sound of water. He
sat down in the armchair, crossed his legs, which were fat
and sturdy like his sixty-one-year-old body. The house
was as quiet as the deaf and dumb girl whose room was
underneath his. If he went down into the lounge, he could
have tea for nothing; in his bedroom it would cost a shilling
extra. He put a shilling into the slot of the radiator, which
meant he would not go to the lounge. He rang the bell.
Eileen would answer it, for that day being Thursday, Mr.
and Mrs. Hailey, the cook-waitress and waiter-porter,
had their afternoon off. He thought he heard the gulls, but
nobody answered his ring. He waited patiently. After a
long while he heard footsteps, and though he expected the
door to open, he was nevertheless surprised when Eileen
came in, gently, as if in her silent world even doors should
abstain from noise.
"Eileen," he said in a low voice, "could I have my tea,
please?"
It had been like that ever since he came to the Red
House, Private Hotel, Unlicensed. The Red House was
called so on account of the heavy red bricks with which it
had been built in late Victorian days, making it resemble a
police station. It was very much a private hotel. Of perma-
nent guests there were only four: himself, Mr. Ribble, Mrs.
More who was the widow of a Scotland Yard superintend-ent, and Mr. Josey the Spaniard. Now and then the manager
of the Crown, if the Crown was full, let Mrs. Hughes have
the guests he could not accommodate, mostly commercial
travelers. Once during a snowstorm a real baronet had
stayed for the night. He was to go shooting next day with
a neighboring landowner, but the snow-covered roads made
it impossible for him to get to his destination. Mrs. Hughes
had never forgotten that august night, frequently hinting
to Mrs. More that she was accustomed to a different type
of clientele. Sometimes Mrs. More took the hint to heart
and grumbled less about the extras. The Haileys had not
been impressed by the baronet: he had forgotten to tip
them.
With Eileen in the same room, Mr. Haxton usually for-
got that he was living in the Red House with its wet,
crumbling William Morris wallpaper. She seemed not to
belong to it, and he hoped he didn't belong either.
"Yes, please," he said. "Tea."
His voice trembled because he knew she would under-
stand him. The first time he saw her after Hailey, taking up
his luggage, had told him that Mrs. Hughes' daughter was
deaf and dumb, he should have smiled and walked on. But
he had to talk. He spoke to her as one speaks to a normal
being; then, wanting to laugh his words away, he saw that
she had understood him. He was frightened and decided
that he would smile at her, nod in her direction, make short
sweeping movements with his arm as the other residents
did. But he did not, because he had to go on talking to her.
One evening he had gone to the cinema with Mr. Ribble.
They came back, and passing Mrs. Hughes' room hestopped. He wished he hadn't, for Eileen appeared in her
blue dressing gown, her hair tied back with a white ribbon.
Mr. Ribble grunted, waved a hand, and continued toward
the upper staircase; Mr. Haxton turned to Eileen and said,
much to his own surprise, “It wasn't a good film, Eileen.
I didn't think much of it." He waited courteously and
added, "Well, good night." He hoped that Mr. Ribble
hadn't heard him.
"My tea, Eileen," he said again.
Not that it was necessary, but in order to keep her
longer in the room, he tried to pretend to himself that she
hadn't understood him. She was facing the afternoon light
that came from the estuary. It was late October light. A
little gray and mostly blunt. Eileen had fair hair, and her
mother always tied it back with a ribbon. Her eyes were
blue and calm; they were steady, too, as though expecting
you never to move. Her mother dressed her in simple, in-
expensive tweeds; in the morning while helping the staff,
she wore an apron. Generally the apron was too long for
her. It made her look old and tired. Her hands were well
shaped but red from the housework.
"A month or so," said Mr. Haxton, "and winter will
be here."
The light was still on her hair, though the sun was mak-
ing a plunge into the sea. "Frankly, Eileen," he said, “I
don't like winter. Mind you, there was a time when I used
to like winter. Nice cozy fires and seasonable weather. But
not now. I think I must be getting old." He laughed apolo-
getically and looked at the picture on the mantelpiece.
The front door banged. Somebody was coming up thestairs. He listened. Would that be Mr. Ribble? The lounge
door opened and closed, the bell rang. It must be Mr.
Ribble back for his tea.
"Tea, please," he said.
Eileen went from the room. She reached the landing, she
stopped, then she went on. Audrey the maid, who served
meals when the Haileys were out, must be getting the tea
tray ready for her.
Mrs. Hughes was mean. The tea would be lukewarm,
the bread thick, probably because she didn't want to use
the bread knife too much. Eileen came back with the coldish
tea and two chunks of bread. He hoped she would stay,
but she left; he listened again and heard her go to her
mother's room. His jealousy of Mrs. Hughes had become a
joke with him. A feeble joke.
"They make you work too hard, Eileen," he said to the
empty room. "We'll all give the Haileys and Audrey large
tips at Christmas, but nobody is going to buy you a shorter
apron."
Eileen came back: she had forgotten the jam.
"Thank you,'
," he said, "but you shouldn't have bothered
about it."
Then she did exactly what he had expected her to do.
She moved to the mantelpiece and stood gazing hard at the
photograph, rapt in serious admiration. It had been a fool-
ish thing to put his wife's picture on the mantelpiece; he
should have left it at the bottom of his trunk. But the
room had seemed so lonely when he arrived that he could
not resist unpacking the picture. The next move was, of
course, to put it on the mantelpiece."One day, Eileen," he said, "I'll tell you all about her."
Eileen poured out his tea, switched on the light, and
went. Though the tea might get truly cold, he could not
resist listening to her footsteps. She did not stop till she
reached the basement. Probably washing up for the Haileys.
The Haileys, he thought, liked Eileen, but the lazy sods
tried to palm off as much work as they could on her. Not
when Mrs. Hughes saw them. In her gaunt way she was a
good mother, and a jealous one. If her daughter had to
work, let her work for her.
But all that was only conjecture. He saw little of Eileen
and less of her mother. The Haileys liked to gossip, though
they did not know much of what went on when mother
and daughter were alone in the room marked PRIVATE. And
most of the time Eileen was in that room.
He heard footsteps on the stairs, then panting: Mrs.
More bringing her asthma to Room 12, which was next
door to his. In the Red House everybody listened. There
was not much to listen to, so they all listened intently,
with a sort of fury. Mrs. More had reached the second
floor; she panted along the narrow corridor.
"Are you in, Mr. Haxton?" she called.
He did not answer: he would see enough of her at
dinner. The front door banged, and he looked out through
the window. It was Eileen. He looked at his watch and
nodded. It was five o'clock. Mrs. Hughes let Eileen go for
a walk between five and five thirty. In the morning after
the rooms were cleaned, Eileen went out and did a certain
amount of shopping for her mother.
Eileen could write and read. Mrs. Hughes often boastedof the money she had spent on having Eileen taught to
write and read by a special teacher. She could read any
book, so her mother said, adding with a sigh that Eileen
was not good at lip reading; and because mother and
daughter conversed in writing, she had forgotten most of
the sign language the expensive teacher had taught her.
Hailey had told Mr. Haxton that where he was con-
cerned he couldn't understand a thing Eileen wrote. "But
the old girl can," he said, being of a disrespectful nature.
Mr. Haxton had never tried the writing. The thought of
her answering him on paper frightened him. He wished
he knew why.
The wind rattled the windowpanes, and Mrs. More's
bed creaked. Probably she had sat down on it. The front
door opened and closed. That was Eileen coming back.
Mr. Haxton sat back in his armchair and dozed at intervals,
during which the electric light would grow in size and
make him close his eyes quickly. Dinner at the Red House
was around seven thirty. Audrey would beat on a gong,
then perhaps they would have to wait for another twenty
minutes because the food wasn't cooked; but Mrs. Hughes
liked to have the hotel guests punctual at table.
Mr. Haxton was the first in the lounge. The lounge was
small; it opened onto the dining room, which, being two
rooms knocked into one, was large and chilly. There were
about twenty tables in it, and Mrs. More would shout from
her window table to Mr. Ribble who sat at the table nearest
to the door, making stray travelers feel rather out of it.
Mrs. Hughes and Eileen ate in their private sitting room.
Mr. Haxton stood in the lounge waiting patiently. Onlyfive minutes had elapsed since the gong had sounded. Mr.
Ribble arrived. Mr. Ribble was not so much thin as flesh-
less. He wore spectacles which he liked to take off and
hold at arm's length. He wore dark suits because he was a
retired professional man. He had been a chartered account-
ant, and as he was a native of the town, he did not think
much of Mr. Haxton, a haphazard stranger. Nevertheless,
he tried to spend as much time as he could with him. They
went together to the Crown and went together for walks.
They disliked each other in the company of flowers,
meadows, trees, the sea, and the fields of sugar beet that
stretched away on the other side of the Red House.
"Well, well, well," said Mr. Ribble. "And what were
you doing the whole afternoon?"
"I'm afraid I fell asleep in
"Not
very good for you,
ous giggle.
my armchair."
ha-ha." Mr. Ribble had a nerv-
"I know only too well," said Mr. Haxton. He knew that
Mr. Ribble had a rather low opinion of him since he not
only wasn't a native but had also been in business.
"What about the Crown after dinner?" Mr. Ribble asked.
"I'll see," said Mr. Haxton in order to be difficult.
A tallish man in open raincoat had entered. An odd
traveler, and Mr. Haxton and Mr. Ribble joined in dislik-
ing him because he was a stranger. For a moment they
were friends.
"Not a nice evening," said Mr. Haxton in a kindly voice.
That broke the spell of friendship.
"It could be worse," said the stranger.Mr. Ribble did not want to be left out of it. "It might
rain tomorrow," he said.
Mrs. More arrived, heralded by heavy breathing. "Of
course," she said, "I'm almost late." She had been at the
Red House for two long years, and every evening she
made that little joke of hers. "Almost late," she repeated,
going to her table near the window. She had a warm red
face. She was full of good will and always ready to smile.
Few people availed themselves of her good will, and her
smile often went unnoticed. But she did not mind. "You
seem to look a little tired, Mr. Ribble," she boomed.
Mr. Ribble giggled and heaved a sigh of relief; Audrey
had arrived with the soup. Mr. Josey had unobtrusively
taken his seat at a table next to the traveler. The traveler
made some polite remark, but Mr. Josey only grunted. Mr.
Ribble took his spectacles off and buried his weak chin in
the soup plate. His mustache was soon wet. It was the same
tinned soup every night. Mrs. More continued to talk, and
the six windows of the dining room all rattled in the wind.
When the meal was over, Mrs. More went to the lounge
to read the Telegraph, which would be followed by a li-
brary book. Mr. Ribble, who ate fast and wolfishly, was
at the door waiting to intercept Mr. Haxton. Mr. Josey
hurried up to his room, and as Mr. Haxton left the dining
room, just a minute after Mr. Josey, Mr. Ribble said, "He's
at it again."
"I don't mind it at all," said Mrs. More from her arm-
chair. "I like music."
From an upstairs room came the sounds of a piano. It
was played with vigor and barely any feeling. The firsttune, "If You Were the Only Girl in the World," was
followed without pause by "Tea for Two," which, having
reached an abrupt end, was superseded by a bit of Grieg.
“At it again,” said Mr. Ribble. “To be quite frank with
you, I don't even believe he's a Spaniard."
"Now, come, come, Mr. Ribble," said Mrs. More. "He's
as dark as any Spaniard." Having been once to Bordighera,
she knew what she was speaking about.
"I never gave
it much thought," said Mr. Haxton.
He had found Mr. Josey at the Red House when he had
arrived five months ago. Mr. Josey had been there, also,
when Mr. Ribble arrived nearly a year ago.
Mr. Josey, whose real name was José something-or-
other, played the piano incessantly. He had been a cinema
pianist in silent film days. He was a silent man, and most
of his feelings apparently went into his playing. To judge
by the harsh way he played, there was not, according to
Mr. Ribble, much feeling in him. He was a favorite with
Mrs. Hughes. In the evening she would go up to his room
and listen to his playing; and when she was not with him,
she generally hummed "Tea for Two" or the Dvořák
Humoresque. There had been a few people in the Red
House who objected to a fellow guest playing, but they
did not remain guests for long. Mr. Josey was clean shaven
and had distinguished graying hair. His eyebrows were
startlingly black. His clothes were neat and shabby.
"Do you feel like a stroll?" asked Mr. Ribble.
Mr. Haxton had been waiting for that. It would be easy
and satisfactory to say no, but the evening would be long
and dreary."All right," he said, and Mrs. More called after them,
"Keep out of mischief." She laughed her friendly, asthmatic
laugh.
Mr. Josey was playing "Rose Marie." Audrey came out
from Mrs. Hughes' room with a few plates and left the
door ajar. Mr. Haxton and Mr. Ribble gave the room a
quick look. Mrs. Hughes was knitting, and Eileen sat with
a book on her lap. She wasn't reading, thought Mr. Hax-
ton, she simply wasn't reading. Often it seemed to him that
not a word was true about Eileen being able to write and
read. He wished he could have stopped and watched her
longer, but Mr. Ribble would have noticed that. He fol-
lowed him down the stairs. The air was cool and there was
a smell of weeds. "Rose Marie" changed sharply into "The
Wedding of the Painted Doll." They closed the front door
quietly, and the wedding followed them till the even sound
of the sea swallowed it up. The night had come in with the
tide.
It had rained the day before. The Red House was on the
edge of the town, precariously placed between agriculture
and the sea. The town itself appeared never to have known
whether it should be a seaside place or a simple market
town; and somehow the Red House shared that indecision.
They walked past the pottery shop, which was open only
in summer. The next house was the boatbuilder's; but the
boatbuilder had died during the war and his son was an
employee of the Co-Op, so the boatbuilder's house was
empty except for the widow who let rooms now and then
in summer. They passed a street lamp and turned the cor-
ner, and before them was the road leading into the town."Let's go to the Common," said Mr. Ribble.
It was their habit to walk round the Common at least
once a day. They walked round the Common, and Mr.
Ribble boasted of his investments. That did not last long.
Then, as was also their habit, they began to pretend they
had come to the Red House in order to look for a suitable
small house in the district, buy it, and settle down to lead
a pleasant, semirural existence. Now and again they caught
themselves forgetting the purpose of their stay, and they
rushed to the nearest estate agent. But the visits to the
estate agents were becoming rarer as time went by.
"Any luck today?" asked Mr. Ribble.
"No, the agent told me he would phone me the moment
he heard of a suitable house."
"There don't seem to be any," said Mr. Ribble. “Still,
I'm a bachelor and in no hurry. Never had a real home of
my own." He could have cried. Behind him were the years
of dreary landladies and heavy loneliness.
"Well," said Mr. Haxton, "in a sense I'm a bachelor,
too."
"Yes, but you did taste the pleasures of married life."
Mr. Haxton smiled. It was too dark for Mr. Ribble to
see that smile. "In which case," he said, "I ought to take
or buy the Court."
Mr. Ribble shuddered. The Court was an early Georgian
house with a fine façade that had crumbled; but for the
creepers that held on tenaciously, the Court itself looked
as if it might have crumbled altogether. Behind the Court
was a row of council cottages.
"And yet," said Mr. Ribble as they walked on, "theit was
Court used to be a big thing, and twenty years ago
as fine a sight as you could wish to see." His patriotism
made him raise his voice. "A very great gentleman lived
there," he said, hoping to imply tactfully that Mr. Haxton
was neither great nor a gentleman.
"I know," said Mr. Haxton. "Everybody here has told
me about him. His name was Denton, and he owned nearly
the whole town."
"Not the whole town," said Mr. Ribble, "but a large
part of it."
They had reached High Street. "You know," said Mr.
Ribble, who loved a little gossip, "I wouldn't be surprised
if Mrs. Hughes married Mr. Josey." He giggled as they
went past the two lonely mannequins in Burton's window.
Listening to Mr. Ribble, thought Mr. Haxton, was like
not listening at all.
"What a couple they would make," said Mr. Ribble. He
laughed out loud. For him a couple was always a couple
in bed. He had lost many years in the pursuit of women.
There had been barmaids, shopgirls, and at times even typ-
ists. But he was tongue-tied when desire held him hard. All
he could do at such moments was to giggle, and yet he
could have gone on his knees to beg the barmaid or the
shopgirl just to let him touch her. If only he could break
the ice, surely he would be understood; but it was so diffi-
cult to break the ice.
It had happened once that he sat in a train with a fine
fat woman facing him. He loved fat women because they
might fill in his loneliness. Wild seductive conversations he
had carried on with the fat woman in the train, but his lipshad not moved, and at her destination she had alighted
without their having exchanged a word. A further mile-
stone of frustrated love. The barmaid at the Crown looked
as if she might become another milestone, but not if he
could help it. Tonight he must talk to her, seriously,
straight to the point.
"Here is the Crown," he said.
The Crown had a Tudor saloon bar which was almost
fifteen years old. The saloon bar was full.
"Mine," said Mr. Ribble, "is a pint of bitter." That ruled
out spirits. "What will you have?"
Mr. Haxton did not answer. He had not been listening.
He had spent his life surrounded by noise. Noise at home,
noise in his office, and now there was noise in the Crown.
How silent Eileen was; and instead of standing outside her
open door and watching her with her book, he had to be
immersed in noise.
"What will you have?" asked Mr. Ribble, much louder.
"Sorry. I'll have a Guinness."
Why, Mr. Haxton asked himself, was he afraid of hav-
ing a written conversation with Eileen? Hadn't her mother
assured him that she was good at it? He should ask her to
his room, make her sit down, take a bit of paper, and he
would write questions and she would answer him.
"A Guinness," laughed Mr. Ribble. "I didn't know you
needed a tonic. With your figure I wouldn't need a tonic.”
Mr. Haxton laughed in order not to show that he was
hurt. He hated his figure. One shouldn't hate, but surely
one had a right to hate one's own body. Often as he walked
in the street, he would catch sight of his bulk in a shopwindow, and every time the same sad hatred would make
him turn his head away. In his thoughts he was never fat,
yet at the end of them there was his heavy, robust farmer's
figure.
Mr. Haxton took his Guinness, and Mr. Ribble worked
himself nearer to the bar. Mr. Ribble was carrying on a
loud conversation with Mr. Haxton to which Mr. Haxton
did not listen. The fat, rosy barmaid did not listen either,
which was a different matter.
"Your turn, old boy," said Mr. Ribble.
"Oh, I'm sorry," said Mr. Haxton. The barmaid some-
how took notice of them.
"I like," said Mr. Ribble, "a joke." He spoke half to her
and half to Mr. Haxton. "Of course, it'll be my round
again. I'm the chap who always pays." Significantly his
myopic eyes ogled her across the bar. He repeated: "I'm
the fellow who always pays."
The barmaid was everything he wanted a woman to be.
She wore a white silk blouse, and he thought her body
must be quite as white. The blouse had short sleeves and
showed her fat arms nearly bursting out from the sleeves.
She had big breasts.
"A pint for me," said Mr. Ribble, perspiring a little, “a
Guinness for my friend, and what will you have, miss?”
"I'm too busy, thanks ever so."
"Perhaps later," said Mr. Ribble. She left that unan-
swered.
Mr. Ribble continued to talk loudly to Mr. Haxton who
was thinking: How would he begin? What would he call
her in writing? Eileen or Miss Hughes? It was a difficultpoint. "Dear Miss Hughes, how do you do?" He blushed
because it was so stupid. "I am very well, thank you, Mr.
Haxton." He blushed again. But it went on, "It isn't a cold
day, Miss Hughes, but I expect rain tonight." He could not
stop. "Yes, indeed, it looks like rain." He swigged down
the Guinness, and a few drops moistened his red chin.
"What will you have?" he asked Mr. Ribble.
"You hear him?" said Mr. Ribble to the barmaid.
"Hear what?" she asked, but moved on to serve another
customer.
"Well, what will you have?" asked Mr. Haxton, rather
in a hurry because he was writing the next sentence. "Do
you like the sea, Miss Hughes?"
"I'll have a whisky with you," said Mr. Ribble, “and
you, miss, have a drink with me."
"I am afraid of the sea, Mr. Haxton," wrote Eileen. “It
frightens me." That surprised Mr. Haxton. He had not
before looked at the sea in that light.
"I'm too busy, thanks ever so," said the barmaid.
"Perhaps a port," cajoled Mr. Ribble.
"I know, Miss Hughes, that the sea is deep and endless."
He must stop that.
"I don't mind if I do, thanks ever so," said the barmaid,
whose favorite drink was port. She poured herself out the
port and then forgot about it. ("The sea is too near,"
wrote Eileen.)
A tall blond man had come in. He was the barmaid's
type. Tall blond men were always her type but there were
so few of them. He had a long nose and a large attractive
mouth. He had hazel eyes. The barmaid had never beforeseen a man, tall or short, with hazel eyes. He wore a dirty
mackintosh which was open, and he had on a tweed jacket
that must have shrunk in too much rain, thus showing his
wrists, and a pair of gray flannel trousers covered with
spots. He had masses of blond hair. No hat. The barmaid
could have laughed with pleasure because he wore no hat.
Bars were usually full of men in hats. The very fact that
he wore none lifted her above the bar. Though he was
going to give her an order, he took no notice of her. That
was thrilling.
"We might just as well have a drink before we go on,"
he said. He was not speaking to the barmaid; he had ad-
dressed a girl with hair nearly as fair as his but not as true.
The girl wore a mackintosh too; notwithstanding the
crowd, the barmaid noticed that she wore no stockings and
her legs were red with cold. The barmaid served them with
two half pints of bitter. He drank his slowly, but the girl
was thirsty.
"So I shall see the old Red House again," he said to the
girl.
Mr. Haxton and Mr. Ribble looked at him. He was about
twenty-eight years old. Since the barmaid was looking at
him too, Mr. Ribble thought he had the appearance of a
bounder.
"We'd better be off," said the blond man.
"Oh, John, could I have another drink?" the girl asked.
"I am tired," he said, and she followed him from the
saloon bar.
"Cheerio," said the barmaid, lifting her port. She sighed
into her glass of tawny port. She had appraised her ownbody long ago. She knew that young men liked to pinch
it here and there, but it was the elderly ones that went
dotty over it. It was just as well to give Mr. Ribble a smile.
"That fellow," said Mr. Ribble, “looked like a real down
and out." Mr. Ribble was fond of old-fashioned expres-
sions. "Now miss," he went on, "I'm sure you haven't been
here a long time, because if you had we'd be friends by
now." He hoped his voice was the jocular voice of a man
of parts, but desire made it tremble. Still, he felt, the ice
was broken.
"Talk to me some other time," said the barmaid. "I'm
busy."
She began to wash up, and Mr. Ribble leaned discreetly
over the bar. The blouse had tightened over the breasts;
the short skirt showed a few inches of her padded thighs
as she picked up empty stout bottles from the floor. Mr.
Ribble took off his spectacles, pulled out his handkerchief,
and wiped his moist glasses.
"Good night, Mr. Haxton," wrote Eileen. "My mother
expects me to go to bed."
"Time, please,” said the barmaid.
That was Mr. Ribble's cue: should he offer to take her
to the cinema or simply put down his wallet and ask her
to help herself? Anyway, there was only two pounds in his
wallet. But he was afraid. Tomorrow, perhaps, would be a
more suitable day.
"Well, cheerio," said the barmaid. The old boy must
have a packet. He had a good suit. She knew what was
and what wasn't a good suit. She didn't go
after the cut,it was the cloth that mattered. She had a shrewd sense of
prices. "You must be off," she said.
Mr. Ribble could have knelt on the floor, beer-sodden
and heavy with cigarette ends as it was, to thank her for
the hope her eyes gave him.
It was raining steadily as they came out into the street.
The asphalt reflected the illumined dial of St. George's
Church.
"Quite a pleasant evening," said Mr. Haxton.
Mr. Ribble said nothing. Furiously he was trying to de-
cide whether he could bring the barmaid to the Red House.
Alas, that was out of the question. Everybody listened too
much in that house. If he kissed her and she coyly giggled,
Mrs. More would hear it. Moreover, Mrs. Hughes would
already have noticed them coming up the stairs. Where
could he take her? Without even an imaginary roof over
their heads he began to undress her. He undressed her too
slowly, for before she had pulled her stockings off, they
reached the Red House.
They had latchkeys. Mr. Ribble's hand would have
trembled, so he let Mr. Haxton unlock the door. Only one
sad bulb burned between the door and the second floor.
As they reached the first floor landing, Mrs. Hughes, who
apparently had been expecting them, burst from her room.
She was still dressed and was not wearing the pink dressing
gown which now and then Mr. Haxton would catch sight
of early in the morning, or when they returned from the
Crown at night. He looked quickly into the room behind
her. Eileen was not there. She had said good night to him,
hadn't she?"I bet you've been up to some mischief," said Mrs.
Hughes. She was an angular woman of forty-five with a
youngish but shriveled face. Though she was full of moods,
she ran the Red House in an efficient, parsimonious manner.
She could have got a license for the Red House if she had
wanted, but she said she would never condescend to be a
publican.
"Had a drink at the Crown," giggled Mr. Ribble. He
was relieved. She was in a good mood. There had been
nights when she took the trouble to follow him to his room
to tell him he had made so much noise that most of her
guests would leave in the morning. It did not help if he as-
sured her he had been born, as it were, on tiptoes. Thank
God, tonight she was genial.
"Pub-crawling, what?" she said. Definitely she was in a
good mood. "Now, would you believe it," she went on, "a
most interesting visitor has arrived? I'm absolutely thrilled.
John Denton and his wife. They're going to stay here for
at least a week, that's what he said. Looks as distinguished
as his father did. I used to know both father and mother."
Which was true, for the late Mr. Hughes had kept an
ironmonger's shop in High Street, and once when the
butler was laid up with flu, Mrs. Denton had come down
in person to order paraffin.
"It must be that tall young man we saw at the Crown,"
said Mr. Haxton. "After having heard so much about the
Dentons, it'll be interesting to meet one."
"He's the last of them," Mrs. Hughes said proudly.chapter two
Mrs. More awoke with a sense
of emptiness. It was like that
every morning. But it did not
hurt any more, which was bad in
a sense, since it was better to
have pain than nothing at all. She
smiled at her bleak room, offer-
ing it friendship and good will,
provided she had not to start thinking again. Yet there it
was. Peggy: always Peggy.
Mr. More had been a gentle, kindly husband who had
brought many criminals to justice. His wife had doted on
him. So considerate, and a man of even temper. He would
make her early morning tea, and was ready to wash up
if they had no servants. However, Mr. More had been her
second husband. The first husband had not been good. She
would have forgotten him long ago if he had not been the
father of Peggy. When they divorced, Mrs. More had nat-
urally been given custody of Peggy. Mr. More was the
best stepfather in the world, which was not difficult if
Peggy was the stepdaughter.
Peggy had been good, kind, and beautiful, as well as ex-
cellent at school. When. the war came, she volunteered at
once. The services had as high an opinion of her as the
fond parents. Soon she was an officer and went to the
Middle East, where she had a very hush-hush job. When-
ever Mrs. More played bridge with her Arno's Grove
cronies and they asked after Peggy, she felt that to put one
finger to her lips was not adequate. Probably Peggy had
known of El Alamein long before Rommel had reachedthere. Eventually Peggy was given leave, which she de-
served so well. Mr. and Mrs. More could not sleep the
night before she was due to arrive. She was flying home,
the priority girl that she was. The plane crashed, and that
was the end of Peggy; also the end of Mr. More, that
terror of criminals, for he took her death so much to heart
that he died within a year. The doctors said it was uremia
because he had always had bad kidneys, but Mrs. More
knew better.
There was a loud knock on the door, and Mrs. Hailey
came with the early morning cup of tea. That meant a
shilling extra, which Mrs. More did not grudge, since that
cup in bed reminded her of the distant home life where
there had been a Peggy, and a Mr. More too.
"Morning," said Mrs. Hailey. She was short, insignifi-
cant, and she used too much lipstick for her pale face. She
would tell you that she had been a typist, and look what
she had come to. So she never said “sir” or “madam," and
she despised Mr. Hailey heartily. They both liked a drink,
but got drunk only when they had their evening off. To-
day she was liverish.
"Now, would you believe it," she said to Mrs. More,
"that new man was sleeping naked in his bed? And there
she lay beside him, cool as a cucumber. She ought to be
ashamed of herself."
"But who is he? A newcomer?"
"Didn't you know?" Mrs. More shook her head, but
somehow or other while she shook it, her double chin re-
mained stationary. "A man called Denton. Mrs. Hughes is
all a-dither about him. His father was a big white chiefhere, or something. But fancy him lying there naked when
somebody comes into the room. Who does he think I am?"
"Had he no blanket over him?"
"That would be the limit! He was naked to the waist.
See you later." She went, and Mrs. More sat up to drink
her tea.
In the good old days when Peggy was small, she would
get into bed with her mother, and they would wait to-
gether for Mr. More to bring up the tea. Mrs. More sighed
and could have cried, but the tea was hot for a change,
and as she sipped it the comfort of heat came over her. The
next thing to do was to get to the bathroom. The Red
House had only one bathroom, but both Mr. Ribble and
Mr. Haxton let her go first. Mr. Josey did not care one
way or the other; probably he never had a bath. She put
on her dressing gown, and her beslippered flat feet made
much noise on the carpetless corridor. She breathed nor-
mally, her asthma would not start till later. Her heavy
plodding was a tacit sign for Mr. Haxton and Mr. Ribble
to remain put in their rooms. She found the bathroom door
locked. That was like letting one's own side down. She
rattled the door and knocked hard.
"Wait a moment," said a male voice.
Oh, she thought, that must be the naked man. She could
have returned to her room, since she would hear from there
the unbolting of the bathroom door. But somehow her
curiosity kept her in the corridor.
It was soon rewarded: John Denton came out and said,
"I'm so sorry. Kept you waiting a long time, did I?"
"Not at all," Mrs. More said, "not at all. What a fineday." It was raining hard. He was not naked; she noticed
that at once. He wore a bath gown which made him look
exceedingly tall, and his nose was beautifully long. Mr.
More had had a short nose, which, though he was a tee-
totaler, was always red. She watched the young man go
to his room and then went into the bathroom.
Every morning she gave herself ten minutes in her bath;
to stay there longer would be unfair to Mr. Haxton and
Mr. Ribble. Often the water was tepid, but today it was
as hot as the tea.
Sitting in the tub, which was almost too narrow for her,
she thought of Eileen. In a curious way she would often
think of Eileen in her bath. Her heart had gone out to her
the day she had come. As young as Peggy would be, and
a poor little thing; whereas Peggy, if she were alive, would
be wonderful in health and beauty. She had tried to speak
to her slowly, for Mrs. Hughes had told her Eileen was
good at lip reading. There were no results. Perhaps Eileen
understood her, but she was quite uncertain about that.
Mrs. Hughes had boasted to her that, sparing no expense,
she had had Eileen taught to write and read. Mrs. More
found an envelope on one of the tables of the lounge and
wrote simply, "We'll be friends, I hope." Eileen looked at
it for a long while and did not take the pencil offered her.
Then she left.
Mrs. More waited for the next opportunity. It came a
few days later. "Now then, Eileen dear," she said, and
wrote a simple sentence asking if Eileen would tell her
how she felt that fine morning. Eileen did write that time.
There were a few recognizable letters, but they formed nopattern of words. As you smile at a child trying to draw a
puffertrain, Mrs. More said it was wonderful. Then Mrs.
Hughes came and fetched Eileen.
"Mrs. More," she said later, "Eileen is very shy of stran-
gers. You won't mind, I'm sure, leaving her alone."
Mrs. More blushed, and though her friendly heart ached,
she left Eileen alone; but whenever she saw her, she tried
to smile her whole understanding and pity at her.
"You see, Mrs. More," said Mrs. Hughes one day, "writ-
ing for Eileen is like speech for others. There are some
people one wants to speak to and some one doesn't."
Mrs. More would have liked to pack and go elsewhere,
but elsewhere the tea might be even more lukewarm.
The ten minutes were up, and she shuffled back to her
room. There was an animated conversation going on in the
room where the newcomers were. She would not have
minded listening, but Mr. Ribble would be going to the
bathroom. She shuffled on.
John Denton was dressing, the girl still lay in bed. “I

"I know I'll love it."

There had been no sugar beet behind the Red House when

Everything with him was perfect. She had been saying

She had a few pennies which might come in useful. More-

Her background was a poor genteel mother in Surrey,
four years in the Wrens, two years a private secretary to
a film producer who never found money to produce films,
and the last eight months looking for a job. Though he
was only four years her senior, he was frequently surprised

a sparrow jumping about gently while hawks circled above.
He didn't quite know whether he was a hawk, too.
"Get up," he said. He went to the cracked mirror to

The Crown bar was empty when he arrived. He knew

Skinner had expected something like that. He was far

other man was apparently always in the right. "All right,”

can just as well go. There's no gratitude in you, is there?”
Skinner was pleasantly surprised that the interview had
gone off so well. He took his hat off, patted his few black
hairs brushed across his bald pink crown, and put on the
hat again. "You must blame Mr. Denton, not me," he said.
There were now plenty of people in the bar. They all

"Can't you," John said, "let me have twenty pounds?
I'm completely broke, you know."
"It would amount to the same thing," said Skinner. He

work you'd get somewhere. I must be going now, the
missis is angry if I'm late for dinner. She will be glad to

remember her? She was old Thomson's widow."
He stood up and slowly, with careful deliberation, took
a pound note from his pigskin wallet. "I don't want you to
say, Mr. John, that I let you go empty handed." He waited
for John to pick up the pound note, and then, well satisfied
every respect, he went home to his dinner.

Mr. Josey was a man who gave
life precisely as little as life had
given him, which was mighty lit-
tle. He had wanted to be a great
pianist, but had been a piano-
player in provincial cinemas in
the days of the silent film. Then
when the silent films-probably
in order to push him out of a job-gave way to the talkies,
he had to look hard for a job. He tried to get work as a
musician, but nobody thought that he was one. Starvation
was close at hand when suddenly he became a commercial
traveler. Nails he sold, and sold them quite successfully.
Now he was fifty-five, retired from nails, and had just
enough money to pay for rent, smoke ten cigarettes a day,
and buy a new suit every five years.
So, taking it all in all, life had been vile to him; but he
had been equally vile to life. Wars and pestilence, provided
they did not interfere with his meager income, could come
and go; here and there, at rare intervals, a man or woman
would extend a friendly hand, which was invariably
ignored. He had drifted to England many years ago, for
there were more cinemas in England than in his native
Santander.
That was the only thing he knew of England, and he
wasn't certain whether England was a kingdom or a repub-
lic, though in his silent film days he'd had to play "God
Save the King" often enough. But life hadn't been com-
pletely an inimical vacuum: he had met Mrs. Hughes, and
in those days there still was a Mr. Hughes. His firm didbusiness with Mr. Hughes, an ironmonger in High Street.
Mr. Hughes died, Mrs. Hughes sold the business and
bought the Red House, and by then Mr. Josey had put
enough aside to retire on practically nothing. For a great
concert pianist it was a comedown to sell nails, but on the
other hand he could tell himself with truthfulness that he
hadn't the vaguest idea what he had been selling.
In Mrs. Hughes' house, which, strictly speaking, was the
floor above the shop, there stood a piano. When Mr.
Hughes one day took the traveler upstairs for a cup
of tea,
he caught sight of the piano, and thus the friendship began.
Mrs. Hughes was the daughter of a doctor, which made

pens to people who come from distant parts, Mr. Josey had
by then not only completely forgotten his native Spanish

ironmonger's wife was deeply moved. Obligingly, Mr.

was started, the piano from above the shop was installed in
Mr. Josey's room, and quietly he allowed Mrs. Hughes to
pamper him. Slowly, under her devoted care, he began to
learn the meaning of the expression, thank you.
He had now finished playing "The Indian Love Call" and

Mr. Haxton sat at the window,
one shoe on and the other in an
expectant position on the floor.
On the other side of the window
the October light was gray like
the water of the estuary. The
huge sugar-beet field was of an
angry green. An island of anger in
a world of quiet gray. The point, needless to say, was who
had killed Mrs. Hughes? It left him detached, and he was
annoyed, not so much with the murder as with his inability
to summon up enough strength to put on the other shoe.
He tried to get encouragement from the photograph. The
small mouth with the small even teeth trying to produce a
smile should have made him hurry. His wife had never
been slow. Her life was regulated by a clock that ticked
faster than a clock should. She managed to get round the
twenty-four hours quicker than anybody else he knew.
Naturally that was praiseworthy, but it somehow made his
day go much slower.
He wondered if she was still in such a hurry or if, distant
from him, the clock balanced time better. It was a detached
thought with no longing and no rancor. The shoe was still
waiting. One day, perhaps, he would try to tackle the
failure of thirty years of married life. As he leaned forward
to pick up the shoe, there was a sharp knock on the door.
Probably another policeman or detective, the house was
full of them.
It was Mr. Ribble, dressed in his best dark blue suit, and
washed and shaved. He had cut himself a little behind theleft ear, but the dried blood was clean and wholesome.
"I thought," he said, "you'd be up by now."
"But I am up," said Mr. Haxton.
"You know what I mean. I didn't sleep the whole night.
It's awful. What will my sister say?" His sister hadn't for-
given him for not going to live with her when he had
retired to his birthplace.
"She can't say much, can she?" said Mr. Haxton. So
there it was. Poor Mrs. Hughes stabbed to death, no longer
of the living, her deaf and dumb daughter left alone in the
world, and everybody trying to work out how far their
lives were affected by the murder. Mrs. Hailey had
brought up the tea almost ice cold, then had spent about a
quarter of an hour with him trying to puzzle out what
would become of the Haileys' job. And he himself would
surely find, too, some personal embarrassment in the mur-
der, which would make Mrs. Hughes take an unimportant
secondary position. By the time they had all sorted out the
problems her death had caused, she might easily be for-
gotten.

"I expect we'll have breakfast."
"I must say I admire your nerve."

stretched hand.
"Good morning," he said.
Mr. Haxton hadn't bothered to speak. By then he had
completely remembered the publican. A stupid man, usu-
ally on the verge of bankruptcy, bullied by his customers
and despised by his landlords, the brewers. The beer had
always seemed flat in his pub. He was definitely uninter-
ested in Mr. Benson.
"Well, gentlemen," said the inspector, “we're no further
than we were when I saw you last in the small hours."
"It's always like that at the beginning," said Mr. Benson.
Mr. Haxton could not resist a smile: he had the same
voice as that publican up North. More educated, but funda-
mentally the same.
"These gentlemen were at the Crown," went on Mr.
Benson, "and you saw them coming back after the waitress
had discovered the corpse." The inspector nodded. “I don't
think I'll need you for the time being," Mr. Benson said
in a final voice.
But Mr. Ribble's curiosity was aroused. "Could you per-
haps tell me I promise I wouldn't repeat it-what is hap-
pening? I am so interested."Mr. Benson smiled. "What's happening?" he said.
"We're looking for the murderer. That's all."
The inspector was kinder. "We have about fifty men
out; they're all over the countryside."
"Fifty men," said Mr. Ribble, impressed. The silence
that followed clearly indicated he should be gone. Mr.
Haxton took him by the arm. Mr. Benson closed the door.
"I have a jolly shrewd guess who killed Mrs. Hughes,"

"I should have told you," he said, "that for the time

"Yes, indeed," said Mr. Ribble. "I can well understand

"You mean to say," said Mr. Haxton, who wasn't pleased

Mr. Ribble giggled, and Mr. Haxton said something

"Mr. Denton," said the inspector, "would you please

John rose and followed him. The inspector said, "We've

He turned his head back in order to smile at John.
"You questioned me enough last night," said John.
The inspector could forgo answering as they had

"Well, well, well," he said, "so we meet again." John

ton. I didn't know you were in the murdering line. Still,
life is full of surprises, what?"
John said nothing. His hands were under control. Never-
theless, it was better and easier to leave them in his pockets.
The sun forced its way through the clouds; since he was
looking at the window the sun was straight in his
turned a little sideways.

"Now then, my friend," Mr. Benson continued, "come

He touched the lapel of John's coat. He had fat hands,
but he touched the lapel lightly. "Not bad stuff," he said,

thick with tobacco. "Come on, Denton, tell me why you

"Leave me alone,” said John.
"I'll leave you alone when you've told me the truth.
But I'll get it out of you, don't you worry." He stopped

“I never killed Mrs. Hughes," said John, trying to pull

"Just stay where you are, my friend," said Mr. Benson.

"No," said the inspector without undue enthusiasm.
"You know," said John, a little more sure of himself,

that sort of bullying the first time. Two years had taught
him what a fool he had been; and when he came out, Wil-

sat on the shoulder of the 'tecs and talked and talked and
talked."
"So you have an alibi,” said Mr. Benson, “and a girl. I'll

"I happened to have been born here," said John, "and all
this around here used to belong to my father. So I thought
I might bring my girl here for a short honeymoon." His
hands were trembling again. If Benson ran into Skinner, he
might end up in having him arrested for attempted black-

every one of them.
"It is quite true," said the inspector, "that his father had
a large estate here."
"And then," said Mr. Benson, "you come back to your
home town, and the first day a lady is stabbed to death.
Tell me another." He raised his voice. "Come on, speak up
and no lying. I'll get the truth from you."
"I didn't know Mrs. Hughes, I never heard of her. Infact," he said triumphantly, "I tried to get a room at the
Crown, but they were full. You ask the manager of the
Crown."
"Go and ring up the Crown," said Mr. Benson, and the
inspector was glad that he could leave the room. "And

"I told you I had one."
"I'll have a talk with her. Your honeymoon won't last
much longer if I tell her a few tidbits about you."
"Do so," said John. He must see Skinner. That was terri-
bly important. But what would he say to him? Or would
it be wiser to say nothing?
"You do look worried," said Mr. Benson. "So you say
you were at the movies holding hands while Mrs. Hughes

"We didn't hold hands."
"Getting cheeky."
The inspector had come back. "Yes, he tried to get a
room at the Crown the day before yesterday, and the man-
ager remembers him going in there yesterday morning for
a drink. He was with a Mr. Skinner."
"He used to be my father's agent," said John. He was
lost, there was no getting away from it.
"Call the girl," said Mr. Benson. When Sheila arrived,
he said to John, "Now you can go outside. Call for Mar-
shall. Let him stay out there with him."
Sheila was puzzled and afraid. She looked at John, then

"Marshall, come up."
Marshall was a seedy detective sergeant with an obse-quious smile that seemed to be stuck to his lips. "Talk to
Mr. Denton," said Mr. Benson with a sneer he enjoyed
immensely, "I'll talk here to the young lady."
John went out to join Marshall on the landing. The door
closed, and he remembered that he should have given Sheila
an encouraging smile.
"A nice day," said Marshall.
"Maybe for you,” said John.
"You needn't be rude," said Marshall, who hated John
because he was taller and certainly more successful with
women than he. That girl of his looked a nice piece.
"And you needn't talk," said John.
Marshall tried to think of a devastatingly rude answer,
but as none came he glared at John. When people were in
his power, he was good at glaring. Mr. Haxton, followed
by Mr. Ribble, came down the stairs. John blushed a little.
Mr. Ribble, who held his spectacles in his hand, was rather
blind and went past John without recognizing him; but
Mr. Haxton took in the situation and tactfully did not look
at them, which made John blush more. They stood there

could not make out what it all meant, so there was no need

"I am Mr. Strong," he said, "Mrs. Hughes' solicitor.
Could I see the chief inspector?"
"He is busy," said Marshall.
"Then I shall be with Miss Hughes," said Mr. Strong.
"And please call me when the chief inspector can see me."He glanced at his brown boots which he loved much. Once
many years ago he had seen a county court judge wearing
brown boots. It had made a deep impression on him, and
ever since he had worn brown boots. That day being a
special occasion, he wore a new pair which were a bit
painful, but he thought only of their glory.
While he was speaking to Marshall, John watched Eileen
standing in the background with the light from the lounge
on her hair and on the white ribbon. As a matter of fact,
he thought, she should be wearing a black one. Then their
eyes met. Her eyes as she looked at him were the eyes of
one who, having entered a dark room, becomes slowly ac-
customed to each object in the room and sees it clearly.
Did she know, he wondered, that her mother was dead?
Or perhaps they hadn't told her. But all that mattered were
her eyes, and he forgot Benson, the murder, and Sheila.
Then Mr. Strong took her to the lounge. She had fine legs;
he hadn't noticed that before.
"What are you doing?" Marshall asked.
"As you can see, I'm lighting a cigarette."
"I don't know if you're allowed to smoke."
"But I do."
Marshall thought again of devastating replies but re-
mained sullen and silent. If a stranger had passed them, he
would have imagined it was John who was guarding
Marshall.
"Come in, my friend, come in," called Mr. Benson. He
was mightily pleased with himself.
John went in and saw that Sheila was a Sheila he hadn't
known before. She stood beside Mr. Benson, one leg for-ward, her belly slightly pushed out, trying to appear sure
of herself, though she wasn't quite. Anyway, that didn't

of herself. That girl had slept with him the night before,
he couldn't lie near enough to her in bed, yet now she was
working hard to secure her passage into a distant world

"Miss Greene," said Mr. Benson, "has corroborated that

"I want to go upstairs," said Sheila to Mr. Benson, "and

"You do that, Miss Greene," said Mr. Benson. It seemed

He decided to take matters into his own hands. "Miss
Greene," he said, "was very upset when I told her a few

"And don't you molest her."
"I don't understand what you mean," said John. But
he did; he had spent his life understanding that sort of
meaning.
"I don't want to speak to him," said Sheila; then she

John took a step forward. "You crook," she said. He
stopped.
“I'll ask Marshall to get your luggage down," said Mr.

“But,” said John, "I told you from the start that I had

Sheila summoned up all her anger to give him a wither-

"Have a cigarette," said the inspector."Thank you very much," he said.
The inspector had a small silver cigarette case, large
enough to hold ten cigarettes. John's fingers trembled as
they picked out one; the inspector pretended that he did
not notice it. John took out a box of matches. The match

"Permit me," said the inspector.
"Thank you very much," said John.

Mr. Benson returned. He pursed his mouth as he looked

"Am I now free to go?" John asked.
"Up to a point. But I want you to stay here in this hotel.
I want everybody to stay, Denton, and that includes you.
Just till we find the murderer, and then you can go back

He was a good one at laughing. When he finished laughing,

"I don't want to stay here," said John. There were eight
shillings left of the pound Sheila had given him the day

so how could he stay on? But he mustn't tell Benson he had

course, it was another case of attempted blackmail. He
should have thought of that before he took the train in
London, but he had not.
"You'll have to," said Mr. Benson. "Now I am busy. I've
spent enough time with you. If you stay here quietly and
try to be useful, you'll be able to go in a few days."
"All right, I'll stay here," said John suddenly, remember-
ing that their return tickets were, or rather had been, in
Sheila's bag.
"I know you have nowhere to go," said Mr. Benson
triumphantly. "But don't you start making a nuisance of
yourself in this town. Your father might have been rich,
but you're not. So don't you run up bills. Have you
enough money to pay for your bill here?"
"Yes," lied John.
"That's good. Now run along." He raised his voice.
"And don't imagine that I'm through with you. Perhaps
that alibi was just a fake." He opened the door. Thank
God, there was no Marshall out there. "If you find out
anything, just come and see me. You're a good one at find-ing a police station." He laughed John out of the room.
The inspector closed the door.
"Well," said Mr. Benson, "we frightened him out of his
wits. He's an unlucky fellow. If he hadn't been caught in
time, he'd have got himself mixed up in a little murder.
You remember the Williams murder?" The inspector said
he remembered it. "He was just on the fringe of it. Knew
nothing about it. Probably they didn't trust him enough.
Now what do you think he's doing here?"
"I suppose he tried to swank to the girl that his father
had owned half of this town and get some money out of
her," said the inspector.
"Very likely, but I don't think she had much. He's a fool
to pal up with girls like that. Such girls have ten or fifteen
quid, and when it's all spent they begin to scream their

is somebody else I want to see here." He smiled broadly.
"Mr. Strong the solicitor wants to see you."
"Let him wait. He's the family solicitor, isn't he?"
"Yes."
"I'll see him presently. In the meantime, tell him that
whatever happens they must keep this hotel open. I don't
want the people here to disperse.”
"You think somebody in this house committed the mur-
der?"
"Definitely," said Mr. Benson, "but I wish there weren't
a stone path leading past the rhododendron. Footprints
would have been helpful." The inspector blushed, though
it had not been Mr. Benson's intention to slight him; for
Mr. Benson was far from a bad man. He was huge instature, huge in his laugh, and loved his food, which he ate
like a pig. His job was everything to him. A criminal was a
person to be apprehended, because apprehending criminals
and detecting crime was what he was born for. Outside his
profession he liked mankind, and if he had not met John
professionally, he would willingly have lent him the money
for his return fare to London. But he hated weakness. He
admired the tough man who killed without remorse and
was ready to shoot when he came to arrest him. The tough
man was, in a sense, Mr. Benson's equal. John and his kind
were pitiful creatures and easy game.
In a distant way he was sorry for John. Denton must be
sad because his girl had left him; but on the other hand, if
he hadn't told her the truth about John, the alibi wouldn't
have been worth while. "I don't think much of that girl,"
he said under his breath. "I won't be long," he said to the
inspector. "Then we'll get cracking."
"I've sent Sergeant Marshall and Sergeant Jones up to
the servants' quarter," said the inspector.
"I bet they won't find much there," said Mr. Benson.
He was a successful detective. He believed that his success
was mainly due to his knowledge of how to handle people.
He hoped the inspector had learned something from the
way he had handled John. On the landing was a policeman
waiting for the inspector.
"By the way," said Mr. Benson, "what was the name
of the man whom Denton drank with at the Queen yester-
day?"
"The Crown," said the inspector, pleased to have his
own back. "A Mr. Skinner, used to be his father's agent.""That's right. Ring him up. One never knows. Tell him
we'll see him some time this evening."
This was work: he loved it and the more there was, the
better he coped with it. Of course it was the murder he
was interested in, but with chaps like Denton something
interesting might crop up on the side. He went into the
lounge. It was empty.
"Which," he asked, "is Mrs. More's room?"
"This way, sir," said Marshall, who had just come down.
"I don't need you," he said to Marshall on the second
floor landing.
Marshall said nothing. He was young and ugly with full
wet lips. He was proud of his lips, but experience had
bitterly taught him that women didn't think much of his
wet kissing. He was lazy and therefore ambitious. One day
he hoped to reach the stage where he could speak to an
underling as Mr. Benson spoke to him.
"Come in," said Mrs. More. Her voice was cheerful. The
excitement of the murder had pushed Peggy almost into
the background. Needless to say, she would be remorseful
but only after the excitement was over. "Well, I never,"
she cried. "Mr. Benson. What a wonderful surprise."
"Dear Mrs. More," said Mr. Benson, and because he
liked a little monkeying in his private life, he lifted her
hand and kissed it heavily. Some saliva remained on the
hand, but that, too, was part of the game. "I bet you're
surprised to see me."
"It's wonderful to see you," said Mrs. More. Mr. Benson
had worked for years under the late superintendent. To see
him was like being back home in Arno's Grove. "I sentyou," she said with playful reproach in her voice, “a
Christmas card last year, but Mrs. Benson never sent me
one." She'd had eleven Christmas cards of which she had
boasted to Mrs. Hughes, but a round dozen would have
looked nicer on the mantelpiece.
"My wife wasn't well last Christmas," said Mr. Benson.
"Well, how are you?"
"It isn't bad here, rather nice in summer. And how's
Mrs. Benson? I hope she's better."
"Middling, but she's very fat again. The doctor says it
isn't good for her heart." Mrs. Benson weighed eighteen
stone. Mr. Benson had one thing in common with Mr.
Ribble: he liked them fat, but eighteen stone was a bit too
much. On the other hand they had been married eighteen
years, and she was a good soul who looked after him well.
"I'm sorry to hear it," said Mrs. More. "And how's that
dear little niece who used to stay with you?"
Mr. Benson told her the niece was well. Thus they
talked of friends and relations till Mr. Benson asked her
whether he could have a cup of tea with her. He liked a
cup of tea that time of the morning. Mrs. More rang the
bell, and if Mrs. Hailey hadn't known the chief inspector
was in her room, she wouldn't have bothered to answer it;
but she came quickly: perhaps at last she would be ques-
tioned by the Scotland Yard man. She was disappointed to
find it was only a matter of tea.
Soon the tea arrived, and as they sat drinking it and
talking of cozy days, Mrs. More said, "What do you make
of the murder?"
"What do you make of it?" Mr. Benson answered."I've been thinking hard," said Mrs. More. "What about
that young couple who came here the day before yester-
day? That's one guess."
"Not much," smiled Mr. Benson. It was nice to sit there
talking to an old friend.
"Two strangers dined here last night," said Mrs. More.
She hadn't yet forgiven their early departure.
"They left before the murder. I checked up on them.”
"How quick you are," Mrs. More said. It was great fun
to be, so to speak, in the C.I.D. again.
"Think of somebody else," said Mr. Benson. She poured
him another cup of tea.
"The Spaniard who lives here," she whispered. "Every-
body knew that Mrs. Hughes had a crush on him. Poor
woman, but really she shouldn't have behaved like that at
her age."
"The Spaniard with the dagger," said Mr. Benson, drop-
ping a third lump of sugar into his tea.
"But he was in his room the whole evening," said Mrs.
More triumphantly. "So I caught you."
"I knew all along that he was in his room," laughed Mr.
Benson.
"You couldn't know that. He lives in the next room. I
was the only person up here beside him. Only I knew that
he didn't leave his room."
"But," laughed Mr. Benson, "you told that last night to
the inspector."
"That's just cheating," laughed Mrs. More. She was glad
to be with a friend once more. "And to think of it," she
said, "when I went out last night for a moment, I musthave passed the corpse twice." She had said it so often to
herself and anybody she could get hold of that the creep-
iness of it had ceased altogether. At first she was stunned
that a murder had entered her life, but that wore off in
twelve hours.
"One wouldn't expect it in such a sleepy little town,"
said Mr. Benson. "And now," he said, beaming, "we're go-
ing to search your room. Don't ask for the warrant because
we've got it."
"How exciting," said Mrs. More. "Of course the mur-
derer, knowing who I am, might easily have hidden the
knife in here."
"He might," smiled Mr. Benson.
Mrs. More watched the men search her room. She ad-
mired their efficiency. Now and then she giggled. It was
fun; but once she gave Mr. Benson a quick glance. Their
past and their friendship had worn off his face. He was
hard, huge, and alert.
"All right," he said when the search was over. "Now
we'll go to the next room." His smile returned. "Good-by
for the present," he said to Mrs. More. "I'll see you anon.
Thanks a lot for the tea."
Her ebullience was gone. She hadn't liked the look on
his face. Perhaps the man was suspecting her. "Good-by,"
she said. She should have objected to them searching her
room.
John's room was searched quickly. There was nothing
in it. "You see," said Mr. Benson to the inspector. “Just as
I expected. She took everything away. Even his tooth-
brush. We'd better look out that he doesn't break intoWoolworth's to get himself another." The empty room
made him despise John. A poor petty crook without even
a toothbrush; and because he despised him, he dismissed
him from his mind. This was an interesting case, and John,
where he was concerned, had ceased to be interesting. He
wouldn't bother to see Skinner. John wasn't in it, and for
confidence tricks and such things he hadn't time for the
moment. He must find the murderer: that was all that
mattered.
Mr. Ribble was equally interested in the murder. There
was a little crowd around him at the Crown. They asked
him questions, and if he didn't know the answers he in-
vented them. "I did," he said for the fifth time, "say to my
friend here as we walked down the path that the rhododen-
dron bush looked very curious to me. Just think of it, poor
Mrs. Hughes was in that bush. But I swear I suspected
something as I went past it."
It was easy for him to talk like that, for Mr. Haxton
wasn't listening. He held his glass of beer and at moments
quite forgot about it. He wasn't thinking of the murder.
It was difficult to appreciate an unknown fact. There had
been, of course, many murders committed in the world,
and there were many more to come. However, since he
had reached the age of sixty-one with murder always at
arm's length, he felt it would take him a long time to
understand that in a sense he was involved in one. He
couldn't dismiss it from his mind because it hadn't pene-
trated his mind. But what did Eileen feel?
"Dear Miss Hughes, I am so sorry to hear that your
mother is dead.” His glass became the pencil, and he heldit up so that it shouldn't be in the way when Eileen wrote
her answer. "Dear Mr. Haxton, I can't believe that my
mother was killed. Please forgive me, but I can't." Mr.
Haxton drank his beer. So he wasn't alone in refusing to
understand murder.
"Now you, miss, have a drink," said Mr. Ribble to the
barmaid.
"I don't mind if I do, thanking you," she said. She was
now better disposed towards him. He had become a ro-
mantic figure. He was in a murder: it was like a film. At
any moment he might turn into Robert Montgomery, who
was her favorite on the screen. Mr. Ribble ogled her de-
lightedly. She sighed, for he would never become Robert
Montgomery. Still, there had been a murder in his hotel,
which was the next best thing. "A port,” she said.
Though Mr. Ribble enjoyed his temporary importance,
the murder left him cold. He wanted a nice fat woman in
bed with him, and the fate of other people didn't come into
that. He was pleased that he had been told to stay on at the
Red House; otherwise his sister might have claimed him.
"Morning, Frank," said a voice behind him. It was Mr.
Strong, his sister's husband.
"Morning," said Mr. Ribble.
The little crowd eyed Mr. Strong respectfully. He was
a prominent local citizen, a councilor, and at any time he
might become the chairman of the Urban Council. Mr.
Strong loved that respect, and it seemed to him, as he was
a short, conceited man, that the respect was built on friend-
ship. "For the time being," he said, as though he were ad-
dressing a public meeting, "we have fixed everything up.""Have they found the murderer?" asked a customer.
"Come, come, Mr. Wills," said Mr. Strong, who did not
care for interruptions. "You're putting the cart before the
horse. What I was going to say was that the Red House
remains open, business as usual, and you, Frank, and you,
Mr. Haxton, you just carry on as if nothing had hap-
pened." He wouldn't have been surprised if they had
cheered him or at least said "Hear, hear."
"What is going to happen to Miss Hughes?" Mr. Hax-
ton asked.
"I'm going to consult my wife about that," said Mr.
Strong.
"She's still at the Red House?"
"For the time being. I haven't yet made a decision. But
I can assure you, Mr. Haxton, that we'll do our best by
poor Eileen."
That was surely an occasion for cheers, but instead of
cheers the barmaid asked, "That's the deaf and dumb girl,
ain't she?"
"Yes," said Mr. Ribble.
"They say she's proper beautiful," said the barmaid.
"I know somebody else who is beautiful," said Mr.
Ribble meaningly.
"Well," said Mr. Strong, blushing for his brother-in-law,
"that's as far as we are, and I'll be seeing you this after-
noon, Frank."
"I might be out," said Mr. Ribble. He waited for his
brother-in-law to depart, then said to Mr. Haxton, “I've a
suspicion that your round is coming." And he laughed at
the barmaid.She did not laugh. She was thinking of the poor deaf
and dumb girl whose mother was dead. "If," she said turn-
ing to Mr. Haxton, "she's deaf and dumb, she can't speak,
can she?"
"She can't," said Mr. Haxton, "but she can write and
read."
"Well, I never," said the barmaid.
Mr. Ribble was becoming jealous of Eileen.
"I think we must get back to lunch," said Mr. Haxton.
"There's nobody to chivvy us about today," said Mr.
Ribble.
"I'm going," said Mr. Haxton.
There was nothing else left for Mr. Ribble but to follow.
He was far from displeased with himself. He was making
headway with the barmaid. If only he found the murderer,
she would surely fall for him in a big way. Mr. Haxton
walked in silence beside him. They went down High Street
and cut across the Common; the road was now straight,
on one side houses and on the other the sugar beet still wet
from yesterday's rain.
It was all very well, thought Mr. Haxton, to tell them
they should stay on at the Red House. Probably the
Haileys, when they were not drunk, would manage it quite
well; but in the long run he knew he would have to go.
He
would have to find some other lodgings in an equally sor-
did room, and there would be no Eileen. His wife would
indeed be delighted if she heard that he had been living in
a hotel where the proprietress had been murdered. The sort
of hotel he would choose, she would certainly say. Perhaps
he had been as unlucky in choosing his hotel as he had beenunlucky in choosing her. Or was it all of his own making?
To have reached sixty-one and to have left no impact on
life. A sad marriage that ended painlessly, and then a mur-
der to chase him from a room he had grown accustomed
to. It must surely be his own fault. If he had killed Mrs.
Hughes, then at least it would be of his own doing; but
throughout his life he had been the spectator who some-
how or other inevitably got it in the neck. But Mrs. Hughes
lay dead, and he was walking in the breeze. Surely he
shouldn't complain. He sighed dismally.
"A penny for your thoughts," said Mr. Ribble.
"They're not worth it."
"I've been thinking hard," said Mr. Ribble. "I know I
shouldn't say this, but I know I can trust you." He dropped
his voice. "Mr. Josey. What do you think of him?”
"I don't think anything of him."
"Well, I wouldn't be surprised if ..."
"You mustn't say that," said Mr. Haxton sharply.
"I said nothing," smiled Mr. Ribble.
They walked on in silence. They reached the corner
lamp; a cat sat in front of the pottery shop, and three peo-
ple stood outside the Red House. They were staring at it
unashamed. In fact they thought it was their prerogative.
Respectfully and with some envy in their aspect, they
stepped back to let the two hotel guests pass. They were
two women and a man. Just then Hailey appeared, drunk.
The excitement and the absence of Mrs. Hughes to hold
him in check were reasons enough for him to have drunk
copiously. Moreover, the Scotland Yard bloke had praised
him for his clever answers."What the hell are you waiting here for?" he demanded.
"I'll call one of the
coppers if you don't go. We've got
plenty about."
The women and the man took themselves down the
road. They waited quietly for Hailey to go back to the
Red House. As the front door banged, without saying a
word to one another, they returned to their watch. Two
little boys and a sheep dog joined them.chapter five
Bank Chambers was an extrava-
ganza of the late Edwardian days.
It was four stories high with two
turrets, four bays, and eight bal-
conies. The Georgian houses on
its right and its left made it look
almost more hideous than it was.
Nevertheless, it had a fine repu-
tation. It had central heating, a lift, and the highest rent in
town. It had been built by a private bank which was, after
the first big war, swallowed up by a larger bank with
offices farther up High Street. Now beside the porticoed
entrance there was a land agent's office, next to it a cleaner,
and next a respectable house. On the first floor was a doc-
tor's surgery, on the second Mr. Strong's office, and on the
third Mr. Strong's flat; on the fourth lived a lady who let
rooms to lodgers, but discreetly because it was against the
letter of the lease.
Mrs. Strong loved her flat. So spacious, she would say,
and such a fine view. "Any moment now," she said to her
daily woman, Mrs. Carver, "that poor deaf and dumb girl
will arrive." Mrs. Carver had heard that remark several
times in the last half hour.
Mrs. Strong resembled her brother in that she was as
lean as he, but her face was astonishingly large. Though
she usually wore heavy tweed jackets, today she was wear-
ing a black dress in honor of poor Eileen whom she had
asked to tea.
"And don't forget, Mrs. Carver, that poor thing can't
hear and can't speak."Mrs. Carver had heard that before Mr. Strong had gone
to fetch Eileen. Over lunch they had debated the girl's
future. Should she or shouldn't she remain at the Red
House? Generally speaking, what was one supposed to do
with a deaf and dumb? They didn't know; moreover, it
was difficult for Mr. Strong to discuss the matter with his
wife, since his knowledge of Eileen was more intimate than
hers, and, professionally speaking, he felt that he shouldn't
divulge more to his wife than was strictly necessary. Out-
side of local politics Mr. Strong was a lazy man. He had a
clerk, one Foster, who did most of his work for him. Not-
withstanding his laziness, Mr. Strong was a fine solicitor in
the negative sense. He kept your secrets, for it was less of
an effort to keep them to himself. He never gave hasty ad-
vice because he preferred not to give any advice at all.
His latchkey rattled, and Mrs. Strong rose to open the
door.
"Good afternoon, miss," said Mrs. Carver, forgetting in
the excitement the many warnings Mrs. Strong had given
her.
Mrs. Strong took three sedate steps forward, her podgy
face and narrow neck bowed with stiff ceremony, her
hand extended, and a smile an undertaker would have en-
vied began to spread from lips to fat cheeks. Silently she
shook Eileen by the hand. They had met seldom, and when
Eileen tried to sum her up in order to pigeonhole her for
the benefit of her own wordless world, Mrs. Strong be-
came nervous under her gaze, though it was neither polite
nor rude, just steadfast. Still holding Eileen's hand, she
took her to the tea table."Should I," she asked her husband, "write out questions
for her, or what?”
"Do nothing of the sort," said Mr. Strong. "She's tired
and upset, and I wouldn't worry her. Just give her tea and
leave her alone."
"How long will she stay?" asked Mrs. Strong.
"I'll take her back in an hour's time."
It seemed to Mrs. Strong that an hour would be quite
enough.
Mr. Foster put his head in the doorway. It was a hairless

"Chief Inspector Benson to see you," he said.
"I'm coming," said Mr. Strong, and with his new boots

Mr. Benson stood at the window. "A nice view, this,"

"It's much better upstairs in my flat. Sit down, my dear
sir."
Mr. Benson did not sit down. "I'm puzzled by that girl,"

"Which girl?" asked Mr. Strong, in order to gain time.
"Eileen Hughes."
"She's just having tea with my wife upstairs."
That didn't interest Mr. Benson. "Look here," he said,

"She's tired, upset, and strangers frighten her.""Do you think that's the answer?"
"I'm sure of it. You see, she understands me. I asked her
to tea, and she came. The proof of the pudding is in the

with himself. A proverb was always a sign of being in con-
versational ascendancy.
"The point is," said Mr. Benson, "that Mrs. More tells

"I don't," said Mr. Benson. "Of course, it's all very

Mr. Strong crossed his legs; thus he was able to examine

"I was her husband's solicitor, her solicitor; I advised her

sounded to him as fine as an armistice day peroration.

"I was wondering myself," said Mr. Strong. "There

no motive then there isn't murder?"
"I suppose so," said Mr. Benson. "But not always. I had
once a case of two escaped Borstal boys. They were in high

her sewing in the window. The previous day they'd
pinched a revolver. They shot the woman and went on."
"How terrible," shuddered Mr. Strong.
"But that's the exception. Who were her enemies?"
"As far as I know she hadn't any."
"What about that Spaniard?"
"There's all sorts of gossip going around about him and

"She wore rather expensive underwear," said Mr. Benson.
Mr. Strong was disgusted. Policemen were the most

"Never mind. You must have drawn up her will." Mr.

"He once served a six months' sentence." Mr. Strong was

As his eyes swept scornfully past Arrowsmith's house,

He watched him getting into his Wolseley. He could re-

Mr. Benson had been walking about in the street, and
when he saw Eileen he followed her. She was still outside
the orbit of his searchlight but he had time on his hands.
While following her, he tried to think hard of her. Her
walk, the way she looked into a shop, how she held her
bag, and the rhythmic movement of the other hand. He
was a firm believer in his luck and was a hard worker. He
was tabulating her; tomorrow or the day after, when he

He waved to John, who pretended not to see him, and

to the estuary, and since the Red House was near and he

John was in a hurry, but by walking slowly he tried to
persuade himself that he wasn't. There were now only six
shillings left of the pound Skinner had given him. He
should have let Sheila pay for the cinema tickets the night
before. But he hadn't, and it was useless to cry over spilled
milk. When he had gone to his room a little before lunch,
he saw at once that Sheila had taken even his pajamas
away.
He wasn't angry. He owed her money, and people
were vicious and revengeful. She couldn't do much with
his shaving stick, but that was her lookout. She wasn't a
bad girl; probably if she calmed down in a day or two, she
would begin to be sorry that she had taken his only spare
shirt. He couldn't think with anger of Mr. Benson, either.
It was bad luck that Mr. Benson was on the job and knew
him intimately. He smiled because he could well picture
Mr. Benson giving Sheila a juicy description of his career.
He was passing a row of new houses. Somebody had told
him that his father had sold those building sites a few years
ago. He supposed that the money his father had received
for that land could buy him all the shirts and shaving sticks
in the world. Skinner, who had transacted the deal, must
have made a packet out of it. In the King's Head, where
John had a pint before lunch, the landlord said that Skinner
was at the moment one of the wealthiest men in the town.
He wondered what Skinner's home was like.
He had spent a restless forenoon trying to work outwhat he should do. If he telephoned Skinner and asked him
not to say anything about their meeting, it might make
Skinner suspicious and give him away to Mr. Benson.
Another attempt at blackmail. Mr. Benson would make
short work of him. But if he didn't get in touch with Skin-
ner, the man, without wishing to harm him, might tell it all
to the coppers. He didn't know what to do, and after lunch
he sat in the lounge answering Mrs. More's many questions.
He was by inclination a polite person, and while he sat
there trying to make up his mind, he said yes, his wife had
gone to London. Had she left because the murder upset
her? That was precisely the reason. Then Mrs. More talked

mained single units, meaningless. Then Mrs. Hailey came
to say
he was wanted on the telephone. It was getting on to

Perhaps Sheila from London to tell him in a repentant

ner asking John to come and see him, as he wanted to have
a talk. That was lucky.
The street lamps, for which Mr. Strong had fought too,

That made him smile. He stopped a man.
"Is this Memorial Avenue?"
The man said it was, and he found Skinner's house. It

John felt almost as if he were living at the Court. He be-

He could say he had mislaid his.

The twenty pound notes were all over the table. They
