As John turned the corner, he saw a police car. He
stopped. Mr. Benson and the odious Marshall went to ring
Skinner's bell. Thank God, he had nothing to worry about

It was full evening and the pubs were open. The first
thing to do was telephone Williams. He must be anxious
to know what had become of him. Facing the Crown was
a telephone box. The operator told him he should insert
two and fivepence. He had no change, so he said he would
be back presently and made for the Crown. At that time of
the evening the Crown was empty; it would fill after eight
o'clock.
The barmaid stood behind the bar, yawning. She could
yawn convincingly, but when she saw John it was the end
of her yawning. In the double life of her hopes and desires
there was the suburban home with a blaring radio that men
like Mr. Ribble should buy for her, and on the opposite
side were those gorgeous tall fellows who liked to borrow
a couple of quid off her just as she was beginning to enjoy
herself. Among those tall fellows John seemed the tallest.
"Good evening," he said. "A large Scotch, please."He had such a gentlemanly voice, but probably that girl
of his would be coming in any moment. Fundamentally,

"It's going to rain again," she said.
"Oh, yes," he smiled.
"You look tired," she said. If he'd let her, her heart

She would have bet her last quid that those hands could

"Gone to London."
"For the day?"
"No, she's gone for some time.”
That was better. He examined his change and drank up

American accent, but everything was phony about him.
“At last," said Williams. "I wanted to get in touch with
you since yesterday morning. Why the hell didn't you ring
me?"
"There was nothing to ring you about. I tried the bloke,
and first I failed, but now I got twenty quid out of him."
"Twenty quid." Williams laughed. "You're going to get
a few thousand, old boy."
"And how do you work that?"
"Stein, the lawyer, looked the whole thing up. You've
got a wonderful case. He says you needn't give him a
penny at the start. He'll take one third if he gets the
money; that's all he wants."John was not good at interrupting, so he waited till Wil-
liams had finished. "The trouble is," he said, "that the man
Skinner made me sign a document saying I had no further
claim when he gave me the twenty quid.”
"But you're mad. You're mad, man, you're mad."
"I know but there you are."
"Listen," shouted Williams, "I'm going down at once.
We'll get that back from him."
"Don't come down, this place is full of them. In the
hotel where I'm staying a woman was killed. One of our
old friends is here, so you keep away from it. Anyway, it's
useless." He had to insert more money.
"Now listen to me," said Williams, a trifle subdued,
"this is what you must do. Go and tell the bloke that your
lawyer says he got that signature under duress and you'll
take action. I bet that'll make him change his mind, but for
God's sake don't accept anything till you speak to me
again. Ring me tomorrow evening at this time."
"I must be careful," said John. Soon after that he rang
off.
The barmaid had been wrong: it wasn't going to rain for
the time being. The stars had appeared, and the sky was
cloudless. John walked slowly back to the Red House. He
wasn't unduly upset. He had twenty pounds, and Williams
had always been an optimist. Well, he wasn't, though
probably Stein was right. In a curious detached manner he
was almost prepared to congratulate Skinner for having so
perfectly got the better of him. But he knew his detach-
ment wouldn't last, and soon he would begin to think of
the
money he had lost; then he would do something silly.That was how his troubles always began. First he would
heed his own warning, then he would be off, with certain
disaster at the end. For the moment he was all right. He
was pleased because the moment was calm and innocent of
danger.
The breeze was southwesterly and mild. The lamp at the
corner shed enough light to reveal two cats, one ginger, the
other black, courting noisily; in the distance a trawler
hooted. A few children were outside the Red House.
Mrs. More was at her table: she looked impatient. Mr.
Ribble was just as impatient, whereas Mr. Haxton watched
his empty plate heavily, as though it would sooner or later
reveal some matter of importance.
John sat down. "I hope I am not late," he said, as he saw
no sign of food.
"Late, Mr. Denton?" said Mrs. More. "I don't think

Her entire life, so it seemed to her, was a large tear for
Peggy; but she liked her meals regularly, and even if she
had been willing to postpone eating, her stomach wouldn't

was more than she was prepared to bear. "It's eight five,"
she said.
"I'll ring again," said Mr. Ribble.
The bell was beside the service door. He rang it. "That's
not enough," said Mrs. More petulantly. "Keep your finger

"I hear somebody coming," said Mrs. More after a while.Mr. Ribble hurried back to his table. He didn't want
Hailey to know it was he who rang so energetically.
It wasn't Hailey but the cook. She was a short, wizened

in the hotel. Audrey made the breakfast, and she came to

"Mr. Hailey," she said loudly, "wants to know who

Mr. Ribble blushed and pretended to be busy with his

"We're waiting for our dinner," said Mrs. More. "It's

"But that's no reason to make that noise," said the cook.
"When is the dinner coming?" Mrs. More asked.
"Soon," said the cook, "but it won't come any quicker if

"Yes," said John.
"Then come here and help yourself. I don't want to

"We couldn't do it worse than the cook," said Mr. Hax-ton. But they were hungry, and with the exception of John
they all ate their

Mr. Haxton knew where he was: he had seen a detective

"He's always so punctual," said Mrs. More.
"I've a shrewd guess where he may be," said Mr. Ribble.
He giggled contentedly.
Hailey appeared. "Rissoles, mash, and cabbage," he said

"Don't any of you say that Mrs. Hailey and me don't

"Oh, Hailey," said Mr. Ribble, "where's Mr. Josey?"
"Search me," he said, and left them.
Mrs. More ate her three rissoles, and the world was aslightly pleasanter place. But where was Mr. Josey? Though
she cared little for Mr. Josey, his absence was intriguing; it
was easy to intrigue her.
"No sweets tonight," announced Hailey from the door.
"Cook ain't as well as she should be." He switched off the

"I'll have a talk with that nice Mr. Strong tomorrow,"

She sat down in the lounge, and John felt that he couldn't
cope with her questions a second time. He was sorry for

"No, it wasn't much of a dinner," he said. He had
stopped, and he shouldn't have.
"Come and sit down, Mr. Denton," said Mrs. More.
"Give a few of your minutes to a lonely old lady."
He sat down. Oh, she thought, he was one of the nice
ones. There were so few of them in the world. She would

John saw Peggy as a little girl, Peggy at school, even Peggy

"It was very kind of you," she said, "to listen to an oldwoman like me, Mr. Denton. I'm going up. So I'd better

"Not at all, good night."
She asked him again if he wasn't offended, and that gave
her another five minutes of his company; then she went.
John did not know what to do with himself. He stood
outside the lounge and hoped he would suddenly feel tired,
in which case he might go to bed. If he went out he would
spend money, and he must hold on hard to the twenty

but with no book on her lap. There was no ribbon in her

He wondered why he did it. Of course she was unaware
that he was in the room. Quietly he closed the door, and it
was a sad thought that if he had banged it, it wouldn't have
made any difference.
"Miss Hughes," he said, and smiled at the sound of his
own voice.
He walked past her, and she saw him. He nodded,
though he didn't know why. She nodded, too, but her nod

Should she or shouldn't she be afraid of him? He grinned,
but that wasn't reassuring enough, so he took her hand. It
was such a cold little hand; and light, he hadn't imagined
that a hand could be so light.
"And here you are," he said, "with your mother killedand nobody taking even the slightest notice of you."
She looked appealingly at him. When he had been a boy
and still lived at the Court, he'd had a Dandy, his own spe-
cial dog. Leonard had had a spaniel and Francis a spaniel.
Both spaniels had excelled at parlor tricks. They could sit
up and beg till one despised them for their humility; but
not so the Dandy, he knew no tricks. If he wanted a bis-
cuit, his eyes said so.
"I'm a fool," John said. "I'll try to speak so that you
should be able to understand me." He leaned forward and

Hughes, that I came to see you." He stopped and waited.
He could see she was straining every nerve and every cell

speaking too slowly?" he asked. She was concentrating
hard; it was painful to watch. "Or should I speak faster?”
he went on. "I'm such a fool." It was no good, she hadn't
understood a word. "Well, well," he said cheerfully, "I am
an idiot. We'll write to each other. I should have thought
of that before."
He looked round the room. There was a lugubrious oak
writing desk beside the fireplace. The desk was in meticu-
lous order: note paper in its appointed place, pencils lying
side by side, two pens in a small wooden box, each pen clean.
Before John had gone to prison, the police had come and
searched his lodgings in Bayswater. They had left his room
as tidy as the writing desk. He took a pencil and a sheet of
paper and with an encouraging smile sat down next to

The best thing was to start with a simple sentence.She picked up the paper with her light hands. She
scrutinized it, and downstairs the Haileys were having an

"So you want me to go on?" he said. He wrote. "I am sure

"So you don't even understand that," he said, and took
her hand which wasn't light any more. "I can't understand
it," he said. In her presence one could think in spoken
words, a curious sensation, but he was becoming accus-
tomed to it.
"I just don't understand it."
She had no handkerchief. He found her bag and gave
her one. "Oh, do find my hanky," a normal crying woman
would have said. "It's there in my bag."
"So you see, Eileen," he said brightly, "it'll be easy for
me to understand you. Yes, you, but there are other things
I don't understand."
She wiped her eyes, took the pencil, and began to write.
It was his turn to watch. Curious shapes, which surely had
a meaning because some of them recurred, filled the sheet.
Like an infant trying to draw, and one pretends it is a
wonderful drawing; but her shapes were even more simple.
She pushed the sheet in front of him, and he tried hard and
nodded so that she should think he understood what shewished to impart to him. He nodded with much encour-
agement, but she took the sheet and tore it to bits. She was
crying again.
"Never mind," he said, "we'll understand each other.
You'll see."
He fetched another sheet of paper and started to draw
for her. He was comparatively good at drawing. He drew

them sadly. She wasn't happy, and he said, "I'm trying to

concentrate on her problem, he had stopped looking at her.

John let go of Eileen's hand. A little frightened, she turned

"Eileen," he said in a voice which was like a low shout,

and why I do that, and perhaps why I live." He noticedJohn. "This
poor girl," he said to him, pleased that he was

nothing, and then in the end they say 'Thank you, sir,'
'Good night, sir,' 'Sorry to bothered you, sir.'" Mr. Ben-

"That's the way they do it," said John. "I wouldn't take
it too much to heart."
"Heart? What about not eating my dinner? Hailey now
is very drunk, and nothing to eat. Now, who comes in

It was Audrey, and she waved to Eileen, who obediently
followed her.
"Where is she going?" John asked.
"To bed," said Audrey. "I always takes her to bed."
"Now," said Mr. Josey when they were alone, "what
you think of all that? An outrage. Because I am poor, be-
cause I am an artist, that is no excuse."
"They questioned me the same way. It's their job to
suspect everybody. We were all living here when Mrs.
Hughes was killed."
"I know, but the time they take. You have nothing to

"I'm afraid I haven't."
"A cigarette?" His case was full, but Mr. Josey sponged
on principle.
John gave him a cigarette. He sat down and puffed hard,
seemingly less outraged."You have," said John, “known Mrs. Hughes a long time?"
"Now don't you ask questions, you're no police. They

"My best friend in the world. I tell them that."
"You see," lied John, "I remembered her when I was a

"You lived here?"
"In this town."
"I come to this town many years ago. First time, you

"And Eileen?"
"Yes, Eileen too. I know her when she was little girl.”
"Mrs. Hughes must have been a very good mother to the

"But Eileen was taught all the things one can teach a

"Yes, yes.
I remember when Mr. Hughes wasn't dead
there was a teacher to teach her. He says expensive teacher,

"Who was the teacher?"
"I can't say. A woman, yes, I remember was a woman.

"I'm going, too."
On the stairs Mr. Josey asked, "Why you ask those

"I am a very curious person."
"I am not," said Mr. Josey, which was true, and he went

eating, he wiped his fingers but not his chin. He sat down

unpleasant aspect to his grief. If she was truly up there,
then she must know he had bilked their tryst; but he didn't
give that much thought. She was killed that he should be

dinner as for his lost friend. A sharp knock came on the door.
"It is nearly eleven, Mr. Josey," said Mrs. More's voice.
"I should like to sleep, please."
It wasn't his lucky day.chapter six
Mr. Haxton was awake at
seven. That was his usual waking
time. Up at seven, down to the
banked boiler to put on some
coal, a tepid bath, followed by a
breakfast he cooked for himself
when they hadn't a maid, which
happened often. In the large bed-
room his wife would lie half asleep and listening, so he im-
agined, to his fumbling in the kitchen. At eight twenty he
would get out the Hillman, take his place in the suburban
car queue, and drive to his office. Once he'd had a puncture
about fifty yards from his house. It took him twenty min-
utes to change the tire. His hands got dirty, so he went
back to wash them. His wife had just arisen and stood in
slippers and an old flannel dressing gown in the hall.
"What are you doing here?" she asked.
He told her that he'd had a puncture and showed her his
hands. He was also perspiring. She was pleased, for she liked
to see him discomfited; she hated him to have any com-
fort. Often he asked himself how it was possible that she
not only had left him but had also stopped being interested
in his lack of comfort. Probably she was so conceited that
she thought life would be just hardship without her. If
anybody praised him, she became pale with rage. A little
self-praise pops out at times, but with him it was strictly
prohibited; and yet he had loved her many years ago. Mr.
Haxton felt sad in the early morning light because it had
all ended in mutual hatred with not even a spark of longing
left in him for her. She had aged curiously: she became alarge woman but it was a matter of bones, as though her
bones had filled out her extending skin.
"Come in," he said. It wouldn't be Mr. Ribble; he never
rose before eight.
"Good morning," said John. "Sorry to trouble you, but
I can't find my shaving kit. Do you think it would be
frightfully rude of me to ask for the loan of your razor,
shaving stick, and shaving brush? I promise I'll clean it all
very thoroughly."
Mr. Haxton had taken a liking to him. Young, which he
wasn't, well bred, which he wasn't, thin and good looking,
which he wasn't, were the reasons he liked John. Perhaps
if he'd had John's advantages, there wouldn't have been a
Mrs. Haxton for him. John was in shirt and trousers.
"Of course you can have them," he said. "The only
trouble is that I use an old-fashioned cutthroat. Do you
know how to use one?"
"I'll try, I've used one before."
In a fine leather case were Mr. Haxton's razors; they
were three and kept beautifully clean. He chose one which
he gave to John.
"I won't be long," said John.
Mr. Haxton put a second pillow behind his head and
tried not to think of his wife. Where would he go and
what would he do once the police let them scatter? It was
actually unimportant. He had put aside enough to keep
him in simple comfort, but thanks to his wife he wasn't in
need of any comfort. If he went, he wouldn't see Eileen
again. The poor girl; what would become of her? If he hadguts, he would go to Mr. Strong and ask him to let Eileen
go with him, he would look after her. Perhaps in that case
they might get down to real written conversation and none
of his imaginary writings. His wife would hoot with laugh-
ter. Her stupid, fat husband living with a deaf and dumb
girl. It would be her standing joke till it pleased God to
take her away. He blushed, for he could hear her laughing.
She had a cold, impersonal laugh, as though she had a ma-
chine beside her which, when she pressed a button, gave
out a mechanical laugh. That machine would be hard at
work if he helped Eileen.
"Here are your things, sir," said John. "Thank you very
much."
"Good Lord, you did cut yourself.".
"I'm afraid I did." He had cut himself across his cheeks,
across his chin, and one ear lobe was bleeding, too. He
should, thought John, have his photograph taken and send

see me, he'd say I got it killing Mrs. Hughes."
"If you don't mind my saying so," said Mr. Haxton, “I
saw you yesterday morning standing with one of those
detectives, and I was really frightened on your behalf."
"That's very kind of you. But you know what the police
are like, and between you and me I wasn't always a good

"I am sorry to hear that," said Mr. Haxton gravely.
"At times even I am sorry. I'm sure you won't mention
that to the other people here."
"I can promise you that."
"You see, I'm rather a weak person and...." He thoughthard for a moment. "I don't want to speak of myself. But
do tell me about Eileen Hughes. She interests me."
"I know very little about her, but I am terribly sorry for
her."
"You shouldn't be so sorry," said John, sitting down.
"Perhaps she is happy in that silent world of hers. Ours is
so noisy. Perhaps she has built herself up something that we
don't understand, and if she could hear us and speak to us,
it would all be shattered."
"I don't agree with you," said Mr. Haxton. "I've been
watching her for a longish time. She isn't happy. I think
she's utterly lonely. Like being out alone in a dark night."
His fat cheeks turned red. He was talking like some stupid
poet. How his old cronies up North would laugh if
they heard him speak like that. But John didn't laugh;
probably that was why he had spoken as he had.
"I think I agree with you," said John. "Do you remem-
ber the lines of Tennyson: 'An infant crying in the night'?”
Mr. Haxton, who had left school at the age of fourteen,
regretfully admitted that he didn't know those lines of
Tennyson.
"That line has been haunting me ever since I came here,"
said John.
"An infant crying in the night,
An infant crying for the light,
With no language but a cry.'"
"Very beautiful," said Mr. Haxton. He repeated," "With
no language but a cry.' Come in.”Mrs. Hailey was green in the face, and dark lines made
her eyes look pink. She smiled as you smile when you
know you won't live for long. “I thought,” she said re-
proachfully, "that Mr. Denton 'ad 'opped it. I see nothing
in your room. No pajamas, no nothing."
"It's all in the wardrobe," said John haltingly. "I'm a
very tidy person."
"But your pajamas,” she said.
"All in the wardrobe," said John.
"Shall I leave your tea 'ere?" she asked.
"No, I'll have it in my room. Thank you again," he said

ask questions; the questions truthfully answered, he would

and get himself a shirt and shaving tackle. Absentmindedly

The twenty pounds were like muscles to him. There was
his life, the future and decisions waited for him, but for the
time being the twenty pounds sufficed. That money was
the wall between himself and thought. He tried to whistle
while he put on his tie. Soon he stopped whistling because
Mr. Josey had begun to play. He played execrably loud as
if the fortissimi were a challenge to the ordeal he had gone
through the night before.
Hailey was still in bed. His trouble was that he liked a
little drink here and there. Gently he admonished himself
and turned on the other side to have a quick sleep before

kitchen. Previously it had been the servants' hall. His wifeand he had made the room look sordid, untidy, and their
whole London-basements background was there with them.
Outside the wind was blowing from the sea, the cloudless
sky canopied the green fields, but in the Haileys' room it
was a home from home. There was also the stale smell of

He bought himself two shirts, socks, and shaving gear.
With the parcel under his arm he thought he would have a

John looked up and down the road: there was nobody
about. He got in beside Williams.
Williams had a decent hat, which to all purposes was the
only decent thing about him. He was short, muscular, and
had a low forehead and thick hair. He spent most of his free
time trying to brush his hair back, to give loftiness to his
forehead. It wasn't successful. He was proud of himself. In
his own eyes he was quick, cunning, and fearless. For sometime he had been overshadowed by his brother, but since his
brother had received a life sentence, he could give freer rein
to his ambitions and many conceits. The finest achievement
in life was to be a great crook. If he could have chosen be-
tween a kingdom or the late Al Capone's gun, he would un-
hesitatingly have chosen the gun. His brother's fate hadn't
upset him. He was convinced he would have been cleverer
in his brother's place. He regretted that opportunities for
crime weren't more frequent. He was a hard worker, and if
a chance for dirty work came along, he grabbed it at once.
He hadn't yet killed anybody, but he was convinced that if
the occasion arose he would rise to it. He'd had two small
sentences and was consequently jealous of his brother. He
was in one respect no fool. He knew when he met a weak
person.
When John had come out of prison, he went to see him.
They hadn't known each other well because John used to
be his brother's friend. Nevertheless, he felt at once that John
might become an asset; now he was certain he was on a good
thing.

They parted, and John walked back to the Red House. It
was early for lunch, and because he didn't know what else
to do, he sat down in the lounge near the door. The lounge
was empty. Mrs. More was at the lending library, and Mr.
Josey was playing "Rose Marie" in his room. Silence was
complete. With a good deal of horror he was thinking ofhis next interview with Skinner. Of course he wouldn't be
forceful; he knew himself too well for that. But he would
keep his promise to Williams, then nobody could bother him
any more with Skinner.
The front door opened, and, like the other residents of the
Red House, he listened to the footsteps coming up the stairs.
They were light and quick, so it had to be Eileen.
As they met on the landing, the light of the large window
facing the lounge was on him. The scars Mr. Haxton's razor
had made were picked out by the light. "Nice to see you,
Eileen," he said quietly.
Her eyes were on the cuts; she came toward him, and her
fingers touched each scar. There was sheer horror in her eyes.
It almost frightened John. He smiled and shook his head,
trying to make her understand that they were light cuts
which he had completely forgotten. But the look of horror
did not leave her. At any moment, he thought, she would
faint. He took her hand and gently pushed it away. The fin-
gers returned. He took her hand again, but she wrenched it
free. She was strong. So he let her touch his face. After a
while the fingers, apparently reassured, left the cuts. Then
her hand took his.
"It's perfectly all right," he said. Now she seemed to un-
derstand. He patted her hand, and she went to her room.
Poor girl, he thought. She would be sitting there alone
for the rest of the day. As the lounge was still empty, he
went to her door and looked in. She was sitting, as usual,
upright in her chair with her back to him. She sat like a
statue and for a moment he thought she couldn't be alive.
He waited, but she didn't move. If he waited there till theend of the world, she still wouldn't move. He closed the
door. He sat in the lounge quietly, his mind working so fast
that after a while he noticed he had been sitting as immobile
as Eileen sat in her room.
Mrs. More had run into Mr. Ribble outside the library. Mr.
Ribble had wanted to go to the Crown, though a morning
session wasn't so exciting as the evening drinks because the
barmaid was mostly in the background and the manager en-
tertained his special friends; also his brother-in-law might

Mr. Haxton had been for a walk along the estuary. He
had watched the gulls and seen a few curlews; and once
again he regretted that hard work, the effort to rise from
obscurity, had killed even the desire to appreciate nature.
"He looks such a sad man," whispered Mrs. More to her
companion. Mr. Ribble didn't bother to answer.
The local auctioneer's assistant lunched at the Red House

alive and at any moment would tick off the Haileys for
being late with the soup.

The whole Eileen business was getting on his nerves. He
shouldn't have let himself in for it. Also, there was some-
thing else that worried him. Had Mrs. Hughes done the right
thing? She had been so plausible, and it was flattering, up
to a point, to be taken into the confidence of a woman who
was so aloof. Mrs. Hughes was not old, and if it had only oc-
curred to him that she might be killed in her prime-though
he couldn't very well blame himself for not having thought

she did and, in the event of her death, she should leave more

dence. He tried to think of the name of that woman. Now

his tobacco pouch, looked carefully at its contents, and filled
his pipe; by the time he lighted it, he felt better, and so de-
cided to think of Eileen at a later opportunity.

Mr. Strong wasn't averse to talk. They settled down to it

Two women were mounting the stairs, both dressed in
their Sunday best. They were short, insignificant women and
wore identical black hats. They stopped outside Mrs. Strong's
door, and the thinner of the two rang the bell. Mrs. Strong
must have been waiting for them because the door opened
at once. "How very punctual you are, Mrs. Ray," said Mrs.
Strong. "And so this is your sister?"
"Yes, Mrs. Strong," said Mrs. Ray, who was the thinner
of the two.
Mrs. Strong gave the sister the same smile she was in the
habit of giving to Eileen, for Mrs. Ray's sister was deaf and
dumb.
The idea, or, as she would have called it, the inspiration,
had come to Mrs. Strong the afternoon before. Mrs. Ray
was their grocer's wife, and when Mrs. Strong visited the
shop, Mrs. Ray often boasted of her deaf and dumb sister
who was so good at sign language that you could talk toher almost as easily as to a normal being. The sister lived
with the Rays. Mr. Ray had a different opinion on the sub-
ject, but he would not vent it if his wife were within ear-
shot, and she mostly was.
The idea was a simple one. Since Mrs. Strong could make
no headway with Eileen, poor Eileen on the other hand
needed some sort of company; surely Mrs. Ray's sister was
the best company one could wish for her. "I hope," she said,
"they'll get on well."
"It won't be Joy's fault if they don't," said Mrs. Ray. She
winked at Joy. Joy was about fifty years old, had a small
mustache, was composed and perfectly sure of herself. She
winked back at her sister, a superior wink.
"There is Eileen," said Mrs. Strong. There was a little
room next to the living room which was seldom used. She
had got that room ready for the momentous meeting by put-
ting near the door two chairs and a small table, since the little
room was unfurnished. Her reason for putting the chairs
near the door was that by leaving the door ajar she could
watch Joy and Eileen.
Like a fully rigged schooner, Joy, after her sister gave her
the sign, advanced on Eileen. Her sister signaled to her to
sit down.
She sat down, and Mrs. Ray said to Mrs. Strong, “You
see, Mrs. Strong? You can talk to her as you can talk
to me."
"Sit down, Mrs. Ray," said Mrs. Strong, sitting down.
They were facing the door.
Joy sat leaning eagerly yet proudly forward. Eileen stood
before her. Joy was fat, fat in the face and fat in the body.
Eileen's slimness made her seem coarse and slightly obscene.There was deep satisfaction in Joy's double chin. Eileen was
afraid, though nobody bothered to notice that, especially
not Joy, who used sign language volubly. She nodded hilari-
ously as her pudgy hands imparted one item after the other.
Eileen watched her, and once or twice raised a hand which
remained hesitatingly in mid-air, then fell dejectedly to her
side.
The daily woman brought in the tea. Mrs. Strong and
Mrs. Ray had tea in the sitting room; Joy and Eileen had
theirs on the other side of the open door. "Joy has a hearty
appetite," said Mrs. Ray.
"I'm glad to hear that," said Mrs. Strong.
"Look how she talks," said the proud sister. Fingers
spread with jam were talking quickly. Joy stopped while she
ate a large slice of cake, but immediately afterwards she re-
turned to the attack, which was now easier, as Eileen had
sat down and Joy's fingers often touched her chin. Eileen's
head was bowed, and she was looking at her cup. Mrs.
Strong began to regret her impulse. It was an ordeal to her:
two deaf and dumbs in her well-regulated house. But she
was doing her duty by the poor girl; she hoped Eileen was
enjoying herself. Mrs. Ray was talking eighteen to the dozen.
"I hate," said Mrs. Strong at last, "to break up our little
party, but my
husband will take Eileen home in a few min-
utes' time."
Joy was loath to go, but Mrs. Ray signaled to her that
high tea was waiting for her at home. That made Joy jump
up. Her hands spoke quickly to Mrs. Ray.
"What does she say?" asked Mrs. Strong. "Did she enjoy
herself? How did they get on?""My sister says," said Mrs. Ray, "that the young lady
didn't understand her. My sister is puzzled by her."
"I see," said Mrs. Strong. "Thank you very much for
coming."
Joy shook her hand, and the sisters departed. Eileen was
still in the little room, standing behind her chair. Mrs. Strong
went downstairs to the office.
"I had," she said to her husband, “a deaf and dumb woman
here to talk to Eileen. Sign language, you know. Well, Eileen
doesn't know sign language, either. You landed yourself in
a big mess by becoming her guardian. Now, how are we
going to make that girl understand anything? She's worse
than a dog."
"Please don't shout," said Mr. Strong amiably.
"Frank, I really don't understand you."
For Mr.Strong looked pleased with himself; he was pleased
with himself, and had good reason to be. As his conversa-
tion with Mrs. More had meandered along, purposeless and
lazy, it had suddenly reached an unexpected, deep lagoon:
much to his surprise, he had offered her the managership of
the Red House just after she mentioned that years ago a
sister of hers had kept a boardinghouse in Eastbourne. First
Mrs. More was surprised by the offer and was going to turn
it down. She had enough money for her needs, she didn't
care much for going up and downstairs too often, which,
as she remembered, her sister did a lot. But it occurred to
her that she would have something to do, and then her ex-
pression became grim because she thought of the Haileys.
If she accepted the job, they would be in her power.
"I accept," she said, and they decided that she would startnext Monday. Terms were easily arrived at and settled.
That was why Mr. Strong was pleased with himself. In a
few days he would tell Mrs. More everything about Eileen.
Mrs. More would look after her, and he felt that soon it
would all work well again, as it had done while Mrs. Hughes
was still of the living. "Don't you worry," he said to his
wife, "Eileen is all right. You won't have to worry about
her. Now I'd better take her home."
"You take things very easily, Frank."
"No, I don't. By the way, I've found a manageress for the
Red House. A very competent woman. You see, I don't waste
my time."
"Who is she?"
"I'll tell you when I come back."
The brown boots ran him upstairs. Eileen was still behind
the chair. He tapped her shoulder and she understood, which
saved him from having to write one of those curious signs
for her. She followed him down the stairs.
"You'll soon be in clover," he said, smiling at Eileen. “A
nice lady will look after you." They had reached the en-

words, which she couldn't hear. Mr. Haxton was just pass-
ing the house.
"Hullo, Mr. Haxton," said Mr. Strong. "Out for a stroll?"
"I went for a walk," said Mr. Haxton, smiling at Eileen.
So young and fresh she looked. On the other side of the
street in Burton's shopwindow he could see the outlines of
his own heavy figure.
"Are you going, by any chance, to the Red House?" asked
Mr. Strong, who never lost an opportunity to save himself
from work of any kind. Mr. Haxton said he was, and Mr.Strong suggested that he should see Eileen back. Mr. Hax-

smiled at her, took her by the arm; then as they started out
he blushed slightly and let go of her arm, but she remained
sadly and beautifully near him. There was little traffic in
High Street, Friday being early closing day.
If he had a pencil, he said to himself, he might there in the
street start writing to her. First he would tell her that she
would get over the loss of her mother. Time made you get
over everything. But was that, strictly speaking, true? He
stopped, and Eileen stopped too. "I'm sorry," he said, and
on they went. A half-empty bus turned around in the small
square that cut High Street in two.
Everything, dear Eileen, had an end, and that included
sorrow; though the sad thing was that in his case the end
had somehow been endless. There had simply been nothing
to it. It was like a lily pond without water or lilies and the
cement cracked at the bottom. His wife's mother lived in
Birmingham, and she used to go and stay with her twice a
year. An evening came, shortly after one visit, when he
found her packing hard.
"Off to your mother again?" he asked.
"Tomorrow," she said.
She was in a bad mood, so he left it at that. He undressed
in his little room, and went to bed in his narrow bed with-
out wishing to be in the large one on the other side of the wall.
Their house had been built by a speculative builder. The thin
walls, which let every sound through, were almost useless.
"This, Eileen," smiled Mr. Haxton, taking good care not
to stop, "would be a long, long letter."
His wife was in her bedroom moving about a lot. Herfootsteps were the heavy footsteps of a bony, middle-aged
woman. Only her hair had remained young, and glancing
at Eileen's hair, he could have shed a tear for the young hair
of that bony woman.
"Fred," his wife called across the wall, "are you asleep?"
"No," he said; and she carried on a long conversation
through the wall. She was leaving in the morning; no, she
wouldn't come back. She was leaving for good. No, he
needn't bother to drive her to the station, she had ordered a
taxi. If he wanted to get in touch with her, he knew where
to find her. A divorce? At their age there was no need for
a divorce. The laundry would be back on Saturday, but he
needn't bother to pick out her things. The charwoman would
see to that and send them on to her.
"Eileen," said Mr. Haxton, "you'd be tired if you had to
read all this." They had reached the Common.
Well, said his wife, that was nice of him to offer her
money. She would have all she needed with mother, but if
he sent her a fiver a week that would be decent, indeed. Oh
yes, she had told the neighbors that her mother wasn't well,
and she was going to look after her. That was the best thing
to say. After all, her mother was seventy-eight. Was there
anything else? They both thought hard on their respective
sides of the wall, and after thirty years of marriage they
had to agree there was nothing else.
He made up his mind that he would wake in time to see
her to the taxi. The alarm clock woke him punctually, but
for some obscure reason he lingered in bed, listening to the
driver carrying the suitcases downstairs. If he put on his
dressing gown, he could catch her in the hall. But he waitedand did not put on his dressing gown until he heard the
driver starting the engine. He hurried to the window and
looked out.
"A boring letter, Eileen," he said, and that moment for
some reason or other her face was turned in his direction.
He was moved because there was so much friendship in her
eyes. "You can speak better with your eyes," he said, "than
I can write a letter."
The taxi in which his wife sat had left the house. The sub-
urban houses were still asleep. There was sunshine in the
street, and the milkman would soon be around. A little girl
came out from one of the front gardens, a maid ran after her
and dragged her back. The little girl shrieked. Further on,
where Acacia Road ceased and Primrose Road began, two
dogs were playing. One was a cairn and the other a yellow
Labrador. The taxi was crawling along. Through the back
window he could see his wife's shoulders, square and com-
posed. She was wearing a brown coat. The taxi had to swerve
at the corner of Primrose Road on account of the dogs.
And that, dear Eileen, was how they parted. He had come
to live at the Red House because he had always wanted to
end his days near the sea; now he was near the sea but sel-
dom looked at it. Many years ago his wife and he had spent
a fortnight's holiday beside the sea. They had been young
at that time. Perhaps at his age he shouldn't have returned,
but this was the end of the letter, for there before them was
the Red House.
"Thank you, Mr. Haxton, for your letter," wrote Eileen.
Mrs. Hailey was standing in the door. "A little fresh air,"
she said, "does you good."chapter eight

It seemed to John that the best thing would be not to go

The evening was mild, and the town seemed dark because
of the closed shops. It would be marvelous, though, if he
could force Skinner to pay up a reasonable sum. What would
he do with a thousand pounds? His instinctive answer was
that he should start a new life. That sounded fine. But how
did one start a new life? If one started a new life, one surely
began by going to some distant place; but he knew of no
distant place. And what would he do in that distant place
where his past couldn't reach him? He had not been trained
for a job. The simplest way out would be to put the entire
thousand pounds on a good horse, and, if the horse won, he
could live in ease for the rest of his life. However, he was
unlucky with horses, dogs, and football pools. After all, he
said to himself with a wry smile, it didn't matter a damn
whether Skinner gave him a thousand pounds or not; and
that, he admitted, wasn't the right spirit in which to ap-
proach Skinner.
As he got nearer to Skinner's house, he began to like theidea of seeing Skinner even less. A girl was taking a dog for
a walk, and he stopped and watched the dog, thus gaining a
little time. The girl and the dog disappeared in the direction
of High Street, so he had to go on. He passed a dark build-
ing with the sounds of an organ and singing coming toward

was also a gain in time, he went in. Benediction; he sat down
in a pew and saw that the congregation was kneeling. Per-
haps he should kneel, too. He knelt and stared hard at the
candles and at the priest's gold and white vestment, and there
was something heavy and sad within him. He should, it
seemed to him, have become a Catholic monk. They must
have peace, those monks. And, good God, what was he? A
cowardly, weak man who had drifted into the company of
crooks without being able to become one of them. Probably
if he were a monk, the other monks would despise him as
Williams and his other pals did. Please God, he prayed,
couldn't you undo the whole thing and let me be drowned
and not my brothers?
The priest and the acolytes had left, so he had better go
and have it out with Skinner. Since he was in a church, it
might be respectable to send up another prayer. Please God,
he prayed, give me one chance, one great wonderful chance.
He didn't quite know what sort of chance he wanted.

The man who was walking with John was no accomplice:
it was Detective Sergeant Marshall. Though short and un-

burning desire to become a great detective. The future for

Marshall, as easily as the flick of a finger, found the crim-

For it was a sad thing to be so short, to have an insignificant
face and many pimples. In busses, trains, and dance halls,

When he arrived at the Red House, there seemed to be

"Oh, I see."

They went up to the top floor, which had a mean corri-
dor with two mean rooms on each side of it. In one of
those rooms lived, or rather slept, Audrey. The whole
floor smelled of poverty and of the distant slum from
which Audrey hailed. Mrs. Hailey opened a door and
switched on a solitary bulb. The floor was littered with
rubbish, and the police search of the day before yesterday
had not improved matters. There were a pair of rusting
skates, a broken fire iron, old papers, and about a dozen
alphabets. The alphabets gave the impression that they
must have been thrown pell-mell into the fire but hadn't
burned completely, then were pulled out of the fire be-
cause the attempt had been unsuccessful, and finally were
dumped by some maid like Audrey, who did not know
quite what to do with them, into the room nobody used.
It seemed simple enough to John. But why had they been
thrown on a fire? Whoever did that must have been in a
bad temper.
"Why," he asked, "did Mrs. Hughes allow this rubbish
to be left here?" There were a couple of broken toys on
the floor. He picked one up, a doll. That must have been
Eileen's doll, and, much to his surprise, he abruptly felt
that he could sit down with the doll and cry.
"She never came up 'ere," said Mrs. Hailey. "You know
she was a funny woman. You think she ran this place?
Nothing of the sort. She just sat in that sitting room, hav-ing Eileen with her the whole day, just letting the girl go
out for a walk when she wanted to go up to Mr. Josey. She
carried on with him, no doubt of that."
John wasn't interested in Mrs. Hughes' love life. "So she
probably never came up here?"
"Never since I was 'ere. I tell you she just sat, and if
somebody complained, then she jawed our 'eads off. Hailey
and I was a bit afraid of her. Now there's the front door-
bell. That must be the two gents for the night. Switch the
light off when you go down."
She ran down the stairs and, still holding the doll, John
stood there for a while. He was experiencing a curious
sensation. All sorts of shapeless thoughts were chasing each
other in his mind. Some seemed familiar, others were
frightening owing to their novelty. He could stop none of
them. It was like a merry-go-round that had got out of
control; but sometime it was bound to stop, and then he
could check up on those thoughts. A few pictures were
leaning face against the wall. He didn't bother to look at
them. Every one of them was framed, and dust covered
them all. He picked up one of the books. There were few
pages left in it. The book said nothing to him: just an alpha-
bet, and it was the same with the others. He switched off
the light and closed the door. Would, he wondered, Mrs.
More have that room cleaned out on Monday?

Mr. Haxton was in the lounge. He smiled at John, but
his smile was preoccupied; in his hand he held an open
telegram, and his hand seemed to tremble lightly, which
was rather curious for such a fat, beefy hand. He looked at
the telegram again.
"Mother died today burial on Tuesday." It was signed
Martha.
Considering her age and years of ailing, it wasn't surpris-

send him the telegram? And why-that being his chief
preoccupation-did she mention the day of the funeral?
Surely she wasn't expecting him to go to the funeral. He

house was always cold; with the dead old woman in it, it
must be even colder. His wife would be wearing black,
now and again she would raise to her nose a small blot of
a handkerchief and gently blow her nose. She wasn't much
of a hand at crying, but whenever emotion overtook her,
she blew her nose at regular intervals; and amid the nose-
blowing she would give him a reproachful look, probably
because he had forgotten to send a wreath or something
like that.
"Good evening, Mr. Haxton," said Mrs. More, making
her entry into the lounge. Ever since she had accepted the
job, her deportment had changed. In the first hour or so,
slowly, then with speed. She had already told Mr. Ribble
in confidence that she would be taking over on Monday.
Perhaps if she had Mr. Haxton alone for a moment she
might, provided he promised not to repeat it, tell him about

with the broken marble slab under the cracked gilt mirror.
She touched the marble, then looked at her hand which
was now as dusty as the marble. She muttered a few angry
words to herself, then sat down. Mr. Haxton was still
standing. Upstairs, Mr. Josey was playing the piano. The
gong sounded.The Haileys, having no ready cash till they would be
paid next day, were conspicuously sober. They served the

tively hot.
Before dinner, Mr. Ribble had been to the Stag and had
tossed down a few large whiskies in order to acquire Dutch
courage. The cold and loneliness of his bed and life had
been haunting him the whole day. Before tea he had run
into his sister in High Street and listened to her for a long
quarter hour. Mrs. Strong was also worried about her
brother's lonely life, though she looked at it from an alto-
gether different angle.
To begin with, she was still peevish about his not having
accepted the hospitality of Bank Buildings when he retired
to the town of his birth. Second, she had never liked his
celibacy, which lately irked her a good deal; for a friend
of hers, a Mrs. Brown, had lost her husband. She was a tall,
angular widow but had considerable property. She toler-
ated nonsense from nobody, which included herself. What
with that and the property, it seemed to Mrs. Strong that
she would make an ideal wife for her brother. Several times
she had tried to hint to him that if he made a little effort
he could make conquest of a fine widow. She hinted again
that afternoon, and Mr. Ribble remained as unresponsive
as ever.
"I should like you," she said, "to come round for a cup
of tea tonight after supper. Jane Brown is coming."
"I've got another engagement tonight."
"Boozing at the Crown?"
Mr. Ribble giggled and remained adamant. But his lone-liness was terrible. The world was thickly populated with
happy mating couples, but his bed was cold and empty;
and there was little time left: soon he would be sixty. The
barmaid at the Crown was ideally fitted to fill his bed with
the warmth of her soft, fat body. Tonight he would speak
to her. He would ask her out for Sunday and let Sunday
look after the rest of it, provided it didn't rain on Sunday.
Four large whiskies were sufficient for him to feel con-
fident that she would accept and the sun would shine on
Sunday. He would take her to that fine old park near
Marshdon Manor. There were benches, and the trees were
plentiful.
"Going to the Crown?" asked Mr. Haxton after dinner.
"I don't know," said Mr. Ribble. He wanted to be alone
with the barmaid.
"I'm going anyhow," said Mr. Haxton, so there was
nothing else left for Mr. Ribble but to go with him.
He walked fast because of the fever within him. Mr.
Haxton would rather have walked slowly, owing to his
need for careful thought. After a few minutes there was a
good distance between them.
Dear Eileen, what was he to do? His wife could hurt
him and squash him so easily. Dear Eileen, how wonderful
her silence was. Oh, if only his wife were as quiet. You
couldn't even think in her presence.
As it seemed to him better to talk even to Mr. Ribble

"Very old. Around eighty."
"One can't grumble at that age."

"Well, write to her and tell her you can't go."
"It's not so easy."
They would soon reach the Crown, and with the hope
that his loneliness would soon leave him for good, Mr.
Ribble decided to give wholesome advice. "If you take my
advice," he said, "then go to the funeral, be civil to your
wife, and make it up with her. Anything is better than liv-
ing the way we do. You take my advice. Here we are.”
He hurried into the bar. Friday night was usually slack
at the Crown. A few of the local tradespeople stood about
with their wives, and the atmosphere was heavy with bore-
dom and the anticipation of Saturday.

handsome young man as if she weren't afflicted? If it were
true, all the faith that had made him work so hard in his
youth would return. It would be a wonderful victory, and
somehow he would be included in it.
John, as far as he knew, was married; but in his elation
he tried to forget that. Lavish, warm pictures flashed
through his mind. John and Eileen would marry, they
would have sweet children with the looks of Eileen and the
voice of John. In his room he sat down and wrote his wifea cold, formal letter telling her that much to his regret he
couldn't go to her mother's funeral. Charity, he thought
as he signed the letter, apparently didn't begin at home.

and John down; nevertheless, he took the letter to the box

wanted to try again on Monday, but tomorrow he would
persuade Williams to abstain from another go. From bitter
experience he knew what it was like to sit and wait for the
outcome of other people's efforts. In the distance he heard
a train. The engine whistled; in two hours the train wouldbe in London. Tomorrow, or at the latest on Monday, he
should be gone, too.
"Miss Eileen is at the window," said Hailey.
John waved to her. She didn't wave back: probably she
had never been taught to wave, and probably nobody had
ever waved to her before. The engine whistled again. John
didn't hear it. He went up to the sitting room, and she was
waiting for him at the door. So, having seen him come in,
she had expected him to go to her. She took his hand
and sat down. There was a chair which she had put near
hers. He sat down.
For a while he said nothing. He played with her hand,
taking each finger in turn, looking hard at them, as though
he were expecting them to answer a question he hadn't yet
formulated. Then he raised his head, and he couldn't help
saying, "You are beautiful, Eileen. But surely you know
that." He stood up, and she stood up, too. Holding her
hand, he took her to the mirror above the writing desk.
"Look how beautiful you are," he said. He pushed her
slightly forward and remained behind her. She looked into
the mirror, but instead of looking at herself, she was look-
ing at him. "Quite hopeless," he said.
He heard the rattling of plates and took a step backward.
The door opened; it was Audrey with Eileen's lunch.
Audrey stared at him with her empty eyes. Eileen me-
chanically went to the table and sat down. John left the
room.
Eileen's mechanical obedience to the outside world he
had noticed before. Her movements, when she was in
touch with other people, were like those of a performinganimal, void of pleasure and seeing no purpose. It made him
angry. Though he might leave in a few days, with him at
least she was her true self; and he felt a curious sense of
despair because she didn't deserve to have him
go.
"Lunch, I see,” said Mrs. More, "is again ten minutes
late."
Hailey didn't bother to answer.
"Mr. Josey," called Mrs. More from one end of the
dining room to the other, "I wonder if you could do us a
small favor."
"What?" asked Mr. Josey, without looking up from his
plate.
"Have you ever thought, Mr. Josey, that there might be
guests here who'd like to have a little rest in the afternoon?
I mean, Mr. Josey, that though we all enjoy your playing,
perhaps if
you didn't play in the afternoon before half-
past three, it might be a very good idea, in case somebody
would like a rest."
"When I came here," said Mr. Josey, "Mrs. Hughes, she
tells me I can play until ten in the evening." He went on

"Oh, I see," said Mrs. More. That was the moment to
tell him that Mrs. Hughes' place had been taken by her,
and, with due respect to the late Mrs. Hughes, her rules
and regulations had ceased to exist. But it would be a mis-
take to spoil the surprise of Monday morning in advance.
She would bide her time.
Mr. Josey had finished his lunch and left them. "Frankly,"
said Mrs. More to Mr. Ribble, "that man doesn't think of
anybody else but himself.""Perhaps he doesn't even think of himself," giggled Mr.
Ribble. In twenty-four hours he would be on his way to
meet the barmaid, so he had good reason to giggle.
"Good afternoon, Mrs. More," said Mr. Strong from the
door. "I have come to take Eileen for a little walk. Perhaps
you'd care to join us?"
"Willingly," said Mrs. More, and soon they were walk-
ing toward the sea with Mr. Strong on Eileen's left and
Mrs. More on her right. A little breeze was blowing, and
the sky was so blue you didn't believe it could know that
winter was in store for it.
Eileen walked mechanically, looking at neither of them.
Her head was slightly bowed, and her eyes were on the
ground, which was mostly pebble. Mr. Strong was in a
good mood. They'd had oxtail for lunch, his favorite dish.
At the council meeting he had floored old Morton who
would be his rival at the next elections. But above it all,
the worry of Eileen would soon cease. When he had asked
Mrs. More to go for a walk with them, he had vaguely
thought that he would tell her the entire Eileen story.
However, it was nice in the open, and Monday would be
as good a day as any. They were talking to each other
across Eileen and at times forgot that she was there be-
tween them.
"The more I think of the Red House," said Mrs. More,
"the more convinced I am that it could be turned into a
gold mine."
"I'd go quietly in the beginning," said Mr. Strong.
"I fully agree with you," shouted Mrs. More because of
the breeze, "but one must never miss an opportunity. Newmanagement, new methods. I tell you, Mr. Strong, it's a
gold mine."
"I hope it will be," said Mr. Strong, who disliked contra-
dicting anybody a second time.
"The only trouble is," called Mrs. More from Eileen's
other side, "that it'll be a lot of work. You know, Mr.
Strong, if my poor daughter were still with us-oh, she
could be such a help to me." For the rest of the walk she
gave Mr. Strong a good dose of Peggy. Peggy this and
Peggy that, and oblivious of the living Eileen she spoke of
her dead daughter for a good half hour.
Because Mrs. More was abroad, John sat undisturbed in
the lounge. He sat there for a while, then to his own sur-
prise he went up to the attic where the charred alphabets
were. He switched on the light, stood for a while motion-
less, and eventually asked himself why he had come up. He
took up one alphabet, then another, then dropped them,
then picked them up again, and again he stood motionless
staring at the cobwebs in the corner. The toys came next.
He looked at them, shook them as though expecting they
would start performing for him. The pictures against the
wall were next.
He didn't know much about pictures but saw they were
a set of French prints, badly foxed. He wiped off some of
the dust. Probably they weren't a set. One represented an
angling scene, another a hunting scene, a third a landscape
with a boat, the fourth a fortress, but the fifth was more
interesting. It depicted a scene in a tavern. There was a
table, and people clad in mid-eighteenth century costume
were playing cards. A broken hand mirror lay on the floor,apparently dropped by a cardsharp whom another man was
stabbing in the back. A dog and a cat, completely uncon-
cerned, were eating from a plate in the corner. The door
was open, and a couple of fowls were standing in the door-
way.
It was, thought John, a good print, superior to the others.
Rapacity coupled with the sudden agony of death had
made the cardsharp distort his mouth. Several years ago in
a London pub he had witnessed a stabbing scene. He had
seen the stabbed man fall over, and the man's expression
was similar to the cardsharp's. Later from the papers he had
found out that the stabbed man had been a racecourse tout
who liked to decamp with his punters' money.
Why, he idly asked himself, were those prints in the
attic? Probably Mrs. Hughes had bought them at an auc-
tion in the same lot with a couple of chairs or a swing
mirror and, as they meant nothing to her, had them carried
up to that forgotten room. He held the picture for some
time in his hand, later he put them all back against the wall
and wondered again why he had come up.
The house was silent. Evidently Mr. Josey had taken
Mrs. More's hint and wasn't playing. He lighted a ciga-
rette, and it occurred to him that there was no reason
whatever to linger in the attic. He gave a glance to the
alphabets, switched out the lights, and went down. He
knew that he was after something, but he wished he knew
what that something was. Never mind what it was, he
should leave it alone. He should think hard of Skinner and
whether it was worth his while to let Williams see him. He
looked into the sitting room, but Eileen was still out.He was wrong where Mr. Josey was concerned. For in
order to spite Mrs. More, Mr. Josey would have hammered
away on the piano even if he hadn't felt like playing. But
the simple truth was that Mr. Josey was abroad. It had
taken him a full day to make his decision; now having made
it, he thought he shouldn't wait any longer. One thing Mr.
Strong and Mr. Josey had in common, laziness. Mr. Strong's
laziness was the laziness of the optimist, whereas Mr. Josey
mostly refrained from action because he was convinced it
would lead nowhere. Still, it was too late to back out, since
backing out would mean having to think again. He walked
as far as Remembrance Avenue. If he could help it, he
would not take a bus. It gave him a sense of satisfaction to
know that he had done the bus company out of twopence.
At the corner of Remembrance Avenue was the Catholic
church. Near the church was the presbytery. He rang the
bell, and the priest's housekeeper opened the door for him.
"Is the Father here?" Mr. Josey asked.
"He has a wedding," the housekeeper said, "but he'll be
back for tea."
"I wait," said Mr. Josey.
The housekeeper knew him by sight. The Catholic com-
munity was small, and it was easy to notice each member
of it. Generally speaking, Mr. Josey was not a good Catho-
lic. He went to Mass whenever he felt like it, which was
rare. He had not been to confession for two years, took
no part in the social life of the community, and when the
plate came round he liked to immerse himself in sudden
prayer. He had not spoken to the priest for the last twelve-
month."Is it very urgent?" the housekeeper asked.
"Very," said Mr. Josey.
"Then come in, please. Father won't be long."
She took him into the study which was full of books;
there were also two leather armchairs, old and not much of
the leather left. On the wall was an oleograph of St.
Thomas of Canterbury and a crucifix on the writing desk.
A picture of the Blessed Virgin hung over the mantelpiece.
Two pipes and a pouch were on a small table.
in
"Please wait here," the housekeeper said.
Mr. Josey was wearing his best dark blue suit. Audrey
wasn't good at cleaning boots. She put too much paste on

every sense, Mr. Josey had cleaned his shoes himself. He

Gardens meant nothing to him. Time went on and he took

At last the priest came. He was corpulent, elderly, and
wore spectacles. He had a slow, polite manner.
"I'm sorry I've kept you waiting, sir," he said.
"You remember me, Father?" Mr. Josey asked.
"Yes, I do, sir. You live at the Red House where that
awful tragedy happened the other day."
"Yes. My name is José Rodrigues.""I remember your name, Mr. Rodrigues. Won't you sít
down?"
Mr. Josey sat down. He was becoming impatient. He
had come with a specific reason, and he wanted to get it
over as quickly as possible. "May I speak?" he asked.
"By all means, sir," said the priest. He had sat down, too.
"It is this," said Mr. Josey. "I want to be a monk." The
priest nodded. "I want to be quickly a monk."
"Perhaps you've had trouble or disillusionment?" asked
the priest. He was trying to sum Mr. Josey up.
"Plenty. But this is it. I am fifty. I've been in England
thirty-one years. My life never was amusing. Very little
fun I did have. I wanted to be a concert pianist, but I was
a player in cinemas. That was before the cinema spoke.
Now they make their own music. Then I was a traveler.
Nails. I made some money, but I kept it because I spend
little. My father, my mother, my grandfather, and grand-
mother they all were always very poor. So I could save my
money. It was much work, that traveling, Father. Always
bad food in the hotels, but I don't eat much. Then I stopped
working because I put enough money in the bank. I lived
at the Red House. Mrs. Hughes, she was my friend. I never
had friends, she was my only friend. If I was ill, she was
upset; if it was cold, she gave me a hot water bottle. She
liked my piano-playing. Then they kill her. Now I have
no more friends, and I was thinking, and I think that I will
be a monk. Please help me."
"Certainly, I want to help you," said the priest. "But is
your loneliness a sufficient reason for you to want to enter
a religious house? What I'm trying to say is that a suddenresolution like that often wears off after a time. I can well
understand what a shock Mrs. Hughes' death must have
been to you, Mr. Rodrigues, but that isn't a serious reason
for you to wish to become a monk. And, if I may say so,
I know practically nothing of your religious life."
"I want to be a monk," said Mr. Josey. "Please help me.
My uncle he was a monk, too."
"I see," said the priest. "I don't want to discourage you.
You know of course that as a monk your lot would be
poverty, perpetual chastity, and entire obedience? And
don't you think that at your age perhaps it is too late to
start?"
"No," said Mr. Josey. "Please help me.”
It was difficult to talk to him, thought the priest. If he
had been an Englishman, he would more or less have
known where he stood with him; but he was a Spaniard
with the overriding background of a Catholicism that was
not abstract or only of the spirit but was the practical daily
companion of his life. Probably to become a monk was as
natural to him as piano-playing had been in the silent film
days.
"Please help me, Father," said Mr. Josey again.
"Mr. Rodrigues," said the priest, "I think I know what I
should suggest to you. You are, frankly speaking, fed up
with the world, and at present it seems that you don't want
to be of the world any more. Now I happen to know the
Cistercian abbot of the monastery. You surely have seen
the monastery.
It's ten minutes' walk from Remembrance
Avenue." Mr. Josey shook his head. “Well, sir, I'll ring him
up, and I can arrange that you should go to the monasteryas a lay-boarder. You'll be away from the world, you won't
be disturbed in your prayer, you'll have religious guidance
at hand, and then you'll be able to make up your mind in
the right surroundings."
"Cistercians?" said Mr. Josey. "My uncle was a Fran-
ciscan."
"Probably you know," said the priest, "that the Cister-
cians are a silent order here in England."
"That's all right," said Mr. Josey, getting up. "I don't
speak much. I'll be a Cistercian monk. When can I go?"
The priest smiled. "I'll speak to the abbot tomorrow.
We were at Ushaw together. Come and see me on Monday
morning. Which way are you going, sir?”
"To the Red House."
"Then we can walk together for a while. I'm going to
South Street."
Mr. Josey walked, wordless, beside the priest. Evidently,
thought the priest, silence wouldn't worry him. He had to
do all the talking. He told him of their Catholic social held
last Wednesday which had been quite a success. Then the
priest went on to say that his parish was poor, consisting
mostly of Irish agricultural laborers. For some time past
they had tried to raise enough money to buy a piano for
their social center, but to date they hadn't enough money
to buy even a gramophone, which was a pity, for he would
like his poor parishioners to enjoy themselves. Quite a few
of them had to tramp ten miles to come to Mass. Mr. Josey
stifled a grin. He had a piano, a piano that had been left to
him, but that piano was his business and nobody else's.
They came to the corner of South Street. A bus was un-loading its tired cargo of passengers. The new moon had
just gone. The priest stopped.
"So come and see me on Monday morning, sir," said the
priest. "In the meantime, pray to Almighty God that He
should give you guidance. Pray hard, and I am sure the
Holy Ghost will guide you in the right direction."
"I will pray," said Mr. Josey. He shook the priest by the
hand.
"I will pray for you," the priest said, and Mr. Josey
thanked him.

Sunday was a bright day with
the sea calm and the sun alone in
the sky. Mr. Ribble had risen at

The Haileys had had a long

"Pull the bloomin' curtain," he muttered. "The bloody

"You've got your tie on," she said to Hailey.
"That's my business," he said.
"Somebody's ringing."
"Audrey can answer it."
She put her head on his shoulder, her nose against his

Incidentally, it was Mrs. More that answered the door.
She had risen early, as she wished to know what the house

heard the bell. She listened, waiting for one of the Haileys

"I want the empties," he said.
Mrs. More looked for the empties but saw none. So she
hurried to the kitchen. There were many bottles all over

"Why?"
"Never mind. What time?"
"Seven thirty."
"I'll have a word with you then," said Mrs. More, shut-

Yawning and grunting, unwashed and sleepy, Audrey

"You gave me a start, ma'am," said Audrey.
Tomorrow, thought Mrs. More grimly, she would give

Mrs. Strong came at quarter past ten to take Eileen for

"You do annoy me at times," she said.

A little commotion started in the Red House at eleven:

"Feeling under the weather, dear?" the cook asked.

There was a corridor facing the sitting room but on the

She left him, and John stood in the middle of the room

He tapped his foot impatiently; it was part of a pattern he
couldn't see clearly yet. It was a barren room. No pictures
on the wall, linoleum on the floor, and a mean hand mirror
on a small table. That room, he thought, had no soul; it
was dead. And there Eileen had to sleep.
He looked for a dressing table, but there wasn't one. A
painted white pine wardrobe faced the narrow bed. The
key was in it. He opened the wardrobe and saw a few
tweed tailor-mades and a few simple cotton frocks. Three
pairs of shoes, brogues every one of them. The lingerie was
hardly worthy of the name. A little cotton but mostly drabwool. He walked round the room. Hard kitchen chairs,
and there were only two of them. He went to the window,
which looked out on the back. The view before him con-
sisted of a few derelict chicken houses, a pigsty that was
falling to pieces, and a lot of odds and ends the dustman
must have forgotten to take away.
He saw Eileen rising in the morning, her woolen night-
gown probably a size too large for her, her feet cold on
the linoleum. In her eternal silence her eyes were surely her
one link with life. Her eyes looked at the bare walls, then
at the chicken houses and the crumbling pigsty. He stopped
at the bed and stared at the flat pillow, the white blankets;
he lifted the pillow and held it to his face before putting
it back. There was nothing more to observe in the room,
but he lingered on. There was an answer to that room. He
was convinced of it; but how get the answer if the ques-
tion wasn't yet formulated?
On his way out he looked into the next room, which he
knew at once had been Mrs. Hughes' room. The curtains
were red, and a gaudy Turkish carpet was on the floor.
The dressing table was large; there were three mirrors in
the room. Two seascapes of little value but with much con-
ventional coloring hung on the wall. John did not examine
the room carefully because time was getting short and he
didn't want to miss Williams.
He took the bus outside the Crown. The bus was quite
empty. First it rattled through the poorer quarters of the
town, then into the open country. The sea ran almost
parallel with them. It was nice to watch the sea in the sun-
shine.Suddenly he asked himself why life had turned against
him from the beginning. If only he could live there quietly
beside the sea, going for long walks, sailing in a boat, living
in peace. But was it truly life that had turned against him?
Suppose it was he that had turned against life? It was easy
to suppose, and probably it was the truth. Still, it would
be nice in a boat. He could see the boat. The sea wasn't so
quiet as today, and the boat heaved and pitched, but
lightly, running with the wind; and Eileen sat behind the
mast. He wasn't surprised about Eileen being in the boat.
And what a bedroom he would give her. Color and com-
fort, vases full of flowers, and a fur rug beside the bed wait-
ing for her small narrow feet.
"This is Marshdon," said the ticket collector.

John shook his head. It was too good to be true, and be-
sides good things didn't come his way. He'd never had six
hundred pounds in his life. Was six hundred pounds
enough to start a new life? If he managed carefully, proba-
bly it was. He might buy himself a partnership in some
small business, and by working like the devil, he might
even succeed. He remembered a chap called Martin who
had a garage in Berkshire, and would be doing well if only
he could get hold of a little capital to enlarge the garage
and buy some modern equipment. Now Martin knew the
instinctive understanding John had of motors and often

"Perfectly. Tell me, who's Mr. Bush?"
"A bookie, but he's got plenty of money, and by money
I mean cash.""Williams, for God's sake don't make any roughhouse.

"You leave it to me," said Williams, a little annoyed. He
always had a sense of guilt when people rubbed it in that

there's no need to worry.'

He saw John into the bus and returned to Mr. Bush.
John found the Red House with the dusty staircase that
seemed to lead into a silent vacuum. Eileen was out, Mrs.
More was sleeping erect in the lounge, and there wasn't
even Mr. Josey playing the piano upstairs. He had an hour
to while away before he could set out for the Maxwells. He
was looking forward to meeting Miss Maxwell as though
she could solve for him matters of grave personal concern.
He heard distant voices mounting from the kitchen. He
went down and, finding the kitchen door open, looked in.
Mrs. Hailey was sitting on a chair, cigarette in mouth,
watching Audrey washing up.
"Hullo," she said.
"I'm bored," said John, “so I've come here, if you don't
mind."
"You're welcome," she said.
He was annoyed with himself because of late he had ac-

knowing why he stopped. Now it was the turn of thekitchen knives. There were many of them. They were all
in a big basket, some dirty, some clean.
"You'll cut yourself," said Mrs. Hailey.

"The only thing in the world Hailey likes doing," she

nerves. 'E's got nerves." She laughed with the cigarette re-
maining stuck to her lips.
John lifted a knife from the basket, then another; they
were long knives and short knives, knives of all sizes and
descriptions. "If you think of it," he said, "any of these
knives might have been the one Mrs. Hughes was killed
with."
"Don't say so," said Mrs. Hailey, dropping the cigarette
from her mouth. "You did give me a start." She went up to
the basket and stared at the knives. "Any of them knives,"
she said. "Well, I never." Audrey was staring, too.
"All the murderer had to do," said John, "was wash the
knife in cold water and drop it back into the basket."
"And then it was Hailey who sharpened it for the mur-
derer," Mrs. Hailey said. "Then he's what they call an ac-
complice."
"Not quite," said John. "Anyway, the knife mightn't
have come from here at all."
"I 'opes not," said Audrey.
"You're a clever man, Mr. Denton," said Mrs. Hailey,
sitting down. "You tell us who you think killed her.”
"I haven't the faintest idea. Don't forget, I'm a stranger
here."
"Well, I think it was Mr. Josey. I know it's not nice tosay so, but I wouldn't blame 'im if he did. The way she
carried on with 'im, I was regular sorry for 'im. Running
after 'im all the time."
"But surely that isn't enough reason to murder?"
"But you know what them foreigners are like, though I
know it isn't nice to say so. If he did it, the police would
have catched him, what?”

"You see," said Audrey.

says who killed Mrs. Hughes? You want me to?"
Audrey had blushed. "I says nothing," she said. "You

John began to laugh, but after a while it seemed to him

"I never says that," said Audrey. "I never says that."

"Do you know," said Mrs. Hailey, "that when Hailey

"Sorry. Anyway, I must be going.”
He had heard the front door open. Perhaps it was Eileen.
As he came out of the kitchen, he saw Mr. Strong leaving

He found her in the sitting room.
"Dearest Eileen,” he said. He took both her hands, and

nobody could be worthy of that look. "I've missed you,"

They looked out through the window, and then he

"Miss Maxwell. You see, Eileen, you're always in my

He bent forward and kissed her. It was a light kiss.While he kissed her, he felt as though he were entering a
room that had never been visited before; and the lightness
