must be off," he said.
His coming gave her pleasure, his going she humbly
acquiesced to. "You ask nothing," he said, "because you
know they would give you nothing." He went out.
The sun was still undisturbed in the sky. It was shining
down on Mr. Ribble's outing, which hadn't started well.
Eddie, the barmaid's driver friend, wasn't available; he had
a funeral on. He gave him the telephone number of another
driver, and the man said he would be at the Red House at
two sharp. The taxi was punctual, but that was all Mr.
Ribble could say for it. It was a small Austin that must
have seen better days many years ago. The door nearly fell
on him as he opened it.
"You give it a hard bang, sir," said the driver. He went
on to say that the starter didn't work. After a lot of
energetic cranking, the car started off. The springs were
tired with age, making Mr. Ribble take a vigorous bumping
before the car reached the Crown. The driver alighted and
paced up and down, smoking a short cigar. As if, thought
Mr. Ribble, he were the one who was waiting for the
plumpest little woman in the world. The driver had a red
mustache and two pimples on his left cheek.
"The lady you said, sir, who's coming with us," he said,
“is she living here?"
"No," said Mr. Ribble. It wasn't the driver's business to
ask him questions."In that case," said the driver, "she's lucky. This is the
most lousy joint in the world."
The barmaid appeared. She wore a trim red suit and
high-heeled shoes. The skirt was short, making her figure
appear fatter than it was. Because of her high heels she
swayed to and fro in her walk. Enchanted, Mr. Ribble
burst open the door in order to help her in. The door clat-
tered onto the pavement.
"Now you've done it," said the driver. "Haya, Gwen,"
he said to the barmaid; then turning his back on her, he
began to fix the door. "You mustn't use that door again,"
he said when he had finished. "I couldn't fix it a second
time. Use the other door. So you're coming with us,
Gwen?"
Mr. Ribble was hot with anger and jealousy as he led her
round the back of the car.
"Why did you get him?" she hissed. "Why didn't you
get Eddie?"
"He wasn't available."
"I hate Bob," she said. "He has no manners."
Apparently she knew every taxi-driver in town by his
Christian name. That wasn't a good beginning for the
feverishly awaited outing. Nevertheless, Mr. Ribble felt
better as he sat down beside her in the narrow tonneau of
the car. As she had climbed into the car, he had seen a bit
of her thigh which nicely bulged above the stocking.
"There is so much I want to talk to you about," he
sighed.
The car began to shake violently. "I must," said the
driver chattily, "always choke her till she bursts."They shot forward, then the car settled down to a crawl.
The barmaid wanted to close the partition between the
driver and them, but the handle came away. She dropped
it furiously.
"You ought to know, Gwen," the driver said, "that it's
only an ornament.”
Gwen, thought Mr. Ribble, Gwen. The driver knew her
name was Gwen, but she had never bothered to tell him.
For a moment he wanted to stop the car, get out, and leave
them to it, but that moment the car took a corner, and
Gwen fell against him. She must have bigger breasts than
he had expected.
"I hope we'll have a nice outing," he said in a small
voice.
"Why did
you take him?” she whispered. "I hate him.
He's so common."
Humbly Mr. Ribble again explained the reasons he had
been unable to engage the other driver. They had reached
the sea and were progressing along a bumpy road. Crum-
bling tank traps decayed in the midst of rusty coils of
barbed wire. Of sea gulls there were plenty, and a small
coaster with a huge pall of smoke was pitching far out at
sea. It was a lonely view.
"You'll see how lovely it is at Marshdon Park," said Mr.
Ribble. "It's simply beautiful. I used to go there a lot when
I was a young man. As a matter of actual fact, I'm looking
forward to seeing it very much. Haven't been there for ten
years, or perhaps more." He giggled and tried to pinch her
arm.
Marshdon's small port had been in his time a fine roman-tic place. A nice teashop, a landing stage, a few boats an-
chored in the bay, and the woods of Marshdon Park
stretching toward the mansion half a mile inland. Rocks
tumbled over one another on the other side of the bay, and
it would be up to her to sit either in the shelter of the rocks
or to stroll in the woods; he remembered there were a
number of benches in shaded quiet spots.
"You'll love it," he said.
Unfortunately, the wood was gone; it had been cut out
during the war. "Here we are," said the driver.
Mr. Ribble took Gwen by the arm and hurried her to-
ward the rocks. On the other side of the landing stage, just
where the rocks began, there was a barbed-wire entangle-
ment and a notice: DANGER. KEEP OUT.
"You brought me to a nice place,” she said.
"But it used to be so different," said Mr. Ribble. “It was
so pretty here. Now where should we go? Come, you sug-
gest some place. We've got the car."
"I don't know no place."
"Now, don't get discouraged. We'll go for a tiny,
weeny walk, and then we'll find a nice teashop and have
tea." He spoke quickly, eagerly.
"And you call this an outing," she said, but followed
him.
They passed three deserted Nissen huts, then a gun em-
placement. Suddenly Mr. Ribble saw a bench. It was a sad
bench, lost and alone. When the wood was cut out, all the
other benches had been taken away, but somehow or other
that bench had been overlooked; or perhaps the military
had become bored with benches."Let's sit down here," said Mr. Ribble.
They sat down, and there was a low concrete wall be-
fore them. If they looked to the right, they saw detonated
sea mines; a gun emplacement faced them on the left. Mr.
Ribble giggled. She had crossed her legs, showing her
fattish knees. There was no getting away from it; she must
be his.
"I want to speak to you very seriously," he said.
"What is it?" she asked, playing with her bag.
Mr. Ribble told her. He told her exactly what she had
always wanted from life. No work, her own home, peace,
a little car, a daily char, a radio-gramophone-in fact he
was depicting her heaven. The girl listened; he was sweat-
ing a lot. His trim mustache went up and down, and his
eyes traveled rapaciously up and down the red suit. Now
and then he touched her, and his giggles were profuse.
"And a nice banking account for you all alone," he said.
She was no fool, she knew precisely what she wanted.
The little banking account meant security, and the daily
woman meant no work. She couldn't wish for more. Her
life at the Crown wasn't an easy one; life in her own home
would be more than she had ever seriously hoped for.
What she hated most was her work: hours in the smoke,
continuously on the lookout for rudeness, and then wash-
ing up
for hours. And her feet, her poor feet. Just to sit
back and not have to use her feet at all. She had a bunion
on her left foot, and the bunion had a corn. The two to-
gether were simply agony. Now here was Mr. Ribble
bringing her salvation. The corn on the bunion would have
to hurt no more.She looked at the Nissen hut, at the mines, at the con-
crete wall, and eventually she looked at Mr. Ribble. With
no shade about, the sun was shining hard on the beads of
perspiration trembling like tiny bubbles on his chin. His
false teeth were ready to nibble at her the moment she said
yes. And that moment, perhaps for the only time in her
life, she rose above herself, or perhaps rose to her own true
level, never to return to it again: but it was fine while it
lasted.
"I want," she said, "a house. I want to stop working, but
I don't want you."
As a life without Mr. Ribble was now before her, she
smiled, notwithstanding her aching foot; then she laughed.
She laughed with deep happiness, and if that second could
last, hers would be its lasting glory. But it didn't. Half
angry with herself, she started to hate Mr. Ribble because
she had said no to him and thus thrown away the house,
the radio-gramophone, and the charwoman.
"You're an ugly old man," she said, rising. “And who
the hell do you think you are to take a girl to a place like
this? Next time you'll take me to a sewage farm, eh?" Mr.
Ribble sat motionless in utter misery. "And don't you," she
scowled, "try to paw me again at the Crown when I serve
you a drink, or I'll have you kicked out."
She walked away quickly. Mr. Ribble stood up; on his
way back to the car he nearly fell over a mine.
"This is a darned lousy place," said the driver, "and
don't get in through that door. It'll fall out again."
Gwen sat erect in the car; she didn't even cross her legs.
"If
you would only marry me," said Mr. Ribble. "I'dnever be lonely again, with a fat little woman like you be-

The car rattled on while Gwen thought of the work be-
fore her, today, tomorrow, and all days. With hatred that
made her voice tremble she said, without bothering to look
at Mr. Ribble, “Now, if you was like that Mr. Denton,
handsome, young, and a real gent, I'd marry you even if
you hadn't a bean."
The car stopped. The driver got out, tried to crank it
up, but the engine remained as silent as Mr. Ribble.
"You've had it," said the driver. "It's no good your wait-
ing here. I'll have to phone for somebody to tow the old

twenty-eight bob."
Mr. Ribble paid. By the time he had paid and received
his change, Gwen was about a hundred yards ahead of him.
She was a fine plump sight, swaying lightly in the middle
of the road.
Mr. Ribble walked slowly, afraid to catch up with her.
She turned into a side street: it was useless to follow her.
He looked at his watch. It was four twenty. The outing

"Hullo," said John.
"Hullo," said Mr. Ribble. After all it wasn't John's fault."Nice and warm," said John.

It was a curious fact about him, and it surprised him at
times, that he was inevitably punctual. At five thirty sharp
he rang Mr. Maxwell's bell. It was a small house with no

"Come in, Mr. Denton, come in," he said. He was wear-

for this time of the year."
"It is, indeed," said John, entering the tiny hall. A stair-

"This way," said Mr. Maxwell. "If somebody had told
me six months ago that Mr. Denton's son would be having

come, Mr. Denton."
The room he took John to was mostly furnished by an
oak dining table which left room for little else. Miss Max-
well was sitting at the table with her back to the window.
She resembled her brother but had masses of white hair. As
she rose, John saw that she was practically a dwarf. Per-
haps that was why she lived in a small house like this. They

ate in order to ingratiate himself with her, all the while try-

one. You could see she would easily take offense, and to
judge from her flow of conversation she hadn't a lowopinion of herself. It wasn't difficult to imagine how simple
it had been for Mrs. Hughes to offend her. She ate quickly
and a large quantity. She wore gold-rimmed glasses, her

"I understand," said John, when she had finished eating,
"that you have now retired, Miss Maxwell."
"I'm getting on, Mr. Denton, but I still take a private
pupil at times. I have now a very promising pupil. Do you
know the Arrowsmiths?" John said he didn't. "They live
at Moat Hall. They have a little boy. After three years of
teaching he can now write and read. I'm very proud of
him."
"Deaf and dumb?"
"Oh, yes. For the last twenty years I've only taught deaf
and dumbs. I started with backward children, but later I
specialized in deaf and dumbs. I taught at an institute for
twelve years."
She was proud of her work; it was all that mattered to
her. John thought there was something fine and moving
about her eagerness.
"It must be wonderful," he said, "to be able to bring life
to people who otherwise would be completely sealed off
from the world."
"If I had my life over again, I wouldn't chose any other
profession," she said. Mr. Maxwell was a silent speck at the
other end of the table.
"And always successful?" John asked.
"Always successful," she said proudly.
"But not with Eileen Hughes."
"That wasn't my fault.""I imagined that. Whose fault was it?”

"I arrived at the Red House the day before her mother

"It isn't true."

"So there's poor Eileen completely cut off from the

Mr. Maxwell blew his nose.
"Better switch the light on," said Miss Maxwell to him.
He switched the light on, then he said he thought he would

"I'll tell you what I know about Eileen," she said. "She's

"Very pretty."
She gave him a long questioning glance, but he just
smiled at her. It seemed to her that he was nice, kindly.
"Eileen was about nine years old when I first met herfather. He was a good man. Of course not a gentleman, but
good and hard working. He loved Eileen very much. To
make a long story short, I agreed to teach Eileen. She has
brains, that girl, believe me that, Mr. Denton. We got on
nicely. I taught her sign language, but anybody can teach
sign language. What I wanted to achieve with her was to
teach her not only to write and read but to understand it
all, to appreciate the book she would be reading, to be able
to write a letter as an intelligent, educated person. I have a
pupil-he isn't my pupil any more-he's thirty-two. But he
writes me letters about the books he's read, about the peo-
ple he meets, and I can assure you that you wouldn't be-
lieve he's deaf and dumb if you saw his letters. He can even
make sounds that are very near to words. But the most im-
portant thing is that he can follow events, he doesn't feel
lost, in fact he considers himself one of us." Her old face
was rosy with enthusiasm. "But you want me to talk of
Eileen. Well, we got on very well. I also believe that my
pupils must have fun. Flowers, color, nothing sad."
"Eileen's room is as bare as a prison cell."
"I'll come to that, Mr. Denton. So we got on well, she
was learning fast, and then poor Mr. Hughes died. Mrs.
Hughes asked me not to come for a little while because she
was so busy settling her husband's affairs. I quite under-
stood that. But I waited two months, and she didn't send
for me, and I knew only too well that it would do the girl's
studies a lot of harm if they were suspended for a long
time. I telephoned her, and she said that she and Eileen
were going away for a few weeks but she'd let me know
the moment they got back. I said that was all right. Youknow, this is a small town and everybody knows every-
body. Two days later I found out they hadn't left. But it
wasn't my business. I waited for another month, and then
I telephoned again. She said she was selling the business,
and she was too busy for the moment. She wasn't what I
would call civil to me on the telephone. Naturally, I
thought she had engaged another teacher. That was her
business, but I was sorry to lose Eileen. She was so prom-
ising."
"How old was Eileen then?"
"Over eleven. Now she must be getting on to twenty. A
week later I passed the shop. It was in High Street. It's still
there but belongs to somebody else, as you doubtless know.
I looked into the shop, and they told me Mrs. Hughes was
upstairs. They lived over the shop. So I went upstairs, saw
nobody, looked into one room, then another, and then I
looked into Eileen's room. When I'd been last time in her
room, she had all sorts of nice things there. Books, picture
books, toys, flowers, and some very nice French pictures
on the wall. Everything was gone. The room was bare. I
was shocked. I heard somebody coming, and it was Mrs.
Hughes. A painful, very painful interview then took place.
She was rude to me. She was as rude as a fishwife to me.
She who pretended to be a real lady. She was a doctor's
daughter and used to pretend that she'd come down in life
when she married an ironmonger. All I can say is that the
ironmonger had much better manners than she had. She
was rude. She said it wasn't my business to go snooping
round the house. Would you believe that a lady should
speak like that, Mr. Denton?""Of course, I wouldn't," said John. He was thinking of
the pictures. He must have another look at them.
"She said," continued Miss Maxwell, "that it was none
of
my business to enter her flat without her permission. I
was firm with her and told her I wasn't accustomed to
being spoken to like that. Then she said she didn't want
me to teach Eileen any more and that I hadn't, if you
please, been a good influence on Eileen. I asked her why.
She said I was putting all sorts of ideas into Eileen's head.
Eileen was her daughter, and she'd do as she pleased. She
said she was going to teach Eileen in future. She was her

She said I was spoiling Eileen's life, and Eileen would come

Eileen, and I asked her sarcastically if it was good for
Eileen to have her pictures and flowers taken away from
her. Then she showed me to the door. Nobody has ever
treated me like that in my life, Mr. Denton."
"Simply horrid," said John, "but was that the last you
saw of Eileen?"
"I saw her again two years later. I'll bet Mrs. Hughes
would have been angry if she'd known that we met again.
It was purely accidental. I ran into her near the estuary. I
was taking a pupil of mine for a walk. I went up to her and
talked to her in sign language, and my pupil joined in, and

don Road. Mrs. Hughes went into a shop, and Eileen

She had forgotten that, too."
It seemed to John that he should remain silent and wait

"Not entirely," said John. "There are a few written
signs which she can understand. Such as come or go. I saw
them in the sitting room, but there are so few of them that
one could wave to her and she'd understand that just as
well."
"What it all amounts to," said Miss Maxwell, "is that
thanks to her mother that girl is completely cut away from
every idea, every thought, and all her fellow humans."
"But tell me, why did her mother do that?"
"I honestly don't know. Nobody could say she didn't
love Eileen, because she did. What I often think is that in
her egoistical heart she simply wanted to keep Eileen alone
for herself and let her have no intercourse with anybody
else. But I might be wrong. I'm an old woman, yet I still
find it difficult to understand people. Anyway, she did
Eileen great harm. The worst possible harm in the world.
But perhaps I am biased.""Can't it be undone?"
"I doubt it. It's the same thing with foreign languages. If
you don't practice them, you forget them. But if you for-
get your only language, and years and years go by with-
out using the language, well, then you're finished."
"But if I ask you, Miss Maxwell, in fact beg you on my
knees, would you try? We can't leave her in eternal dark-
ness, can we?"
Miss Maxwell was moved. She would have liked to pat
John's hand, but it wouldn't be right to take such liberties.
"You're a very nice gentleman, Mr. Denton," she said.
"I'm not, really. You know I was in jail, and I've been a
very bad boy."
"I knew that. Everybody in this town read it in the
papers at the time. But it wasn't your fault. You've had a
bad deal."
John shook his head. "I don't want to speak about my-
self. It's Eileen. Will you please try, and try hard?"
"I promise you
I will. When do you think it would be
best for me to go and see her?"
He wanted to say as soon as possible, but instead he said,
"I don't think tomorrow would do. What about Tuesday
morning?" By Tuesday the awful merry-go-round that
was now carrying the basket of kitchen knives would
surely have stopped.
"I'll look at my engagement book," said Miss Maxwell.
Her engagement book was a large affair. "It must be
early," she said. "I'm engaged at eleven. Do you think nine
would be too early?"
"Nothing of the sort. You are kind, Miss Maxwell. Godwill bless you for that. Just tell me one more thing before
I
go.
Did Eileen have many alphabets?"
"All my pupils have as many as I can find for them. I
want them to like letters, be interested in them, and then
they'll love words."
"In that case, you'll have to buy Eileen new ones. All
her books were torn and burned by Mrs. Hughes. She was

Miss Maxwell nodded. "You're very devoted to her, Mr.
Denton?"
"I'm very devoted to her."
Miss Maxwell had to shout for Mr. Maxwell. She re-
peated her promise to be at the Red House at nine sharp on

evening was in, and John was surprised to find that instead
of feeling hope and elation he was depressed, though where

into words what he had suspected. And why didn't he
want her to come tomorrow?
By the time he reached the Crown it was seven, so he
thought he could do with a drink. The drink cheered him

ing, Miss Maxwell would restore Eileen to the world, Wil-
liams would get the paper back from Skinner, and in the

Eileen.
"Isn't the world lousy?" he said to the barmaid.
"You're telling me," she said. The glow of her triumphhad worn off, the bunion was hurting badly, and the saloon

"Hullo," said Mr. Haxton.

"I don't think I can do much about it," said Mr. Haxton.
Nothing that day had succeeded in putting him into a good

The unwritten letters consisted without exception of the

ago. The snow was hard, and the frost seemed everlasting.
In their small snowbound garden he had picked up a half-
frozen green woodpecker. He had taken it indoors, and his
wife had nearly been in tears. She put the woodpecker into
the airing-cupboard, which was hot, and hoped the heat
would revive the bird. The woodpecker was found dead
the following day, though the bowl that had contained
milk and bread crumbs was empty. His wife cried; and
now he wouldn't even go to the funeral of her mother.

Voices rose in the lounge. Angry voices, and he thanked

admit that he wasn't surprised at seeing her. She had aged:
but he had aged too. She was tired, and from the expe-
rience of many dead years he knew that she was unhappy.
He also knew that she was ill at ease. That curious move-
ment of her eyelashes always betrayed it. She was wearing
shoes he hadn't seen before, the black bag was the same he
had bought her at the time of her father's funeral. She was
a familiar stranger, who, though in several ways unknown,
still carried the memory of the typhoid fever she had
nursed him through just after the first great war. She had
risen. She could be very erect, her lame leg notwithstand-
ing. He used to be proud of her carriage.
"So you've arrived, Martha,” he said. It wasn't said to
put her at ease in front of Mrs. More and John, but it was
the first sentence that came into his head.
"It was a long journey," she said.
"I bet it was," he said.
"And now," said Mrs. More, "Mrs. Haxton wants to
travel back this evening. She'll be done to the world by the
time you get back. You ought to persuade her to stay the
night.""Out of the question," said Mrs. Haxton.
Mr. Haxton couldn't resist a smile. It wasn't her habit to
tolerate other people interfering with her plans, in fact it
annoyed her if they supposed she could do with advice.
Mrs. More, having reached the conclusion that Mrs. Hax-
ton wasn't an amiable person, left them to hurry the meal.
The Haileys were in the kitchen, and while Mrs. More
gave orders to the cook and tried to explain to her that
even tinned soups could be served hot, she pretended not
to notice them, which was something of a task, as the
Haileys talked eighteen to the dozen.
"Lunch," she said, returning to the lounge, "will be
ready in a few minutes." She was wrong; they waited for
half an hour.
Mr. Haxton decided not to introduce Mr. Ribble to his
wife. Mr. Ribble ogled, smiled, and curiosity made him

More went to the kitchen. "If we have to wait much
longer," said Mrs. Haxton, "we'll miss the three twenty."
Mr. Haxton pretended he hadn't heard her.
"Is there anywhere here where we can talk?" she asked

"Come to my room," he said. She followed him up the

"No, thank you," said Mr. Josey.
"That was a nonsensical answer," said Mrs. Haxton,
following him into his room."You don't get many logical replies in this house," said
Mr. Haxton.
"It's a shocking place, and look at this room. It gives

"The whole place should give you the creeps," said Mr.

"It doesn't surprise me," she said. He was expecting her

"No, it was always there. I mean, ever since I came here."
"I must have been quite pretty in those days." They

"You know why I've come here."
"Is that the answer?"
"No, but let me sit down. My leg hurts." She sat down.
"You have no idea how much I had to do since mother

"Poor Martha. But why did you come here? Didn't you

"I got your letter. That's why I came. You must come

to live with her, and as I didn't want to inflict her on you,
I let
you go off for a while.""They must think you're the perfect wife."
He could almost hear the change coming into his life. A
few hours ago he had been convinced that the Red House

"I thought," said Mrs. Haxton, "that it was best for both

"One can't say you haven't perfect logic," smiled Mr.

on the photograph, and it tore many years off her.
"You're too clever for me," she said. "So you are com-

"I won't need much," he said.
"Surely you're not coming back here?"
"Where else should I go?" He started to say good-by in
earnest.
"I've been thinking hard, Fred. We're both old people

"Are you certain?"
"Just look at this room. Do you want to end your life

"I never hated you.'
""
"There you are, Fred. I'm going to pack now. You'll

"All right, Martha."
"Try to look more cheerful."
"I'll try," he smiled.
In spite of her bad leg, she packed quickly, efficiently.
"One of your razors is missing," she said.
"Now where could I have put it?" They searched for it

"We can't miss the train because of that razor," she said.

"Dear Eileen," he wrote, "I am saying good-by to you

"What a pity, but I don't blame anybody for not want-

She can read everything, can't she?"

"I did."
"Because I can't find it. You remember when you

"I'm sure I didn't. I don't want to cut myself again."
"Fred," called Mrs. Haxton."It's of no importance. Well, good-by, Mr. Denton. Best
of luck. Good-by."
"Good-by," said John; and because it was so much a fact

He heard the Haxtons go, he heard Mr. Haxton speaking

which the cardsharp was knifed. He heard Audrey shout-

"Where's Mr. Haxton?" he asked Mrs. More.
"They've just left.”
He flew to the front door which was still open, but the
taxi had gone. He went back into the house. How foolish

it didn't matter. Somebody would know Mr. Haxton's ad-

He wished Mrs. More had been in; he could have sent it off
right away.
Mrs. More, however, was walking fast toward High
Street. Mr. Strong had telephoned her that he wanted to
see her. She felt a bit diffident about their forthcoming
meeting. Her managerial career hitherto hadn't been a suc-
cess. She frankly admitted it. The waiter and waitress had
gone, the cook had given a week's notice, and now the
Haxtons had left. Still, the Haxtons were no concern of
hers, the Haileys were lazy drunks, and good riddance to

hoped Mr. Strong wouldn't ask too many questions. It
turned out that Mr. Strong had no questions whatever to
ask her. On the contrary, he wanted to impart some in-
formation.
It was no good, so he told himself in the morning, to de-
lay it any longer. The week had begun, and if he told the
whole thing to Mrs. More, the rest of the week would be
free. Luckily all the matters he had to attend to during the
week could easily be left to his clerk.
"I'm expecting a lady," he said and sat down at his desk.
It was such a nice desk. No papers on it and no letters wait-

pulled out a drawer, took a sheet of paper, and continued
to prepare his speech for the next council meeting. They
must have another public lavatory at the corner of Re-membrance Avenue, and no nonsense about that either.
"Progress is cleanliness," he wrote as Mr. Foster ushered
.Mrs. More into the room.

"Mrs. Hughes, you see, adored Eileen, but as far as I can

able lady taught her to write and read. Mr. Hughes died,

had no communication with the world, which could only
harm her."
"I can well understand that," said Mrs. More. Oh, if she
could only have saved Peggy from the dangers of the

that Eileen should communicate with the outer world only

"It was a very cruel heart," said Mr. Benson. "Nobody
has the right to tuck a person away. If she'd kept her in a

"Please let me finish," said Mr. Strong. "You'll change

"And, Mr. Benson," he said raising his voice, "the cruel

"You can learn it in a quarter of an hour. Look how easy

"She's a Miss Maxwell. Do you want her address? I'll

Mr. Benson walked from Bank Buildings with a curious
sense of detachment. He felt as though he had been in an
uncomfortably hot room and was in need of fresh air and
relaxation. He was proud of his mind because once again it
had lived up to his belief in it; but now it needed rest. He
refused to think any more. There was no hurry. It was
surprising that though he was so near to his goal there was
plenty of time left. He could switch the searchlight off.
It was a good searchlight.
The breeze was warm, and he could easily have sat down
in the gutter and had a good rest. But before his rest began,
he must think just for a moment of his program for to-
morrow. First the Red House, then Miss Maxwell, then he
must telephone the Yard and let the experts crack. He
hadn't dealt with a case like this before; probably nobody
ever had. He imagined the experts would be doctors or
something. What if he was wrong? He smiled and saw a
nice cozy teashop, which seemed preferable to the gutter.
He ordered himself tea, toast, and cake. Tea, he decided,was the finest invention in the world. Three cheers for tea.
He drank his tea with relish and noise. He spread mar-
garine and jam on his toast, congratulating his brain for
having thought of going to the ironmonger's. It hadn't been
a matter of luck. His brain must have worked up to it. But
who was the other person who had questioned the shop
assistant? And on the same day, too. Probably a coin-
cidence.
He paid for his tea, and with endless time at his disposal
he strolled toward the nearest bus stop. It was getting on
to six. Vaguely he planned to take a bus ride, and if he saw
a pub or a cinema he fancied, he would alight and have a
drink or see a flick. He asked himself whether he was en-
joying the case, and frankly answered that he wasn't,
though his satisfaction with his powerful brain didn't
diminish on account of that. At the bus stop there was a
small queue. John was in the queue laden with several par-
cels.
"Hullo, Denton," he said, "out on a jaunt?"
"I'm waiting for somebody," said John truthfully. It
would be no good to pretend that he was waiting for a bus
because Benson might quite likely wait to find out which
bus he took. Besides, if Williams saw them together, he
would be clever enough to wait for a while.
"That's a pity," said Mr. Benson, "because I was going
to ask you to come and have a drink with me in some little
pub."
"That's very kind of you, but I've got a date."
"I suppose you must know many people round here?"
"Hundreds. You see, I was born here.""I'll take this bus here. Doesn't matter which I take. I
am a gentleman of leisure tonight. Cheerio."
As he stepped into the bus, he had a good mind to step
off and ask John if it was he who had questioned the shop
assistant; but there was plenty of time. He sat down.
John watched the bus go; he watched it carefully in case
Benson might jump off. The bus disappeared, so Benson
was gone. Benson's voice, however, had oozed with self-
satisfaction. What could that have meant? He bit his lips;
he would know before long why Benson was so satisfied,
he felt certain. Then he tried to dismiss Benson's voice and
to concentrate on Skinner and Williams. That wasn't diffi-
cult. He could easily picture Williams ringing the bell and
Skinner opening the door; but as Williams entered the
house, John forgot Benson altogether because he became
frightened. He looked at the people in the queue, wonder-
ing whether they were aware of his fear.
For he had stood like that once before and waited in the
same way. True it wasn't in a bus queue but in a public
house in St. Pancras. The public house was dim, almost
empty, and he just stood and waited. At last Eric came.
Eric was pale and in a dither. Something must have gone
wrong. There was hardly reason for Eric to speak.
"John," said Eric, "run, go. I'm going. Don't wait for
Bill. I'm off. Don't wait for him."
Bill Williams had killed a man a few minutes before, and
John and Eric were arrested two days later.
Now he saw Skinner lying on the floor. His hairy hand
twitched for the last time. A policeman, having heard the
shot, arrested Williams as he left the house. He had a goodmind to leave the queue, go to the station, and catch the
first train to London. It was easy to make such a resolu-
tion, but one needed a certain amount of strength to move
away,
which he lacked. He had been a coward most of his
life. A rabbit had a burrow, a hare fast legs, but he had
neither. Composed, practically calm, he viewed his panic
as if from a distance.
"Okay," said Williams behind him, "it couldn't have
been easier."
"Take your place in the queue," said a woman, "and
don't push forward."
"Sorry," said Williams, "but I was here long before you.
Everything went off as it should. Frightened him out of
his wits, and he gave me back the paper. Ten o'clock to-
morrow at Marshdon. I'll take this bus. See you in the
morning."
He laughed, jumped into the bus, and waved to John.
"Some people," the woman began, but John said quickly,
"Take my place, please." With his parcels he began to
walk back to the Red House.
So Williams had been successful. Well, Williams was
more astute than he was. Probably it was the easiest thing
to frighten Skinner. There were people who had the knack
of frightening. It was a pity he hadn't. Still, he would have
six hundred pounds tomorrow. That brought Eileen back.
He was sorry because she had been absent from his thoughts
for a good ten minutes. In a day or two he would write to
that mechanic near Reading. Or he would go and see him,
which would simplify matters. Life could be uncompli-
cated if one only tried hard enough.The two bulbs were burning in the hall, but the staircase
was in darkness. There was apparently nobody about,
which suited him provided Eileen was in. He switched on
the light at the top of the stairs. He took the parcels first
to his room, then came down and looked into the sitting
room. Eileen was there, tomorrow he would have the
money, and in that second of happiness he became fright-
ened again. Suppose Williams in the family tradition had
simply bumped Skinner off and the police were on their
way to arrest him? She was sitting with her back to the
door, so he could leave the room without her noticing him.
He rang up Skinner.
"Hullo," said Skinner's voice. It wasn't a contented
voice. John put down the receiver, shaking with relief. The
moment was his again. As he returned to the sitting room,
Eileen was standing, facing the door. She must have sensed
that he had been in the room. Their soundless understand-
ing almost disturbed him.
"My darling," he said, and she didn't wait passively as
used to be her habit, but came straight up to him. He kissed
her on the mouth, which he hadn't done before. "I'll have
to teach you a lot," he said after he had kissed her.
She put the two chairs side by side; as they sat down, she
took his hand. How beautiful she was there beside him. He
must stop her wearing that white ribbon in her hair, It
made her look virginal, remote. He let his thoughts become
pointless, then disappear. It was only her presence beside
him that existed, and her hand in his was the perfect means
of communication. She was aware of his thoughts, pro-
vided he had any; perhaps Miss Maxwell wouldn't be anecessity after all. Eileen was not looking at him, and
slowly the lightness of her touch went, while her hand be-
came rigid and hard. There was again that merry-go-round
turning in his mind. It was fast no longer, in fact it might
stop now any moment. He didn't want it to stop, for what
he would see, Eileen would see too. It was stopping and he
shook his head, as though that could make it move faster.
He tried to think quickly of the garage. It was on a by-
pass, and it could be turned into a good business proposi-
tion. Plenty of work, of course, but for Eileen's sake he
wouldn't be afraid of it. Eileen let go of his hand.
"What's the matter, darling?" he said.
She stood up and without looking at him went from the
room. He followed her down the stairs. She didn't glance
back. She opened the kitchen door. The kitchen was in
darkness. Evidently the cook was going to be late. She
switched on the light.
John stopped in the doorway. There was, he told him-
self, no reason for him to go into the kitchen. He could
turn back. It would be better to turn back. He must turn
back. Eileen had stopped, too. That was better. Let her
remain like that forever. He wanted to beg her not to
move. But she would, and he could do nothing about it.
Eileen went up to the knife-basket, took out a kitchen
knife, and gave it to John.
"Oh my God," he said, "oh my God. I knew that, my
love, but why must you tell me? Why must you?"
She smiled, her eyes never leaving him, while he tossed
the knife back into the basket. He didn't look at her, he was
looking at the knives which noticeably needed cleaning.She touched his arm. He turned around, and her smile was
gone. She was timid, afraid, she was sad and young. He
hadn't seen her look so young before.
"It is still forever, my love," he said. He took the hand
that had held the knife that stabbed her mother to death,
and kissed it. The hand was light again. "Forever," he said.
With much noise, sighing, panting, and banging the
scullery door, the cook was coming down the passage.
"We must go," said John. He took Eileen by the hand, led
her to the sitting room. "I'll be back tonight," he said.
From the door he glanced back at her. She wasn't worried
because he was leaving her. She knew he would come back.
Mr. Ribble was in the lounge. He sat shrouded in gloom,
a gloom of the peevish sort. "This house," he called to
John, "is like a charnel house. I wouldn't be surprised if I
found myself stabbed in the back, too. Come in, sit down.
There is nobody to talk to in this damned place."
"I'm so sorry," said John, "but I must go upstairs."
"At least please tell me where the switch of the chan-
delier is? Nobody can be expected to sit in this gloom."
Good manners prevailed, and John found the switch.
More light, but Mr. Ribble's own gloom remained un-
disturbed.
"I try to speak to Josey," he complained, "but he just
smiles at me, and that is that. I wish he'd play the piano,
at least."
John hurried up to his room. Mr. Ribble listened. That
silly old cook must be exceptionally silent, he thought. He
wished she would drop a couple of saucepans. That at least
would break the silence. He heard hammering upstairs.Somebody's coffin, he thought with some satisfaction. If
only that fool of a Haxton hadn't left. But they were all
the same. Complaining of their wives, bemoaning the
wasted years, and eventually running back to their wives.
Still, they were the lucky ones. At least they had a wife to
go to. If they had wasted the years, at least they didn't
waste them alone. Alone. That was what he was, com-
pletely, utterly alone. Not even wanted by a barmaid. The
barmaid was right. Why should she want him? Why
should anybody want him? He ought to become a hermit.
He should sit in a cold cave. There might be rats in the
cave, so he wouldn't be utterly, completely alone. Haxton
was by now probably pinching his lame wife. Three cheers
for Haxton. He had somebody to talk to. The hammering
ceased. He nodded. The coffin must be ready. As far as he
was concerned the whole house was a coffin. A big silent
coffin, with not even the Haileys singing drunkenly in their
room.
The front door opened. For a moment he had the wild
hope that a commercial traveler was coming to stay the
night. He might be an amiable sort of person, and they
could have a good powwow, which would help things a
lot. Slowly, stopping now and then to take breath, Mrs.
More appeared. There was something sinister, so it seemed
to him, about her slow appearance. First the hat, which
came to a standstill, then the hat rose to show the forehead,
another standstill, and the double chin and the neck fol-
lowed quickly. A full minute passed before he saw her
black shoes.
"This place tonight," he said, "is like the end of theworld. I wouldn't be surprised if I heard Eileen singing. I
wouldn't be surprised at anything."
"If you hadn't been gossiping," said Mrs. More, “then
at least the Haileys would still be here. Blame yourself."
"Blame myself? Perhaps I've killed Mrs. Hughes, too?"
"It wouldn't surprise me," said Mrs. More, taking a deep
breath preparatory to the ascent to her room. "At any
rate," she said, "dinner will be punctual. I'll see to it."
As he heard her door close, he knew he couldn't face
another evening in the Red House. Very likely that nice
young fellow would be out, probably having a good time
with the barmaid. So there would remain himself and Mr.
Josey for dinner. Mr. Josey would smile that newfangled,
stupid smile of his, and of course Mrs. More would try to
nag him about the Haileys. And upstairs the hammering
would start again. Then Eileen would sing, and it wouldn't
be surprising if Mrs. Hughes looked in on them with her
head under her arm. He went to the telephone, and the
electric bulb in the hall, having reached the end of its
allotted time, blew out. Soon, he thought grimly, all the
lights would be out; then the big show could start.
"Do you think," he said to his sister on the telephone,
"that you could give me supper and put me up for the
night?"
"But of course," she said. "We're having company in.
You don't mind that, do you?"
“The more the merrier," said Mr. Ribble truthfully.
"But why stay the night?"
"I'll tell you when I see you."
He
put down the receiver. It must be getting chilly, buthe couldn't be bothered to fetch his overcoat. At least, he

you've put twopence on the table, if it was a local call."
"Here's the twopence," he said, "and I am not dining here.”
"You should have let me know beforehand."
"Charge it to my account," he said, “and I shall want my
bill in the morning. I'm leaving."
Mrs. More became frightened. They were going one by
one. "But why are you leaving, dear Mr. Ribble?"
"I've been here long enough," he said, and rushed down
the stairs.
She went to the kitchen, told the cook dinner would be

She had been unfair to Mrs. Hughes. What a wonderful

Mrs. More pounded the gong. The gong echoed through
the house, and when she stopped pounding it, the silence
blew back. She sat down at her table. She counted the
tables; she had never bothered to count them before. Ten
tables, all told. There seemed to be too many of them. Six
would look cozier, but even six empty tables were too
much of a good thing. She wished there were only one

the next room. No strangers anywhere, just her and Peggy
alone and happy. No Haileys, no murder on the premises,
just warmth with Peggy.But where was Mr. Josey? He hadn't come down, and

Still, his company would be better than none, even if, as

must be the reason he was going to a monastery. It wasn't
her business. Her business was to find him and bring him

remain empty. She and her table couldn't cope with the

She knocked on his door, and he said, "Come in." He
was sitting at the piano. It was almost like old times.
"Your soup is getting cold, Mr. Josey," she said.
"I am very sorry, madam, but I am not coming because
I am not hungry."
"But you mustn't do that. It's very bad to go to bed on

"Please forgive me, I am not hungry."
She argued with him; it was of no avail, and she thought

"Very early."
"Good night."
He closed the door behind her and sat down on the
piano stool. The beginning had been comparatively easy.
His ancestors had prayed before him, he had prayed as a
child, and thus prayer was easily unearthed. It took him
back many years. He was back in the cold winter and theburning summer after the harsh spring winds. Over his
bed hung a picture of Nuestra Señora de Merced, and he
shared his room with two brothers. It was easy to depart
again from that room and work his way through his life
with prayers for the people he hadn't liked and who hadn't
liked him. On his knees, with his back to the piano, he
could pray his way past them, forgiving and asking for-
giveness. But he felt, ever since he had returned to his orig-
inal core, that Divine Grace was still eluding him. He
prayed more, enjoying the pain of his knees against the
hard boards, but the peace he wanted was still far from
him. With his fresh honesty he ruefully admitted that in
the present state of his soul the hope of salvation remained
aloof. He must rid himself of anger. At the end of the eight
o'clock Mass that morning it had seemed to him that anger
had left him. But at lunch he had been angry and impatient
again. By the time the bells of Santander were tolling for
Vespers, he believed he had conquered anger. Yet some-
thing was missing, terribly missing.
He sat down on the stool and asked himself if he had
ever been the great pianist of his boasts. With frightening
candor he admitted that he had been a bad pianist and
could never have reached the concert platform. He put
his head against the cold wood of the piano and cried
bitterly. He tried to stop his tears, but it was no use. A life's
hope, a life's belief were being washed away with heavy
tears. It wasn't the world that had misunderstood him: it
was he that had misunderstood himself. Down on his knees
again he prayed for deliverance from pride, and his hands
became wet from his tears. But the Word was made flesh,and that must be his consolation, and away with tears.
Then he cried for a long time. Stupid José Rodrigues had
imagined that he had talent. Stupid José Rodrigues had
no talent whatever. Probably he had been a better nail-
salesman than pianist. He looked at his wet fingers. They
had been no good. Perhaps joined in prayer they might
atone for their bad playing.
He said a Hail Mary, sat down on the stool, and knew
that something was still terribly missing.
Then Mrs. More interrupted him. It was her right to do
so, he mustn't blame her, mustn't be angry. He regretted he
couldn't oblige her by going down to dinner. He hoped
she would forgive him for it. He sat quietly, his hands in
his lap; his rosary, which somehow or other he hadn't
mislaid through the years, lay on the piano. Time went by,
and his cheap alarm clock said it was nine. He put his
finger on a key. A slight sound, and he removed his finger.
It was a fine piano. Good Mrs. Hughes had left him the
piano. Always he had coveted a piano. Now he had one.
His own piano, and a beauty at that.
Suddenly he sat up. A smile began to appear on his face.
It began slowly, and in the beginning it was timid, well-
nigh frightened. After a long while, which according to
the alarm clock lasted thirty seconds, the smile broadened,
and now it was triumphant and sure of itself. He took his
hat, pocketed the rosary, and went down. In the lounge
sat Mrs. More with the Telegraph. She hadn't the strength
to rise and leave the silent empty place. Mr. Josey disap-
peared down the stairs. The air was cool and up there, he
thought, was He who was at last going to give him Hispeace.

"No, only from the Red House to the Catholic Social

"One grand concert piano," said Mr. Josey.
"Nine o'clock all right?"
"Thank you. It is all right."

The evening rolled on, and intimacy grew. Mrs. Strong

"Never mind," said Mr. Ribble gallantly.
They hadn't noticed John. They began to run slowly
with much lifting of knees, as middle-aged people do. The
water splashed around them, and from a great wet distance
John heard Mr. Ribble's giggle cut in half by the rain.
John had watched two trains leave for London. The
first was crowded, the second half empty. He got into the
first one but jumped out a minute before it left. He didn't
get into the second train because he knew he wouldn't stay
in it. All his life he had liked railway platforms. Trains
were an escape. It didn't matter where they took you so
long as they started off with you. Engines letting out steam
were like a pledge of the future. The whistle of the train
was a full stop to the past. Many engines let off steam, he
heard plenty of whistling; then he thought he could just as
well have a drink at the Crown. The last train would be
leaving at eleven. He reached the Crown as it closed. That
was too bad. An hour to kill. He had better go back to the
station.
The stars were still up and no sign yet of the rain that
would soon be coming. Instead of starting back for the sta-
tion, he remained outside the Crown idly watching the last
visitors come out through the now-darkened front en-
trance.
A few shopkeepers, mostly with their wives, primlyseparated, wishing one another quiet good nights. Two
local drunks, both from the taproom, appeared and stood
laughing noisily. One of them broke into a song, which his
companion received with more laughter. The laughter sub-
sided, and the second man began to sing, too. They sang
different songs, but that didn't worry them. They walked
off together; at the corner one of them fell, and there was
cursing followed by a silence that ended when they moved
on again, singing the same different songs. John thought he
should light a cigarette, but that was as far as he got. There
was no hurry to return to the station. It would make no
difference if he remained outside the Crown a little longer.
From a side entrance Gwen the barmaid appeared.

have

His mind was soothingly blank. He attributed that also
to the rain. Plenty of time left for the train. If he came to
think of it, one had so much time left that one simply
didn't know what to do with it.
He was a fool, for he had forgotten to ask Mrs. More for
Mr. Haxton's address. He would write tomorrow telling
her which drawer he had left the razor in. She could post
the razor on to him. What a bore that he had taken it back
to his room. What, he asked himself, knowing the answer
perfectly well, were those lines he had quoted that morn-
ing to Mr. Haxton? "An infant crying in the night." Quite.
"An infant crying for the light." Precisely. "With no lan-
guage but a cry." He was the only person who understood
that language.
The rain wouldn't stop. Its initial strength gone, it settled
down to wet the houses and the streets for the rest of the
night. Eileen must already be in bed. Surely she had waited
for him till either Mrs. More or Audrey had signaled herto bed. It was no good for her to sit up in bed waiting for
the front door to bang. She couldn't hear it. She was lying
quietly in bed, expecting him; hope was her only connec-
tion with the closed door. He hated himself for having
pretended that he was leaving.
Though it was his intention to reach the Red House as
quickly as his long legs would take him, he went round the
Common; there in the rain, behind the crumbling wall, was
the Court. Would Mr. Bush live in it, or would Stein get it
in the end? It couldn't matter less, and he regretted the
time he had wasted going round the Common.
The front door of the Red House was locked. He rang
the bell. Nobody came, so he rang again. He heard a win-
dow open.
"Who's that?" said Mrs. More's voice up in the rain.
"John Denton."
"Oh, it's you, Mr. Denton. I'm coming at once."
Mrs. More was an elderly sight in her dressing gown.
She locked and bolted the door again behind him. "I'm so
glad you've come back, Mr. Denton," she said. “At least
now there's a man in the house. Do you know, I had a

This house is driving me mad. Would you like some tea?"
"That's very kind of you, but I'm tired and should like

"Now that I know you're here, I shall sleep all right.”
"Isn't Mr. Josey here?"
"I don't consider him a man. Anyway, he's off tomor-

More than enough.""And what will happen to Eileen?”
"Frankly, Mr. Denton, I'm of course sorry for her, but

"Certainly not." And he had been going to run away
from her. They had reached the second floor. He opened

He found the parcels on his bed. He undid them, and
from the wardrobe he took the roses. He remembered
there was a vase in the lounge: he had better find the vase.
The two toy dogs he put on the mantelpiece. The red on
one side, the blue on the other. They were cheap, clumsy
toys, but children liked that sort of thing. He hung the
prints, after he had dusted them with his towel. Should he
hang the print that showed the cardsharp being stabbed?
He smiled, for it mattered little. So he put that one over
the head of the bed; as good a place for it as any. Would
Eileen have stabbed her mother to death if she hadn't had
that print? Idle speculation, just as idle as wondering
whether, if her father hadn't been an ironmonger, she
would have played about with knives. Only facts mattered,
and it was a fact that if he didn't find the vase he could
throw the flowers out. He went out, but first he locked his
door because Mrs. More wasn't yet asleep. He could hear
her moving about in her room.
"Who's that?" she called.
"It's me, Mrs. More. Don't worry, I'm going downstairs
to get myself a glass."
Mrs. More grumbled about bad servants who didn't put
glasses in bedrooms. The vase was in the lounge; he took itupstairs, arranged the roses, and sat down on his bed to
wait.
The silence had almost a sound of its own. He felt as
though even the sea was bereft of ships and fish. The
silence must be extending all around the earth. Volcanos
were asleep, nations had stopped breathing, and the lion at
long last had lain down with the lamb, and they lay in
happy quiet. Still no matches. He should have asked Mrs.
More for a match.
They were nice prints, and he remembered a Vermeer
painting his father had been rather proud of. It hung in the
drawing room at the Court. His eldest brother, Leonard,
had been a hard little chap. He didn't hang cats, but in his
own way he was arrogant and brutal. Francis, the younger
of the two, used to scribble a lot, and the fond father often
said that since Francis had an artistic temperament he
would leave the Vermeer to him.
His father had been comparatively decent to him till
Leonard and Francis went out in the boat that capsized.
Then, he, John, who had been for a walk with his gov-
erness, became the scapegoat of his father's despair. Ap-
parently, so he was told much later, when the news of the
tragedy reached Mr. Denton, he first imagined that all
three of his sons had drowned. He was in London at the
time. He drove down in his huge Delauney-Belville and
found John waiting for him with the governess standing
behind him. "So it's just a lie," said Mr. Denton, lifting up
his son. "Hullo, old boy. Now I'd better find Leonard and
Francis." That was the last time he saw his father, for
when Mr. Denton found out that his other sons were dead,he never forgave John for that moment of relief. But per-
haps that was just a story he had been told. Mrs. More was.
now snoring.
He tiptoed downstairs. He lighted the light on the land-
ing and went down the corridor. Did Eileen lock herself
in? He opened her door. There was no key in the keyhole..
That, of course, was in tune with her life. She was at
everybody's beck and call, provided they knew a few
written signs. The room wasn't hers, existence wasn't hers.
Keyless silence was what she was considered fit for. Well,
he would change that.
The light was on. She was sitting up in bed: so she was
waiting for him. The drab dressing gown lay on a chair.
She jumped out of bed as she saw him. He took the dress-
ing gown, put it around her shoulders. He looked for slip-

was a pair of tennis shoes. They hadn't been cleaned for a

she followed him to his room. She would, he knew, follow
him to the end of the world. Mr. Josey had joined Mrs.

"Look, Eileen," he said, opening his door, “look. Do
like them?"

He pulled her to him. "Stop playing with those dogs.
No, I'm not angry. I can't be angry. All right, play with

He kissed her vehemently, and she forgot the dogs. Now
it was her turn to stroke his hair.
"They're going to find out," he said. "They always do.
I don't suppose they would hang you. They would just
put you away in some silent place and give you the same
cruel silence your mother gave you. They would say it's
for your good and for society's good. Perhaps it is. But I
won't let them. It can be for their good, but not for yours.You shouldn't have been born if you weren't to be allowed
to live. But what can we do against it? Nothing. Just plain
nothing."
She was stroking his hair peacefully. He saw she was
getting sleepy. He mustn't keep her awake too long.
"Nothing," he went on. "The whole thing is a big steel
wheel, and once you fall off it you can never get back.
You fell off it when you became your mother's daughter, I
fell off it when my father went mad, but the difference be-
tween us is that I have no excuse. Probably even now I'm
taking the way of least resistance, though frankly, my love,
I'm a bit frightened. But I mustn't be, because you're be-
side me."
It was no good talking on; he simply couldn't express

speak any more. I'm just talking rubbish."

In his childhood days at the Court the nightingales had
the habit of singing in broad daylight during May. Wait,
he used to tell himself, till June, when they will sing as
arranged at night. June, he thought, stroking Eileen's hair,
was one of those far-off things.
He embraced her. They lay close to each other while he
went on talking to her, but only in his mind. It was easier
like that. His voice might be harsh and frighten her. Also,
it was easier like that.
Later, much to his surprise, he fell asleep. It was a short
sleep, but when he awoke, he was terrified lest they had
slept till the morning. He wanted nothing to do with the
morning that would soon be breaking. He would be a
stranger in it, and Eileen would be lost in it: just lost.
"Darling," he said, yawning a little, "I'm not asleep."
Then he said quickly, "Don't be afraid."
It was easy to say that because for the moment he didn't
quite know why she or anybody else should be afraid. But
it came back. He smiled: it was bound to come back. Pity
he couldn't postpone it. "It won't hurt," he said with little
conviction. Now he was wide awake. He kissed her. She
smiled, and her smile said she knew it wouldn't hurt. That
smile gave him courage.chapter thirteen
The sun, which was the sun of
the living and the dead, came out
of the rain of the night before. It
was a kindly, fresh sun, and im-
mediately on its release from the
departing clouds it began to
pound the shiny roof of a Daimler
limousine crawling toward the
town at the rate of thirty-five miles an hour. The limousine
was of a midnight blue; midnight blue, too, was the color
of the chauffeur's uniform. The chauffeur was sleepy. He
had been in Mrs. Archibald Denton's service for many
years, but her continuous early risings were almost more
than he felt he could bear. He yawned, he tooted at a lone
cyclist pedaling peacefully in the middle of the road, he
yawned again. He'd had to rise at five because the evening
before she had expressed the desire to reach the town at
eight thirty. Arriving early was as much her rule as never
letting him drive faster than thirty-five miles an hour. And
she was good at guessing the speed of the car. He was con-
vinced she carried a speedometer in her bag.
"One always finds people in at such an early hour,” she
would say. She would also say, "I don't approve of fast
driving." The chauffeur approved, but he would have
preferred to sleep till ten. The Daimler overtook the
cyclist after a while. The cyclist was half asleep: he had all
the chauffeur's sympathy.
Mrs. Denton sat up straight in the back. She was full of
energy; the art of relaxation was unknown to her. She sat
as though she was ready to bounce. The back seat was wellupholstered, yet if anybody had glanced in through the
window, he would have concluded without undue hesita-
tion that she must be sitting in utter discomfort. She crossed
her legs, leaned forward, but not even that brought the
town any nearer. She thought she would smoke, opened
her gold cigarette case, then, closing it, decided not to
smoke. She vaguely wondered what her nephew John
looked like. She hadn't seen him for donkey's years.
Her husband, Archibald Denton, had died twenty years
ago. Twenty years was a long time; often she wasn't cer-
tain exactly why she was called Mrs. Denton. He had been
a fine young man: now she was his elderly widow. A fine
young man who had left behind her sagging chin and the
thick
pattern of wrinkles on her forehead. She thought of
him seldom. She remained his widow, as she had been his
wife. A good wife: now twenty years later she was his
good widow. To be a wife, she vaguely felt, required the
same disposition as being a widow.
Though the late Archibald was distant and mostly for-
gotten, she kept as close as possible to the Denton family.
John's father had been something of an exception. He
hadn't liked her. She had tried hard to make herself ac-
ceptable as a sister-in-law to him. Being no fool, she gave
up trying once she understood that with him she was up
against a wall; he disliked her for no reason.
When the funeral of the drowned brothers was held, she
went to their graveside, shed tears, and Mr. Denton pre-
tended she wasn't among the mourners. The drooping line
of John's career filled her with sorrow, but there were
plenty of other Dentons who did well, whose lives wereregular and on the up and up; that is to say they lived on
unearned incomes and went to their graves without fuss.
Thus she hadn't many opportunities to think of John.
She led a well-ordered, busy life. She lived at Richmond,
played bridge, and gave little luncheon parties with one
cocktail before, one glass of indifferent wine during, and
no port at the end. The food was well cooked, and there
were invariably three courses. No cheese. The rate of
mortality had lately risen among the Dentons. The late war
had had a certain amount to do with that. The old Dentons
had become very old during the war and died of old age;
the young ones perished in shot-down planes, in Africa, on
commando raids, and a few went down in ships. Eight
days before the motorcar journey, the last of them, Miss
Emily Denton, had died at the age of eighty-four. Mrs.
Denton was badly shaken. The last of them gone; the lone-
liness of widowhood stared her darkly in the face. The
weeds she had discarded twenty years before were now
heavy and black.
Miss Emily had lived at Cheltenham. She was a robust
maiden lady who came to London once a year. Invariably
on the second day of her London sojourn, the Daimler
pulled up in front of Brown's Hotel, and Miss Emily, un-
bent by the years and wearing a shiny silk dress, took her
seat in the car. She was then slowly taken to Richmond,
where luncheon with Mrs. Denton and four selected elderly
guests awaited her. She was toothless and greedy. She took
no cocktail but swallowed a little sherry. She thought Mrs.
Denton's sherry of poor quality and said so. At the funeralMrs. Denton bitterly blamed herself for not having stocked
better sherry.
Miss Emily left her a fat legacy. It was a general habit of
the last Dentons to leave her considerable sums of money.
She was rich in her own right, her husband had been rich,
and the legacies had come in at a quickening pace. Now,
with poor Miss Emily gone, there would be no more of
them; on the other hand, she already had more than she
needed. Money, however, was the last thing she thought
of. The final severing of her ties with the Dentons was
what worried and saddened her. Why couldn't Miss Emily
have lived till the age of, say, ninety? There were people
who lived till ninety and a little longer. Miss Emily should
have followed their example. As she brooded on that, she
remembered John. She was saved; there was, after all, a
Denton left in the world. A young one at that. Her mem-
ory worked feverishly, bringing back John whom she had
last seen in Switzerland, when he was a lad of sixteen years,
fresh, eager, and pathetic.
She had at the time gone to Montreux for a few sedate
autumn weeks. The sun beat down gently on the lake, the
mountains were high and politely aloof. John's school
wasn't far from Montreux. She rang through, and two
days afterward the boy came to lunch at her hotel. The
first thing she noticed was his natural good manners, which
were somehow based on his acceptance of the fact that he
was nobody out of the ordinary. So few people accepted
that. She liked him at once. He was lanky, his hair a bit
untidy, he wore a tweed jacket that was too short for him,He showed plenty of knuckles and seemed narrow in the
chest.
She offered him a cocktail. He said, "No, thank you,"
and listened to her carefully while she asked him about
this, that, and the other. As they went in to eat, she asked
after his father.
"I don't know how he is," John said apologetically.
"You see, Auntie Rose, I don't hear from him at all."
"But that's monstrous," she said. "He's your father. I
never heard of a father behaving so unnaturally. It's his
duty before God and man to take an interest in you."
"I am afraid he doesn't."
"Will you have white or red wine?" she asked. She was

"I am so sorry," said John, "but I don't drink wines and

"Why not? You're not a child any more."
"It isn't that. I don't want to become a drunkard."
She laughed. She expected him to laugh with her, but he

"It isn't that," the boy said seriously. "I want to evade

glass in it.
Oh, I am such a prig."

She must start where the father left off when he turned

If it were feasible, she would send him back to school. He

"We'd better get cracking, sir," said the head keeper,
some time later. "You move up to that fence, and we'll tap
that little wood over there, take the clover, and come down
through the sugar beet."
"Right," said Major Humphries, on whose estate they
were shooting. He was an elderly man, slightly deaf. He

for that swing would be nothing like it once the birdsstarted to come over. The girl looked around. It was lovely

was splendid.
"Have you had this shoot a long time?" she asked.

"You're not. You hear every word I say."

But in those days one hadn't to do any walk-
