him-for rabbits, you know, miss. 'There's a rabbit coming
up, Mr. John,' I says to him. He got that rabbit, so help me
God, at a good fifty-five yards, and he was shooting with
only a sixteen. I paced it out. Fifty-five yards and a clean
kill. He was a good little boy, he was.”
The niece sighed. It wasn't the information she wanted.
During the next drive she stood beside a bald old man;
while he talked to her of the impending ruin of the coun-
try, she comfortably daydreamed of the young, handsome
Denton who must be a pet, for he had gone to the dogs. In
the distance was a church tower. An old cock pheasantflew down the line. Four guns shot at it, and each of them
missed. Unperturbed, the cock disappeared in the direction
of the steeple. A retired admiral who was the gun on the
right came up at the end of the drive.
"That old cock," he said, "must be sitting on that steeple
laughing at all of us."
The girl in a lazy daydream saw the unknown, hand-
some John Denton with great gold wings alighting on the
steeple. She sighed because it was so good to be alive. She
closed her eyes from the heat of the sun. She wished she
wasn't wearing a heavy tweed jacket, it made her feel too
hot.
The sun shone through the poor stained glass of the
Catholic church. The priest, as he came from the eight
o'clock Mass, loved the sun, for it sucked up the puddles;
the only boots he owned were still wet from last night.
His
empty stomach longed for a heat no sun could give it:
the first cup of tea, which the housekeeper would surely
have waiting for him. The priest found his pipe, put it
empty into his mouth. He wasn't much of a smoker, in fact
he hadn't smoked for donkey's years; but two months ago
they'd had a Mission week, and the Redemptorist Father
who had conducted the Mission strongly advised him to
take to a pipe, at the same time suggesting he should cut
out drink altogether. The drinks had consisted of rare
glasses of sherry with the few and far between better-to-
do members of his flock. "Surely you won't say no to a
glass of sherry, Father," and he said no, he wouldn't say
no. Well, he was now landed with a pipe: perhaps sooner
or later somebody would urge him not to smoke a pipe.first
He sucked at the pipe, and the sun left the puddles for the
lofty heights of the sham Gothic towers of the town hall.
The priest walked a bit faster in order not to keep his
cup of tea waiting. There was no getting away from
it, he wasn't as young as he used to be. His feet hurt, wet
boots or no wet boots. Last Sunday during the sung Mass,
his third Mass that morning, he had had a bad headache.
The bishop had told him he might get a dispensation for a
cup of tea between the second and third Mass on Sundays.
The bishop was a wise man who appreciated that, what
with the first Mass at eight, a second Mass twelve miles
away in an R.A.F. camp at nine thirty, and a sung Mass at
ten thirty, a feeble human being-even if he was God's
priest-would be fortified with just one cup of tea in be-
tween. He had humbly refused, which was a sign of pride.
A wicked sign of pride, and well-deserved humiliation
would come his way if eventually he had to admit that he
should have taken advantage of the dispensation. If he was
lucky, he could carry on another year or so without the
cup of tea; perhaps in the meantime the R.A.F. camp might
be moved.
He walked briskly. For reasons he couldn't explain he
thought of Mr. Josey. The priest's father had been a hard-
working shopkeeper in Sunderland. There was no non-
sense about him. Work was work, andis Presbyterian
background was rooted in Scotland, whence he hailed.
Then one day he became a Catholic: the priest at the time
was a ten-year-old boy. It often seemed to him that his
father's miraculous conversion happened so that his son
should become a priest. That, however, was a thoughttainted with pride, which, he felt certain, the bishop, who
was a holy man, would-the cup of tea notwithstanding-
by no means approve.
He shook his head, for he envied Mr. Josey in a sense.
He, the priest, knew of himself that generations of Presby-
terian ancestors dogged the footsteps of his conscience.
Often he believed that John Knox's jealous hand held onto
his cassock. There had been a time when he wanted gilt
artificial flowers in his church, but with deep lack of cour-
age
he had abandoned the idea. How different and there-
fore how easy it was for that Spaniard. For him it was like
returning to the mother's lap, back into the spacious nurs-
ery, each nook and corner of which remained cherished
and well remembered. On one side was the world, on the
other the Kingdom of Heaven. He believed that stepping
from one to the other was no more difficult for Mr. Josey
than for him to avail or not avail himself of the cup of tea
between the second and third Mass; probably much less
difficult. Only a hundred yards remained between him and
the presbytery with the nice cup of tea the housekeeper
had ready. He said a quick prayer for the heaven-bound
Mr. Josey, then opened the door.
"The cat had three kittens, Father," the housekeeper
said. "Just after you left."
"How nice for her," the priest said.
In the study were the teapot, the milk, sugar, and a cup.
He poured out the tea and waited a little impatiently for it
to cool. The housekeeper went to fry sausages in the
kitchen. Before he could start drinking, she appeared at
the door."It'll be ready in a moment, Father," she said. He
thanked her. "So you really think, Father," she said, “that
I should go on that pilgrimage to Bruges?"
"I think that would be a capital idea,” he said.
"But what about all those foreigners, Father? I don't
think I'll feel at home with them."
"Oh, you'll be all right with them. Probably none of
them will speak to you, anyhow."
The housekeeper seemed relieved. He thought he would
allow himself a mild joke which she surely wouldn't under-
stand. "I always maintain," he said, "that the next Pope
should be a North Countryman." He smiled because she
didn't; and how could she, since she was from Somerset?
That made him think again of Mr. Josey. It was a happy
thought, but then the happiness went, for there were many
in the world who saw not so clearly, whom darkness en-
gulfed, and the light for them shone in vain.
"Look, Father," said the housekeeper, running to the
window, "what a smart car." The priest wasn't interested.
"The lady," said the housekeeper, "is speaking to the
driver. Probably they lost their way."
"We often do," said the priest sadly. "What about those
sausages?"
As a matter of fact, Mrs. Denton's chauffeur hadn't lost
his way. He was a conscientious man who never set out on
a long drive without glancing at a map. He could have
come into the town by a shorter route, but that was neither
here nor there. The reason Mrs. Denton leaned forward
as they passed the presbytery was that sudden hunger had
made her feel almost faint. It frequently happened thatway. Her mind made quick plans, the plans were awaiting
fulfillment, and somewhere in the middle her body inter-
vened.
"Bryan," she said. "I must have breakfast."
"The Crown, madam," said the perfect chauffeur, "is an
R.A.C. and A.A. hotel."
"The Crown, please,” she said.
She shrewdly guessed that if she reached the Red House
a quarter of an hour later John would probably still be in
bed. Men with friends like the Pimlico trollop didn't rise
early. Certainly there could be exceptions to the rule, so
she wouldn't spend much time over breakfast. The Daim-
ler stopped outside the Crown.
The manager of the Crown was angry. The new maid
hadn't risen in time. There lived in, a kitchen maid of six-
teen whom he could always rush upstairs with the early
cups of tea. Nothing to worry about, but when he went to
answer the front bell and saw the smart chauffeur and the
expensive car, he became apprehensive. The chauffeur
asked for breakfast, a fine lady alighted from the car, and
then the manager's anger with the new maid became real.
He was on the point of refusing breakfast but noticed the
barmaid going toward the back entrance for her breakfast.
"Five minutes, please,” he said.
"It mustn't be more than five minutes," Mrs. Denton
said.
"Dining room on the first floor," said the manager.
He met the barmaid in the kitchen. "You must help me
out," he said quickly. "We must serve two breakfasts. Will
you be a good girl and take them up?""Not likely," said the barmaid.
"Now, come on," said the manager, who was funda-
mentally a disillusioned man, "I'll give you an extra two
bob, and you can keep their tips. They look posh people."
"All right," said the barmaid. "Who cares?"
She thought the chauffeur was a good looker. He sat at
the table nearest the door. She liked his dark blue suit,
heavy jowl, and when he gave her a quick look, she had a
good mind to give him an equally quick one. But by then
she had noticed Mrs. Denton. Now that woman must have
plenty of money. The hat, the coat, and the bag must be
worth a lot. Mrs. Denton had a curious, almost coy, smile
when she was deep in thought. She was thinking hard of
John's future, plans were maturing, the road of John's
happiness was being meticulously paved. The barmaid put
the porridge before her.
"No, thank you," said Mrs. Denton. "I'm in an awful
hurry."
"The sausages are coming," said the barmaid. There
might be a tip of a shilling, but she wouldn't say madam
even for half a dollar. She served the sausages.
She went and stood near the window, pretending not to
notice the chauffeur; after a while she forgot him alto-
gether. The woman fascinated her. She was the sort of
woman, she believed, that a boy like John Denton would
hobnob with. A rich old woman like that would probably
give him money, and he, with that beautiful sad smile of
his (God bless him), would play up to her because he was
poor and miserable and she had all the money in the world.
Besides, she ruefully admitted, she wasn't fat.Her eyes left Mrs. Denton's table, roaming the past,
which for her was the night before. He had kissed her on
the cheek. Was he, poor little duckie, afraid she would do
some baby-snatching? The notion made her indignant.
Didn't he know she loved him just because he wasn't like
the others? She might be a fat girl, but she wasn't a bad
girl. What she wanted was kindness and a pal. That kiss
was the kiss of a pal. A good kiss that made you feel better.
He was such a good boy. Perhaps he hadn't left last night,
and she would see him again.
It was out of the question, Mrs. Denton decided, that
John could be truly bad. It was a matter of environment,
bad companions, and the cold hatred that had followed
him through life.
"The bill, please," she said.
He was a good boy, thought the barmaid. She began to
feel sure he hadn't left the night before. He would be
around just before one o'clock.
"The bill, please," said Mrs. Denton.
The chauffeur tried to give the barmaid a deep, promis-
ing look. She haughtily ignored it. If the man knew who
had kissed her the night before, he would have the surprise
of his life. Moreover, it would put him in his place, too.
"Which way," the chauffeur asked, "is the Red House,
miss?"
"First to the right, then left, then left again, then straight
on," she said. With swinging hips she went to Mrs. Den-
ton's table to collect the tip. But why were they going to
the Red House? She was alarmed: one generally hates to
see one's surmises come true.The chauffeur drove the Daimler even slower than be-
fore. He knew from his long experience with Mrs. Denton
that they invariably arrived too early. It would now be the
same. Everybody still in bed, nobody to answer the door.
He had seen that often enough. But here he was wrong.
Mr. Josey had wakened early. He'd had a splendid
dream. In his dream he saw the Lamb of God flying in
through his window. The Lamb flew twice around the
room, then flew out through the door. He followed the
Lamb flying along the corridor; it disappeared through
John's door. He tried to open the door, but it was locked.
It was a radiant dream, leaving splendor and warmth be-
hind. Therefore he wasn't surprised when on waking up
he found himself outside John's door.
"In there is Agnus Dei," he said to himself. He knocked
on the door, and as there was no answer, he tried it. He felt
it was his duty to tell John that his vision had led him to
his room. There must be something wonderful in store for
John. If he came to think of it, the young man was a sad
young man. The vision might cheer him. He tried the door
again, waited, then knocked again. There being no reply,
he knocked louder.
"Mr. Denton," he called, "Mr. Denton."
Somewhere in the distance a tractor was making much
noise. Otherwise there was silence, which seemed to culmi-
nate on the other side of the door. Mr. Josey was sorry that
John was so deep in sleep he couldn't hear his knocking.
He consoled himself with the thought that probably John
had seen the Lamb of God, too. He became aware of his
faded pajamas, which Mrs. Hughes had mended manytimes for him. Modesty sent him back to his room. He
prayed for a while. Meantime, the sun had come up.
Packing he expected to be a simple affair, but it turned
out to be more complicated. He possessed so little that he
became confused and uncertain. Should he take that tie
with him? It was a blue tie with red stripes, and some im-
becile had once asked him if he had served in the Brigade
of Guards. He thought he should leave the tie behind.
There would be no need for it. It refreshed him to think
that from now on he would never have to wear a tie. One
more bond of the world gone. On the other hand, the tie
had been given to him by a colleague when he had played
in a real orchestra. True, the orchestra had been a cinema
orchestra, but an orchestra all the same. His colleague had
given it to him for Christmas, 1926. He had no reason to
give it to him since they weren't friends, and met, so to
speak, only on duty. He was a big, sentimental kind of
man, a violinist to boot. He said at the last performance be-
fore Christmas, which was the third performance of the
day, "You must be pretty lonely, having nowhere to go
for Christmas. I've got my wife and kiddie and would ask
you in, but my mother-in-law is coming, and my brother
and his wife, so there won't be much room. But," he said
blushing, "I bought you this, and a merry Christmas to
you."
With the sun squarely facing the window Mr. Josey
stared hard at the tie. He should have thanked the man for
it. Perhaps he would keep the tie as a memento of his lack
of charity. He put it into his coat pocket.He examined his other belongings. Most of them re-
minded him of Mrs. Hughes. The leather cigarette case was
her present, the alarm clock had been given by her, too.
The alarm clock would come in useful. He was going into
a world without end, but even there one should be punc-
tual in one's eternity. What should he do with the ciga-
rette case? It was a worldly possession. He had been told
in his time that there were people who spent vast sums of
money on gold and platinum cigarette cases. Though his
was plain leather it looked too luxurious. The best thing
was to leave it behind. He sighed because he could think
of nobody to leave it to. He became worried. He walked
up and down, his head bent. Of course, he said after a
while. It was silly of him not to have thought of it before.
John and he had shared a vision in a sense. They had some-
thing in common. The young man was of the world and
would probably remain in it. A cigarette case would come
in useful for him.
He wanted to go to John's room and present him with
the cigarette case, but his shyness was too much for him.
So he
put the case in a large envelope and addressed it to
John. They would find it in his room after he had left and
give it to John. He felt he had satisfactorily disposed of his
belongings. He remained for a while beside the table on
which the envelope lay. It was, however, no good pretend-
ing: he must face the issue. Slowly, almost gingerly, he ap-
proached the piano. It was a fine piano; if anybody said it
wasn't, the bigger fool he. He pulled up the stool, sat
down, and opened the piano. The house was still quiet.Softly he began to play. He would wake no soul, his play-
ing would be heard by no one. It was his farewell per-
formance.
Touching the keys lightly, he listened to "Rose Marie."
First came "The Indian Love Call" which was followed
closely by "Totem Tom-Tom." Soon sheer habit gave
way to soft playing. By the time he had reached "Rose
Marie, I Love You," the notes rang out harshly through
the house.
They made Mrs. More jump out of bed. She was angry.
Her last sleep in that accursed house had been interrupted
by ghastly noises. It had been like that straight from the
start, but thank God she was leaving. Nevertheless, she
hadn't yet left: the noise must stop. She put on her dress-
ing gown, pulled on her slippers which sported some holes,
and hurried to Mr. Josey's door.
"Please stop that at once," she said. "You're waking the
whole house."
She banged hard on the door, which then opened. Mr.
Josey stood there looking a little past her. If he wanted a
row, she thought, he could have it.
"I am, Mrs. More," said Mr. Josey, "so very, very sorry.
You will never more have my badly playing. I am very
sorry."
"I can't have any noise before ten o'clock," she said.
"A very bad noise," said Mr. Josey, bowing stiffly. He
closed the door, was soon in prayer, and the sun shone
noncommittally on the keyboard.
Mrs. More stopped in front of John's door. There was
no denying she had a soft spot for him. If he only had alittle more guts, he would make the right young man for
Peggy. What with the sun and the relief of the knowledge
that she was leaving the Red House for good, Peggy's re-

could be proud of, even if the man's father had owned the
whole countryside. She knocked on the door. She didn't
quite know why she knocked on the door, but it would be
comforting to see the man who, if he possessed more guts,
would be the right man for Peggy. She knocked loudly,
then called, "Mr. Denton," but no answer came. Well,
young people were sound sleepers. Anyway, it wasn't yet
eight thirty, so let him rest a trifle longer.
She decided against having a bath. Her next bath should
be elsewhere. She washed quickly, dressed in haste, and by

"Help us down with a piano. Won't take a minute."
"Not on yer
life."
"But, Alfie, it's half a dollar. It won't take long."
"I doesn't like this 'ouse. The woman was killed, yer
know."
"Don't bother about that, Alfie. Give us a hand.”
Without deigning to say either yes or no, Alfie preceded
him into the house. The mate gave the chauffeur a last
angry look.
"Where's the piano?” Alfie asked.
"Second floor," said the mate.
"I don't want to go to the second floor."
"Don't be awkward, Alfie.”
"It's too big," said Alfie, when they reached Mr. Josey's
room.
"Here's Alfie to help us," said the mate.
"Not me," said Alfie. "Too big.""Don't talk so much, Alfie," said the driver. "Get hold
of it over there, you hold it there, mister. Off we go."
Curiously unmoved, Mr. Josey helped them to carry the
piano from his room. Alfie and the mate carried on a some-
what panting conversation about pianos and their lack of
usefulness.
"I'd take a hax to a thing like that," was Alfie's verdict.
They stopped on the first floor landing in order to regain
their breath. Mrs. Denton was by then quite bored with
Mrs. More's talk.
"A piano," she said, hoping to interrupt Mrs. More who
was describing Peggy's achievements in detail.
"Belongs to that funny foreign man. My daughter's
school certificate-" The bell rang. "Audrey," she shouted,
"the bell."
Mr. Benson and Marshall had walked in a leisurely fash-
ion to the Red House. Time was Mr. Benson's, his work
was done, and, if he came to think of it, he had preferred
other jobs to this one.
"You know, Marshall," he said, as they crossed the Com-
mon, "I always wanted to settle down in a small town like
this after I retired. Nice and quiet, good little pubs, you
know your neighbor, a game of darts, picnics on hot sum-
mer days, skittles and God knows what, and garden pro-
duce and a fresh egg every morning. Well, I've been here
just long enough to know that I'd go crazy in a quiet little
place like this. Give me Piccadilly tube station every time."
"Yes," said Marshall, hardly listening.
"Probably," went on Mr. Benson, "it isn't this town's
fault at all. I just don't like this job. It's an awful thing,Marshall, if one begins to think nonprofessionally. That
poor girl has made me think in a way I shouldn't. I wish,"
he said with deep sincerity, "I wasn't such a good detec-
tive. If I wasn't, I wouldn't have found out it was she who
killed that old croak." He laughed. "Heard what I said?
Now, my boy, if you ever want to get anywhere in your
career, just memorize my words and never speak or think
like that. Then you'll get somewhere. What's the matter
with you? Why are you hurrying?"
"It's nearly nine," said Marshall. It was a habit of his
to be afraid of Mr. Benson. Now because he was speaking
to him in a friendly tone, he was perhaps even more afraid
of him. The quicker they reached the Red House, the
quicker that frightening intimacy would end.
"Nine?" said Mr. Benson. "What's the hurry? She won't
run away. You ordered the car for nine, didn't you?"
"I did," said Marshall, relieved. This was official busi-
ness with nothing frightening about it. For him the case
had been a fine one. He admitted he should have suspected
the deaf and dumb girl from the start. Still, there remained
plenty of time for him to persuade himself that he had sus-
pected her from the start. He was hard at work at it.
He would, thought Mr. Benson, have a word with Den-
ton. He couldn't help it, but he had a sneaking liking for
the boy. He wouldn't tell him how much he had helped
in finding out it was Eileen who had killed her mother.
That would hurt the boy. But he would tell him the best
thing was to forget what he knew and scram.
"That fellow Denton-" Marshall began.
"Leave him alone," said Mr. Benson. Would the girlunderstand why he was coming? He wished he could have
sent somebody else. He began to walk faster. The little
boys were standing in the middle of the road, the dog had
remained on the pavement.
"What are you doing here, children?" Mr. Benson asked.
"You'll be run over. Get back on the pavement."
The boys stared at him, neither of them moving.
"Are you deaf?" Marshall asked aggressively. The boys
withdrew to the pavement. As Mr. Benson rang the bell,
the door was pushed open by the lorry-driver's elbow. The
chauffeur put down his Household Novel; he wanted to
enjoy watching them sweat and pant with the piano.
"If," Alfie said to Mr. Josey, "I don't get double rup-
ture, it won't be this 'ere bloomin' piano's fault."
"It is for a very good cause you do this," said Mr. Josey.
"Oh, please now," he called, "you be careful with the
door. She can bang back."
Mr. Benson waited for the piano to pass through the
door. The piano came to a halt on the pavement. Alfie
pulled out his shirt and wiped his forehead with it.
"Going?" said Mr. Benson to Mr. Josey.
"Going," said Mr. Josey.
"Coppers," said Alfie, as the two men went into the
house.
"A child could have guessed that, you big lazy oaf,"
said the mate.
"You're a clever one," said Alfie. "Well, 'ere's the piano.
Give us the 'alf dollar, and all the best."
"We must get it on the lorry," said the driver.
"That'll be another 'alf dollar," said Alfie."No," said the lorry-driver. "Come on."
With his shirt hanging out, Alfie swore loudly, which
amused the little boys. They considered themselves lucky
for having come in time for the fun. They felt there might
be even more fun in store for them.
"Good morning to you, Mrs. More," said Mr. Benson.
"What a fine morning. It's a joy to be alive on a morning
like this."
"This is Chief Inspector Benson," said Mrs. More, “and
this is Mrs. Denton, John Denton's aunt."
"Pleased to meet you," said Mr. Benson.
Oh, thought Mrs. Denton, she had come too late. John
must have got himself into mischief again, and before she
could help him, they would take him away. She was sur-
prised at her own panic. She shouldn't have set her heart
on trying to rescue the last of her relations through mar-
riage.
"Mrs. Denton," gushed Mrs. More, “has come for John.
She's going to help John in every way. John will have a
simply wonderful time."
"I'm glad to hear that," said Mr. Benson, sitting down.
"You mean you have nothing against him?" asked Mrs.
Denton.
"Nothing at all. I wish Denton luck. So you will stand
by him and help him?"
"That's my intention. The poor boy never had a chance."
"Very glad to hear that, I am sure," said Mr. Benson.
"But if you don't mind my giving a bit of advice, be firm
with him. He's not a bad lad. More sinned against, and
that sort of thing. But one can't go on forever being sinnedagainst without getting into real trouble. Be firm with him,
Mrs. Denton, and make a man of him.”
"I'll do my best," said Mrs. Denton.
"You're bloody well killing me," came Alfie's voice
from the street.
"Well," said Mr. Benson with a sigh, "I didn't come here
to talk to you ladies altogether. I came to have a word with
Eileen. A bit of that special mother's language. Where's
the girl, Mrs. More?"
"She's gone for a walk. She'll soon be back."
"She does go for walks, doesn't she? The poor thing.
What time did she go out?"
"Early," said Mrs. More, in order to say something.
Mr. Benson nodded. A little time to spare. It surprised
him how pleased he was by that little time. He crossed his
legs. Marshall was waiting on the landing.
"I imagine," said Mr. Benson, "that young Denton is as
happy as a lark. It's a fairy tale. To go to bed as a penniless
outcast and awake in the morning as a cherished, rich
young man is like an old-fashioned film. Is he packing?"
"As a matter of fact, we haven't wakened him," Mrs.
More said.
"But it's nine now," said Mrs. Denton. "Perhaps you
could send the maid to wake him."
"I'll go myself," said Mrs. More. "I promise I won't tell
him anything. I'll just tell him there's somebody here to
see him." She bustled upstairs.
Mr. Benson turned to Mrs. Denton. "There's one thing
I think I should tell you," he said. "Keep him from his so-
called friends, especially from a man called Williams.""I want to take him abroad."
"A very wise idea. Keep him away from his past. He'll
be all right then.”
Mr. Josey came up the stairs. He was on his way to
fetch his two small suitcases. The men were strapping the
piano in the lorry. They had chipped off a bit of the var-
nish, which was extremely upsetting. He nearly collided
with Mrs. More, now coming down from the second floor.
"That's funny," she said in a worried voice, “I knocked
and knocked, and there wasn't any answer. I rattled the
door, too. I hope he's all right. He must be sleeping very
soundly."
Mr. Benson rose. "I'll wake him," he said, and signaled
to Marshall. They ran up the stairs. Mr. Benson knocked
hard on the door. "That ought to wake the dead," he said,
and knocked again. "Break down the door," he said.
This was the sort of job Marshall enjoyed: his strength
pitted against the door. He was so good at it that as the
door burst open he nearly fell into the room.
"Stand back," said Mr. Benson. He had taken it all in
even before he entered the room. He vaguely hoped that
one or the other might still be alive, though it seemed
doubtful. He went up to the bed: there they lay, side by
side. Before his strength had ebbed altogether, John must
have pulled the blanket over their hands. He lifted the
blanket, the blood had mostly soaked into the sheets but a
few pools remained. "Doctor," he said to Marshall.
He went to the window, let the blind up, and returned
to the bed. "You poor sap," he said. He could think of no
words for Eileen. He took up the razor, tried the edge,then put it down. He shrugged his shoulders, which was as
good as anything he could think of or say.
With his two suitcases Mr. Josey, unseeing, passed the
open door. The enterprising Audrey, who had been at the
window again, came to the door.
"Go away," said Mr. Benson.
It was too late: Audrey began to shriek. Mrs. Denton
and Mrs. More rushed up the stairs. At the same time Miss
Maxwell, two colorful alphabets under her arm, had en-
tered the house. Dear Eileen, she thought.
"Now," said Alfie downstairs, "give me that 'alf dollar."
He didn't like the police car that had drawn up behind the
Daimler. He'd been fined twice in one year for drunken-
ness. The farther away the police, the happier he would
be. The lorry-driver paid him. Mr. Josey put his suitcases
into the lorry. The driver told him to sit beside him; the
mate would travel with the piano.
"There's some trouble up there," said Alfie, and was
gone.
Up there, Mrs. Denton thought that before widowhood
took hold of her altogether she could at least pay for
John's funeral. Mrs. More sobbed loudly, for Peggy would
never return now. Audrey, having vaguely heard the lorry
start up, pushed past Miss Maxwell, who dropped her
alphabets and ran downstairs. The lorry was on the move,
but Audrey caught up with it.
"Mr. Josey," she shrieked, "Mr. Denton and our Eileen
has killed themselves."
Mr. Josey looked down from the lorry. The sun was on
the piano held by its bonds of rope."I shall pray for them," he said.
His eyes turned back to the windshield, on the other side
of which waited his peace and eternity. With so many
people to pray for it would be a busy eternity. Audrey fell
behind while the two little boys and the dun-colored sheep
dog began to chase the lorry.UNIVERSITY OF MICHIGAN
